\title[Atiyah--Floer cobordism maps II]{Cobordism maps and Atiyah--Floer isomorphisms\\
for framed instanton homology II}
\author{Bernd Johannes Wuebben}
\date{First version: 30 September 2026. This version: 8 October 2026}
\subjclass[2020]{57K31, 57R58 (primary); 53D40, 58J05 (secondary)}
\keywords{Atiyah--Floer conjecture, framed instanton homology, coherent
orientations, determinant lines, Lagrangian Floer homology, cobordism maps}
\begin{document}
\begin{abstract}
We prove that the mixed isomorphisms of Daemi, Fukaya and Lipyanskiy type between framed
instanton homology and the Lagrangian Floer homology of a Heegaard splitting intertwine the
integral instanton cobordism maps, oriented by almost-complex structures, with signed symplectic
cobordism maps, up to chain homotopy and one overall sign for each cobordism. This holds for
every connected cobordism between connected three-manifolds carrying a framed arc and an $\SO(3)$
bundle trivialized along the ends and the arc. Where the integral framed instanton homology is nonzero, the
symplectic maps are signed counts of holomorphic triangles, with the Lagrangian correspondence of
a compression body as one boundary condition for one- and three-handles. At three-manifolds whose integral framed instanton homology vanishes, the complexes are contractible and the maps are zero. The orientations are transported from the gauge theory through the mixed
anti-self-duality and holomorphic curve equation. Consequently these maps form a projective
functor on homology, independent of the handle decomposition up to sign. The unsigned analysis is that of the comparison modulo two; the new input is that two degenerations of the mixed
equation, the collapse of a compression body and excision along a torus, preserve orientations,
and that where this homology is nonzero the sign by which the orientations change when a sphere is attached to a strip is constant
under small perturbations and invariant under reversing the splitting. The relation with
homology-oriented integral instanton maps is left open.
\end{abstract}
\maketitle
\setcounter{tocdepth}{1}
\tableofcontents

\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}

\subsection{The integral comparison}
Let $M$ be a closed connected oriented three-manifold. The framed
instanton construction of Kronheimer and Mrowka~\cite[Section~4.1]{KM-instantons} replaces $M$ by
$M^\#=M\#T^3$ and uses an $\SO(3)$ bundle which is trivial on $M$ and odd on a torus in the added
summand. A Heegaard splitting determines two Lagrangians in the moduli space of flat connections on
the stabilized surface. Daemi, Fukaya and Lipyanskiy construct an isomorphism between the resulting
gauge and symplectic Floer homologies~\cite{Floer,Floer-Lagrangian}, an instance of the
Atiyah--Floer conjecture~\cite{Atiyah}, by counting solutions of a mixed equation~\cite{DFL}. The
mixed equation couples the anti-self-duality equation on a four-dimensional region to a holomorphic
curve equation on a surface; the two solutions have matching flat boundary values.

Floer homologies over $\ZZ$ carry information that is invisible modulo two: every count is a signed
count, and the signs come from coherent orientations of determinant lines. The construction
of~\cite{DFL} gives an integral map for each three-manifold. Functoriality with respect to
four-dimensional cobordisms is a separate question. The companion paper~\cite{PaperI}, referred to as
Paper~I, answers it over the field $\FF$ of two elements: isomorphisms of the type
of~\cite{DFL} intertwine the Kronheimer--Mrowka cobordism maps with maps defined by holomorphic
curves alone. These maps count holomorphic triangles for two-handles, and for one- and three-handles
holomorphic triangles with the Lagrangian correspondence of a compression body as one boundary
condition; we call the latter compression cups and caps. The analysis of Paper~I rests on two
degenerations of the mixed equation: a compression body collapsing next to a holomorphic strip, and
excision along a torus.

This paper proves the integral version, up to one overall sign for each cobordism. The symplectic
maps are the same geometric counts, now with orientations; they are not defined by conjugating
instanton maps through the isomorphism. Three orientation questions arise.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item The action of the lifting class used in framed instanton homology, the class in $H^1(M^\#;\ZZ/2)$ whose action by bundle automorphisms is divided out in~\cite[Section~5.1]{KM-unknot}, must preserve orientations, so that the quotient complex is defined over $\ZZ$. This is settled by the invariance of the almost-complex orientations under bundle automorphisms (Section~\ref{sec:gauge}), by Proposition~\ref{prop:character-gauge} for the conversions, and by Proposition~\ref{prop:transport} on the symplectic side.
\item The two degenerations must preserve the orientations of individual solutions. This is the
content of Theorems~\ref{thm:B} and~\ref{thm:excision-intro}.
\item The orientation system transported through a reflected mixed domain must be compared with
the dual of the original system. The compression cup supplies a tensor, and this comparison turns it into a map $\CF(M,p)\to\CF(M^+,q)$, the form in which Paper~I states the cup. This is Theorem~\ref{thm:C}.
\end{enumerate}
An unspecified sign on each generator would not suffice: the ambiguity in a cobordism map must be a
single sign.

On the gauge side we use the almost-complex orientations of
Kronheimer--Mrowka~\cite[Section~5.1]{KM-unknot}, which are invariant under bundle automorphisms.
The almost-complex structure on a cobordism is fixed near the odd torus, and its restrictions to the
ends need not be the reference structures of the objects. We therefore compose with the
isomorphism of orientation lines between the two structures, called a \emph{conversion}. When the index of the operator interpolating between the two structures is odd, the position of the conversion relative to the generator orientation line changes the map by the grading operator rather than by a sign (Section~\ref{sec:gauge}). We write $\IG$ for
the resulting gauge functor.

\subsection{Statement}
The category $\bB$ is described in Section~\ref{sec:geometry}. An object is a closed connected
oriented three-manifold with the framing data needed for its torus summand. A morphism is a
connected oriented cobordism $W$ equipped with a normally framed arc $\nu$ joining its ends and an
$\SO(3)$ bundle $E$ trivialized at the ends and along the arc. A presentation $p$ consists of a
Heegaard splitting, metric data, small primary holonomy perturbations and regularizing data. A
presentation is \emph{small} if it is regular and its primary perturbation has norm below a
radius that depends only on the splitting and the metric data
(Definition~\ref{def:admissible-intro}); small decompositions of a cobordism are words of
elementary moves through small presentations (Section~\ref{subsec:admissible}).

Write $C_G(M,p)$ for the integral gauge complex with the almost-complex orientation convention, and
$\CF(M,p)$ for the symplectic complex with transported orientations. The graded homotopy category
$\cK(\ZZ)$ and the notation $F\simeq\pm G$ are defined in Section~\ref{sec:algebra}. For a
small decomposition $d$ of a cobordism, $S_d(W)$ is the composite of signed holomorphic
triangle maps, compression cups, compression caps, continuation maps, and the pushforwards and
conversions induced by frame changes, that is, changes of the trivialization of the bundle at an end (Section~\ref{sec:geometry}), and by other isomorphisms of decorated data (Section~\ref{sec:descent}).

\begin{thmletter}[Integral comparison]\label{thm:A}
Orient the gauge complexes by almost-complex structures and the symplectic complexes by transport through the mixed equation, and let the presentations be small. Then the mixed maps
\[
 N_p:C_G(M,p)\longrightarrow\CF(M,p)
\]
are isomorphisms of finite free integral complexes. For every morphism $(W,E,\nu):M_0\to M_1$,
small endpoint presentations $p_0,p_1$, and small decomposition $d$, one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:main}
 N_{p_1}\IG(W,E,\nu)\simeq
 \pm S_d(W,E,\nu)N_{p_0}
 \quad\text{in }\cK(\ZZ).
\end{equation}
The sign is one sign for the entire map. Consequently the symplectic maps are independent of the
decomposition up to sign and chain homotopy. On homology they define a projective functor and $H(N)$
is a projective natural isomorphism from $\IG$ to that functor. Each symplectic homology map has the
parity of the corresponding gauge map.

At objects with nonzero integral gauge homology the symplectic maps are the geometric maps of
Paper~I with the orientations constructed here. If the integral gauge homology vanishes, the complex
is contractible; maps incident to it represent the zero morphism in $\cK(\ZZ)$, with the convention
of Section~\ref{subsec:zero}. Reduction modulo two recovers the maps of Paper~I, up to chain homotopy in the contractible case.
\end{thmletter}

The Euler characteristic of the framed instanton homology of a rational homology sphere $M$ is $|H_1(M;\ZZ)|$ \cite[Corollary~1.4]{Scaduto}, so the integral homology is nonzero for every rational homology sphere; it is also nonzero for $\#^k(S^1\times S^2)$ \cite[Section~7.8]{Scaduto}. Whether framed instanton homology is nonzero for every closed three-manifold is an open question~\cite[Question~1.5]{Yang}. If the integral gauge homology of an object vanishes, so does its framed instanton homology with complex coefficients; such an object would answer that question in the negative. The second paragraph of Theorem~\ref{thm:A} covers this case.

The qualifier ``projective'' has the usual meaning: maps are identified with their negatives.
Multiplying individual elementary maps by signs is permitted, whereas multiplying different matrix
entries by unrelated signs is not; the composition identities hold up to these signs.

\subsection{The orientation comparisons}
Theorem~\ref{thm:A} rests on three further statements. The first two say that the two
degenerations of Paper~I preserve orientations; the third compares the transported orientations
with their reflections.

For a two-handle cobordism the mixed domain of the comparison contains a compression body $C$ next
to a holomorphic strip. Paper~I shows that, as the metric on $C$ collapses, the regular index-zero
solutions correspond bijectively to those of a limiting problem in which $C$ is replaced by the
Lagrangian boundary condition $L(C)$; stretching ordinary necks then separates the holomorphic
triangles with boundary on the two Heegaard Lagrangians and on $L(C)$. The limiting solutions carry
the orientation induced from the mixed orientations through the collapse
(Definition~\ref{def:limit-orientation}).

\begin{thmletter}[Orientations under collapse]\label{thm:B}
The orientation of Definition~\ref{def:limit-orientation} is well defined and coherent at breaks,
and the collapse bijection of Paper~I between regular index-zero mixed solutions at small collapse
scale and regular index-zero solutions of the limiting problem is orientation preserving. This holds
compatibly with ordinary gauge and strip gluing and in compact parameter families.
\end{thmletter}

Together with the integral identification of the pieces into which the limiting domain splits (Lemma~\ref{lem:vertex-id}) and of the gauge two-handle map with a triangle map (Lemma~\ref{lem:gauge-triangle-Z}), Theorem~\ref{thm:B} gives the two-handle square
(Theorem~\ref{thm:two-handle}): for a two-handle cobordism the gauge two-handle map and the
holomorphic triangle map are intertwined by the mixed maps in $\cK(\ZZ)$, up to one sign.

For one-handles the comparison passes through a mixed domain whose gauge input is a connected sum
along a torus. On the gauge side such a manifold is split by Floer's excision theorem, in the form
of~\cite[Theorem~5.6]{KM-unknot}. Paper~I proves the corresponding excision for the mixed
equation over $\FF$ by stretching a torus collar. Let $M^+$ be obtained from $M$ by $k$ one-handles.
The two sides are the torus-summed objects $(-M)^\#$ and $(M^+)^\#$, and the closed cut is
$Z\cong(-M)\#M^+\#T^3$ (Section~\ref{subsec:handle-geometry}). Write $N_\pm$ for the mixed maps of
the two sides, $N_Z$ for the mixed map, with gauge input $Z$, of the folded pair $(A_0,A_1)$, where $A_i=\widetilde P_i^-\times R_i\subset\Msf_h^-\times\Msf_g$ are the product Lagrangians of Section~\ref{subsec:handle-geometry}, and
$\overline{\mathsf E}$ for the reverse excision map of Kronheimer and Mrowka from the tensor product
of the gauge complexes of the two sides to that of $Z$. The output complex of the folded pair is a complex of the product $\mathbb X$; write $\mathcal K^{\mathrm r}$ for its identification, of degree equal to the parity $t$ of the conversion at the cut, with the tensor product of the symplectic complexes of the two sides. It is canonical up to one sign and absorbs the orientation line of the conversion at the cut (Section~\ref{sec:excision}). Here and below $\otimes^{\mathrm r}$ is the tensor product of maps with the Koszul rule of Section~\ref{sec:algebra} in which signs arise from passing the right-hand factor.

\begin{thmletter}[Oriented torus excision]\label{thm:excision-intro}
After the conversion at the cut, the mixed map $N_Z$ of the folded pair composed with reverse excision is chain homotopic over $\ZZ$, up to one sign, to the tensor product of the mixed maps of
the two sides:
\[
 \mathcal K^{\mathrm r}N_Z\overline{\mathsf E}\simeq\pm\,(N_-\otimes^{\mathrm r}N_+).
\]
\end{thmletter}

The chain homotopy is assembled from signed counts of isolated solutions in explicit
one-parameter families, composed or tensored with closed maps (Theorem~\ref{thm:mixed-excision}).

The third statement concerns the \emph{sphere character}: the sign by which a coherent orientation
of strips changes when a sphere representing the generator of $\pi_2(\Msf_h)$ is attached
(Definition~\ref{def:sphere-character}). For orientation systems induced by relative spin structures, the effect of sphere bubbling is governed by the background class~\cite[Chapter~8]{FOOO-book}, \cite{WW-orientations}. The transported system is defined through gauge theory and is not known to be of this form, so the sphere character has no a priori topological formula; Theorem~\ref{thm:C} uses only its constancy and its invariance under reflection. The
character matters because a
coherent continuation orientation system with prescribed ends exists exactly when the sphere
characters at the two ends agree (Lemma~\ref{lem:rigidity}). Reflection interchanges the two
compression bodies; $\bar p$ is the resulting presentation of $-M$.

\begin{thmletter}[Reflection and the diagonal cup]\label{thm:C}
Let $M$ have nonzero integral framed instanton homology, and fix a Heegaard splitting, metric
data and the reference almost-complex structure. For primary perturbations in the small ball
(Definition~\ref{def:admissible-intro}), the sphere character does not depend on the perturbation,
and it is unchanged when the two compression bodies are exchanged. Consequently there is an
isomorphism
\[
 \Phi_p:\CF(-M,\bar p)\longrightarrow\CF(M,p)^\vee ,
\]
unique up to one overall sign and compatible with the orientations of the determinant lines over
all strip configurations. With $\Phi_p$, the compression cup of the trivial compression body ($k=0$), whose correspondence is the diagonal, is chain homotopic, up to one sign, to the symplectic continuation map.
\end{thmletter}

Theorem~\ref{thm:C} needs only that the sphere character is constant in the perturbation and agrees across a fold; its value is not
computed, and no relative spin structure on the compression correspondence is chosen.

\subsection{Strategy}
The main new ideas are the constancy and reflection invariance of the sphere character
(Sections~\ref{sec:characters} and~\ref{sec:reflection}), which make the reflection pairing and the
identification of the diagonal cup with continuation possible; the comparison of orientations under
collapse through a two-parameter family of operators (Section~\ref{sec:collapse}); and, for
excision, the identification $\mathcal K^{\mathrm r}$ of degree $t$, which absorbs an odd conversion at the
cut and is read off at a single constant configuration (Section~\ref{sec:excision}). Because
orientations must be transported along configurations that are not solutions, about a third of the
paper is linear analysis on configuration spaces (Section~\ref{sec:determinants} and
Appendices~\ref{app:inputs} and~\ref{app:shifting}).

\emph{The mixed isomorphism} (Section~\ref{sec:vertex}). The integral mixed map has a unit diagonal
in the filtration used in Paper~I. The diagonal configuration is a constant pair, and its
orientation is the canonical orientation of an invertible operator. An odd integer on the diagonal
would give a mod-two isomorphism but not an integral one; the unit diagonal gives the integral
isomorphism.

\emph{Collapse} (Section~\ref{sec:collapse}). Determinant lines are transported through the collapse
family, from the preglued configurations to the solutions. The continuity needed is continuity over
smooth configurations with all derivatives controlled on compact sets and prescribed asymptotics;
strong convergence of solutions alone does not identify the operator families. The preglued
configuration is not a solution, and the chart in which the contraction runs is not the chart that
defines the determinant bundle. Both are interpolated in a two-parameter family of Fredholm
operators that is invertible on three edges of a square (Proposition~\ref{prop:affine-square}), so
transport carries the canonical orientation at the preglued configuration to the canonical
orientation at the solution (Lemma~\ref{lem:square}). The
collapse plays the role, for the mixed equation, that strip shrinking and the composition of seam
conditions play in quilted Floer theory, where the corresponding orientation comparison is made
in~\cite{WW-orientations}.

\emph{Excision} (Section~\ref{sec:excision}). On the joined torus domains every solution carries
two orientations. The closed-cut orientation combines the gauge orientation of the reverse excision
cobordism, the conversion at the cut and the orientation of the closed-cut piece; along a long
closed neck its counts become $N_Z\overline{\mathsf E}$. The product orientation combines the
orientations of the two separate domains across torus completion; at large width its counts are
$N_-\otimes^{\mathrm r}N_+$. The two are related by one isomorphism of lines for each output, read off at the
joined constant pair, and these isomorphisms assemble into the closed map $\mathcal K^{\mathrm r}$. The
one-parameter families of Paper~I supply the homotopies.

\emph{Reflection and the cup} (Sections~\ref{sec:cup}--\ref{sec:continuation}). A folded triangle
in $\Msf_h^-\times\Msf_g$, the folded form of a quilted triangle with seam condition $L(C)$, gives a
tensor in the product of the reflected source complex and the target complex. Excision identifies this tensor with a
relative invariant of the one-handle filling, which gives a cup formula before any reflection pairing is inserted. It remains
to identify the pairing with the one for the symplectic continuation maps. We prove that the sphere character is constant under small changes of primary data, and then compare it across a fold. The doubled folded triangle is a disc with two outgoing strip ends, modelled on the reflected lower presentation and on the upper one. A sphere attached near one end can be moved through the unfolded disc to the other end, at zero collapse scale, where the image of the diagonal is an interior line of the unfolded disc; since node gluing and the transported orientation are continuous along the move, the two sphere characters agree (Proposition~\ref{prop:fold-character}).
Nonvanishing homology enters here: a generic path of primary perturbations has a finite subdivision
on each piece of which some intersection point persists (Lemma~\ref{lem:persistent}), and the
character is read off at these points. Lemma~\ref{lem:rigidity} then identifies the diagonal cup with
continuation. The cup formula gives naturality under small changes of primary data, the inverse
relation between the reflected mixed map and $N_p$, and the one-handle square. Reflection gives the
three-handle square.

\emph{Descent} (Sections~\ref{sec:choices} and~\ref{sec:descent}). Over $\ZZ$, equality on homology
does not imply that two chain maps are homotopic. The proof therefore keeps the homotopies supplied
by the one-dimensional moduli spaces and uses the invertibility of the mixed maps only after the
elementary squares have been established.

\subsection{Organization}
Section~\ref{sec:geometry} sets up the geometric data, states the analytic foundations
(Propositions~\ref{prop:foundations}--\ref{prop:selfgluing}), defines admissibility and lists the
results used from Paper~I. Sections~\ref{sec:algebra} and~\ref{sec:gauge} fix the sign conventions
and the integral gauge functor. Section~\ref{sec:determinants} constructs the transported
orientations, and Section~\ref{sec:vertex} proves that the mixed maps are integral isomorphisms.
Theorem~\ref{thm:B} is proved in Section~\ref{sec:collapse}, Theorem~\ref{thm:excision-intro} in
Section~\ref{sec:excision}, and Theorem~\ref{thm:C} in Sections~\ref{sec:cup}--\ref{sec:continuation}.
Sections~\ref{sec:choices} and~\ref{sec:descent} prove Theorem~\ref{thm:A}. The appendices prove the
analytic foundations, the relative preparation and shifting, a sign calculation, and the ordinary
integral excision of Kronheimer and Mrowka, with lifting groups, that the mixed excision uses.

\subsection{Scope}
The almost-complex convention used for $\IG$ is part of the statement. We do not identify it with
Scaduto's homology-oriented integral cobordism maps~\cite{Scaduto}: an odd conversion can introduce
a grading operator rather than an overall sign, and Section~\ref{sec:gauge} describes the additional
grading information a comparison would need. We likewise do not identify $N_p$ with the integral map
of~\cite{DFL}; their reductions modulo two are compared in Paper~I. Comparison of the transported
orientations with the relative-spin orientations of quilted Floer
theory~\cite{WW-orientations,WW-quilt} is a separate question: the curves and correspondences are
those of Paper~I, but an equality of mod-two counts does not supply an integral orientation
comparison. Disconnected objects, units, counits, closed pairings and nontrivial bundles on the
objects are outside the present statement; bundles on cobordisms may be nontrivial relative to the
trivialized ends.

\section{Geometric data and analytic foundations}\label{sec:geometry}

\subsection{Framed objects and cobordisms}
Fix an oriented ball in $M$ and form $M^\#=M\#T^3$ along that ball.
Write $T^\circ=T^3\setminus\operatorname{int}B^3$. Choose a torus
$T_0\subset T^\circ$, a neighbourhood $N(T_0)$, and an odd bundle
$P_T\to T^3$ with $\langle w_2(P_T),[T_0]\rangle=1$. The bundle
$P_M\to M^\#$ is obtained by joining $P_T$ to the trivial bundle on
$M$. The lifting class
\[
 \alpha_M=\PD[T_0]\pmod2\in H^1(M^\#;\ZZ/2)
\]
acts on the determinant-one configuration space by bundle automorphisms that do not lift to
determinant-one gauge transformations; the framed complex uses the quotient by
$\phi_M=\langle\alpha_M\rangle$, as in~\cite[Section~5.1]{KM-unknot}. A class in $H^1(\,\cdot\,;\ZZ/2)$
acting in this way is called a \emph{lifting class}, and a group of such classes a \emph{lifting
group}.

For $(W,E,\nu)$ as in the introduction, remove a tube around the
normally framed arc and insert $T^\circ\times[0,1]$. Denote the
result by $(W^\#,E^\#)$. The lifting subgroup $\phi_W$ is the one
induced by this torus tube, with the endpoint restrictions $\phi_{M_i}$.
At an intermediate disconnected cut we retain the full subgroup
specified by the tube; it need not be the product of the groups on
the components. This distinction is essential in the excision
calculation in Section~\ref{sec:excision}.

The category identifies isomorphic decorated cobordisms and uses
gluing along the fixed endpoint data for composition. The connected
source and target condition permits a handle decomposition with
one-, two-, and three-handles, together with changes of presentation.
As in Paper~I, elementary words also include metric changes, Heegaard moves and frame changes. A
\emph{frame change} of an object $M$ is a map $u\colon M\to\SO(3)$ equal to $1$ near the fixed
ball; it changes the trivialization of the bundle at an end and defines a morphism $[u]\colon M\to
M$ (Paper~I, Section~4.5). On the symplectic side it acts through the symplectomorphisms of
$\Msf_h$ induced by bundle automorphisms. A \emph{filling frame} of a two-handle cobordism trivializes the bundle over the pieces of the standard decomposition, with a transition of the bundle's lifting class over one of them (Paper~I, Definition~4.6); the \emph{global frames} are the trivializations over the two ends obtained from it (Paper~I, Section~4.4). The presentation graph is
connected and the gauge cobordism map is independent of such a
word. These are the geometric inputs to the descent at the end of
the paper.

\subsection{Compression bodies and the symplectic complex}
If $M=H_0\cup_\Sigma H_1$ is a Heegaard splitting, adjoin $[0,1]\times T^2$ to each handlebody.
The resulting torus compression bodies $C_i$ have boundary $\Sigma_h\sqcup T^2$, where $\Sigma_h$
is the stabilized Heegaard surface and $h$ is its genus, one more than that of $\Sigma$. Let $\Msf_h$ be the moduli space of flat connections on the
odd $\SO(3)$ bundle over $\Sigma_h$, with the lift convention of
\cite[Section~2]{DFL}. Its symplectic form is the usual
Atiyah--Bott form. Restriction of flat connections on $C_i$ gives a
Lagrangian $L(C_i)\subset\Msf_h$.

\emph{Primary perturbations} $\mathfrak h_i$ are finite sums of averaged holonomy
functions supported in $C_i^\circ$, away from the fixed collars; they move the Lagrangians.
Their critical equations are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pertflat}
 *_3F_B+\nabla_B\mathfrak h_i=0.
\end{equation}
We use a sign-sensitive trace convention for fixed-determinant
$\U(2)$ holonomy: on each solid torus choose a continuous square
root of the determinant holonomy and take the trace of the resulting
$\SU(2)$ part. The choices are made once for the finite family.
Equation~\eqref{eq:pertflat} determines perturbed Lagrangians
$P_i=L_{\mathfrak h_i}(C_i)$. At transverse data, their intersection points
correspond to the gauge critical orbits. \emph{Secondary perturbations} are holonomy perturbations supported in the interior of a mixed domain, away from the matching boundary and the collars, or, on a gauge cobordism, in compact collars of its ends as in~\cite[Section~3.7]{KM-unknot}; they make the moduli spaces regular. \emph{End perturbations} are the perturbations of the Floer
equation on the gauge ends. The \emph{regularizing data} of a presentation are its end
perturbation, the cutoff of the end perturbation in the mixed equation, and its secondary data; as
in Paper~I, Section~3.5, the secondary data may include a compact deformation of the almost-complex
structure of the section away from the matching line.

For each intersection point $x$, let $a_x$ be the corresponding
gauge critical point and $L_G(a_x)$ its real determinant line.
For a real line $L$, write $\orZ(L)$ for the rank-one free abelian
group generated by its two orientations with the relation
$[-o]=-[o]$. The integral symplectic chain group is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:chains}
 \CF(M,p)=\bigoplus_{x\in P_0\cap P_1}\orZ(L_G(a_x)).
\end{equation}
The differential counts rigid strips with the transported
orientation system of Section~\ref{sec:vertex}. The mod-two
reduction of~\eqref{eq:chains} is the chain group of Paper~I.

\subsection{Mixed configurations}
A mixed domain consists of a gauge region and a symplectic surface
meeting along a matching boundary. On the gauge side a connection
satisfies a perturbed anti-self-duality equation. On the symplectic
side a map to $\Msf_h$, or to a product of such spaces, satisfies a
perturbed Cauchy--Riemann equation. Along a matching boundary the
surface restriction of the connection is flat and represents the
value of the map. A piece, end or configuration is \emph{ordinary} if it is purely gauge-theoretic or purely symplectic, as opposed to mixed. At ordinary ends we prescribe irreducible
nondegenerate gauge limits or transverse Lagrangian intersection
points. At a mixed end the asymptotic values range over a critical manifold; we keep them as
finite-dimensional \emph{stationary coordinates}, separate from the decaying part, as in the
extended weighted spaces of~\cite[Definition~3.9]{DFL}.

The mixed domain on which $N_p$ is defined is the special quintuple of~\cite[Section~3.1]{DFL}; a
\emph{domain of DFL type} has the same pieces and ends, up to compactly supported changes of the seam placement and of the neck lengths and to the translation-invariant data on the mixed ends, with the metrics and data allowed in~\ref{I1}. The mixed domains used below are the domain defining $N_p$, mixed and folded
triangles, their strip gluings, the closed-cut mixed domain, the
finite-width torus domains, their completed limits, and the collapse
families. We also use ordinary strips, continuation strips, and the
disks obtained by doubling and capping a folded triangle. Statements
about determinant lines apply on configuration spaces, not just on
their solution subsets.

We use the smooth topology on these spaces: in fixed end
trivializations all derivatives converge on compact sets, the
asymptotic limits converge with their parameters, and the decaying
parts converge in the chosen weighted norms at every derivative
order. On a completed torus domain of Paper~I, Definition~10.1(b),
the torus tail $T\times[0,\infty)_r\times\RR_\theta$ is one of these
ends, with limit the odd flat connection $\alpha$: in the fixed product
trivialization of the tail, the difference of the connection from
$\alpha$ is a decaying part, measured in $H^m_\delta$ with the weight
$\tau_0(\theta)$ of Paper~I, Definition~10.1. A continuous compact
family means a family continuous in this topology. Sobolev completions are used locally to construct
Fredholm operators and determinant lines. They do not replace the
smooth topology when a holonomy family is differentiated repeatedly.

\subsection{Analytic foundations and shifting}\label{sec:analytic}
The unsigned moduli spaces, their energy-index estimates, and the
low-dimensional compactifications are those of Paper~I. The linear
analysis used in addition is collected in the next proposition. It is
proved in Appendix~\ref{app:inputs} from the local theory of
\cite{DFL,DFL-mixed}, the end models of Paper~I, and standard results
on pseudoholomorphic curves. The comparison at affine centres and the independence of the end weights, described after the proposition, are Propositions~\ref{prop:affine-square}
and~\ref{prop:weights}, proved in Section~\ref{sec:collapse}
and Appendix~\ref{app:weights}.

\begin{proposition}[Analytic foundations]\label{prop:foundations}
For each domain listed above, and each compact family of smooth
configurations and data used below, the following hold. The families
may vary the almost-complex structures and perturbations smoothly, the
metrics among those which near every matching seam are products up to
a conformal factor depending on the base coordinates, the finite torus
lengths and widths in compact ranges, and the collapse scale in compact
intervals of positive scales; on the completed torus domains they are
compact in the topology above.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item Every smooth configuration with fixed asymptotics has local
matching-boundary charts. The realized operators of
Definition~\ref{def:realization} and their formal adjoints on the dual
weighted spaces satisfy local estimates and compact-remainder
estimates uniform on the family. They are Fredholm, their kernels and
cokernels consist of smooth elements, and they form continuous
families. Changes of chart and the permitted changes of realization
give the same determinant line.
\item At ordinary ends the weights lie in common spectral gaps; at
mixed ends the stationary coordinates are separated from the decaying
part. The scalar Neumann operator $d_A^*d_A$ is invertible on the
weighted spaces at every configuration whose restriction to an odd
surface or torus collar is irreducible, with bounds uniform on the
family. Regular zero-dimensional solutions and the constant reference
pairs with no secondary or inhomogeneous data have invertible realized
operators, including the constant pairs on the finite, completed and
joined torus domains and on the closed-cut piece; by openness the same
holds for sufficiently small data. For the preglued families of
Lemma~\ref{lem:bridge-gluing} the estimates of (a) are uniform as the
torus lengths tend to infinity, with the completed domains as limits.
At positive collapse scale the estimates are uniform on compact intervals of scales bounded away
from zero. The zero-scale limit domains are included.
\item The pure symplectic continuation, cap
and strip-gluing moduli spaces have regular low-dimensional
compactifications, with no additional bubbling boundary in the
dimensions used here, and along the gluing paths from preglued
configurations to regular solutions determinant transport preserves
the canonical orientations. Let a disc contain an interior node and be
separated from the matching and boundary collars. Then the real
Cauchy--Riemann part of a mixed or pure operator glues at the node:
the determinant lines form a continuous line bundle in the plumbing
topology, the node contributes its complex fibre, canonically oriented
and even, and node gluing is associative and commutes with ordinary
strip gluing.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

The weights lie in the signed spectral gaps of the ends, the adjoints use the opposite signed
weights, and every stationary and parameter column lies in the weighted target. The
fixed-positive-scale comparison at affine centres (Proposition~\ref{prop:affine-square}) uses one
sufficiently small weight in the indicial gaps; independence of the weight within the full spectral
gap is proved for solutions and their counts (Proposition~\ref{prop:weights}). The constants are
uniform on each fixed compact family. As the collapse scale tends to zero the estimates of~(b) are
not uniform; Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family} works with the logarithmic loss of Paper~I.

The second input concerns relative preparation and shifting in
families. Daemi, Fukaya and Lipyanskiy state the one-input shift on
their special quintuple, the model mixed domain of~\cite[Section~3.1]{DFL} on which their
isomorphism is defined, and sketch its family version
\cite[Lemmas~4.62 and~4.64]{DFL}; the domains, topology and families
used here, and the several-input form, are not covered there. The
following proposition is proved in Appendix~\ref{app:shifting}.

\begin{proposition}[Relative preparation and shifting]\label{prop:shifting}
Let a compact family of smooth configurations be given on one of the
domains below, with fixed asymptotic labels, where the data and geometry
may vary as in Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item On a one-input mixed domain (the domain defining the mixed map, a separate finite
or completed torus domain, or the closed-cut piece with its ordinary
input $Z$), relative preparation makes the original tails stationary
beyond a common cutoff, preserving members already stationary there.
The subsequent shift deforms the family to gauge paths followed by
a constant mixed pair $C^c$, where $c$ is a representative of the
critical point over the output label (Appendix~\ref{app:shifting}),
keeping members which are already of this form in that subspace.
\item On the triangles and folded triangles at positive collapse scale,
and on the finite-width joined torus domains, the deformation of
Definition~\ref{def:mixed-orientation} retains the ordinary cobordism and reaches the reference
configuration described there, taken for the torus domains at the closed cut of~\ref{I2}; its
relative form preserves the subspace of configurations already of that form.
\end{enumerate}
These deformations are continuous in the topology of this section jointly in configurations and data, and coherent with ordinary gluing at the gauge input.
\end{proposition}

Coherence of the orientations at the output end is proved in Proposition~\ref{prop:transport} for
the domains of~(a), after Proposition~\ref{prop:transport} for the triangles, and in
Lemma~\ref{lem:G-properties}(ii) on the joined torus domains.

Limiting triangle orientations are not obtained by shifting to a constant triangle; they are defined
by inverse collapse (Definition~\ref{def:limit-orientation}).

Section~\ref{sec:excision} uses Propositions~\ref{prop:foundations} and~\ref{prop:shifting} on the
separate, completed and joined torus domains and on the closed-cut piece; its opening paragraph
describes how they enter.

\begin{proposition}[Ordinary determinant-one self-gluing]
\label{prop:selfgluing}
Consider the nonsingular gauge cobordisms and designated closed cuts
in the torus-excision construction of Section~\ref{sec:excision}.
Fix the ordinary Floer data on the original ends, including their
trajectory perturbations. The end critical sets are finite,
irreducible and nondegenerate, the low-dimensional trajectories are
regular, and the restricted lifting groups act freely on these sets.
There are compatible data on the new torus ends and invariant
secondary perturbations, supported in fixed compact end collars,
for which the cut moduli spaces and the required parameter families
are regular. For these choices the following hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item For fixed regular cut data and all sufficiently large neck
lengths, the index-zero solutions are exactly the gluings of matched
determinant-one cut solutions on the prescribed bundle. The gluing
fibre is the torsor of Lemma~\ref{lem:gluing-multiplicity}, and the neck-length threshold can be chosen uniformly over all index-zero solutions.
\item Determinant gluing uses the graded symmetry of
Section~\ref{sec:algebra}. Its signs are determined by the ordered
degrees and the fixed orientation choices. In the nonseparating
torus case the determinant-one count is twice the graded partial
trace, before the larger lifting-group quotient is taken.
\item Relative regular families give the integral composition and
change-of-data homotopies. In a family the data parameter is shared
by the pieces and occurs once in the linearization.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

The proof is in Appendix~\ref{app:selfgluing}. It applies the
ordinary instanton compactification of~\cite[Section~3.9]{KM-unknot}
and its signed self-gluing formula~\cite[Proposition~5.5]{KM-unknot}.
Regular cut data are essential: nondegeneracy of the end critical
points alone does not regularize a cut solution. Part~(c) is a
statement about parametrized moduli spaces, rather than a pointwise
gluing bijection in every fibre of a family that is regular as a parametrized family.
Neither Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations} nor
Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting} is used in this
proposition.

\subsection{The one-handle geometry}\label{subsec:handle-geometry}
The one- and three-handle comparisons, and the excision of Section~\ref{sec:excision}, use the
following geometry from Paper~I, Section~10.1.

\emph{Compression bodies.} Let a presentation $p$ of $M$ consist of torus compression bodies
$U_0,U_1$ with boundary $\Sigma_h\sqcup T^2$, $h\ge2$. For $k\ge0$ let $C=C_{h,g}$, $g=h+k$, be a
compression body from $\Sigma_h$ to $\Sigma_g$ which adds $k$ one-handles, and put $V_i=C\circ U_i$.
Then $V_0\cup(-V_1)$ is the torus-summed object of $M^+=M\#^k(S^1\times S^2)$. Restriction of flat
connections on $C$ gives an embedded Lagrangian correspondence $L_C\subset\Msf_h^-\times\Msf_g$.

\emph{Height Lagrangians.} The clean intersection $L(V_i)\cap(L(U_i)\circ L_C)$ is a copy of
$\SU(2)^{g-1}$. Choose a primary perturbation $\zeta_i$ of $V_i$, the \emph{height function}, whose restriction to it is a product Morse function with $2^{g-1}$ critical points. For small $t>0$ perturb the primary data of $V_i$ by $t\zeta_i$; the
resulting Lagrangians $R_i\subset\Msf_g$ are the \emph{height Lagrangians at height} $t$. The lower
data are perturbed Lagrangians $\widetilde P_i\subset\Msf_h$ of $U_i$ close to $L(U_i)$.

\emph{The folded pair.} In $\mathbb X=\Msf_h^-\times\Msf_g$ put $A_i=\widetilde P_i^-\times R_i$. The
\emph{incidence conditions} require the three pairs among $(A_0,L_C,A_1)$ to be transverse and their
triple intersection to be empty. Holomorphic triangles in $\mathbb X$ with boundary on
$(A_0,L_C,A_1)$ are the folded form of quilted triangles with seam condition $L_C$; the compression
cups and caps count them. The gauge pieces are $D_i=(-U_i)\cup_{T^2}V_i$, with
$\partial D_i=(-\Sigma_h)\sqcup\Sigma_g$; flat connections on $D_i$ restrict bijectively to pairs of
flat connections on $U_i$ and $V_i$, so $L(D_i)=L(U_i)^-\times L(V_i)$, perturbed to $A_i$. The
\emph{closed cut} of the folded pair is
\[
 Z=D_0\cup(-D_1)\cong(-M)\#M^+\#T^3 .
\]

\emph{The torus domains} (Figure~\ref{fig:torus-domains}). Near the torus component of its
matching surface, the special quintuple $X_D$ of a pair of torus compression bodies contains a flat
product region $N_X=T\times[-1,0]_s\times\RR_\theta$. Since the moduli space of the odd torus is a
point, the matching condition on its face only requires the restriction of the connection to each
torus fibre to be flat. Attaching $T\times[0,\mathsf R]_r\times\RR_\theta$ along the face gives the
\emph{collared domain} $W_{\mathsf R}$, with the same flat-fibre condition at $r=\mathsf R$; for
$\mathsf R=\infty$ it is the \emph{completed domain} $W_\infty$, whose torus tail is an end with limit
the odd flat connection $\alpha$. For the two sides, the reflected presentation of $M$ (subscript
$-$) and the enlarged presentation of $M^+$ (subscript $+$), the \emph{joined domain} $W^J_\lambda$
glues the collars of width $\lambda$ along their torus faces; the glued collar
$T\times[-\lambda,\lambda]\times\RR_\theta$ is the \emph{bridge}. The domains $W_{\pm,\mathsf R}$ and
$W_{\pm,\infty}$ are called \emph{separate}. The joined domain has gauge inputs $(-M)^\#$ and
$(M^+)^\#$ and strip output at $A_0\cap A_1$. It contains a closed hypersurface
$Z_{\rm cut}\cong Z$ in its gauge part, which cuts it into the reverse excision cobordism
$\overline{\mathcal E}_\lambda$ of Kronheimer and Mrowka~\cite[Section~5.3]{KM-unknot} and a domain
$M_\lambda$ of DFL type for the folded pair with gauge input $Z$; see~\ref{I2}.

\subsection{Small presentations}\label{subsec:admissible-intro}
\begin{definition}\label{def:admissible-intro}
Fix a Heegaard splitting with its metric data. Its \emph{small ball} consists of the primary
perturbations in the span of a finite family of sign-sensitive holonomy functions, containing the
fixed family $F_0$ of Section~\ref{sec:characters} and subject to the conditions of Paper~I,
Definition~3.3, whose norm~\eqref{eq:primary-norm}, a Lipschitz norm of the gradient, is less than
a radius $r_\chi>0$. The radius depends only on the splitting and its metric data, and is transported by isomorphisms of decorated data. A presentation is
\emph{small} if it is regular and its primary perturbation lies in the small ball of its splitting and metric data. Small presentations are admissible in the sense of Paper~I (see~\ref{I5}).
\end{definition}

The radius $r_\chi$ is fixed in Section~\ref{subsec:admissible}, so that the perturbed Lagrangians
lie in the neighbourhoods of~\ref{I5} and of Section~\ref{sec:cup}. Small fold pairs, compatible
handle data and small decompositions are defined there in terms of small presentations.

\subsection{Principal results used from Paper~I}\label{subsec:paperI-results}
The following results of Paper~I are stated there over $\FF$; only their unsigned content is used.
In addition, Sections~\ref{sec:collapse} and~\ref{sec:excision} and Appendix~\ref{app:inputs}
adapt arguments of Paper~I, Sections~7 and~10, to stabilized families of configurations that are
not solutions; each adaptation states which steps change.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(I\arabic*),ref=(I\arabic*)]
\item\label{I1} \emph{Deformation invariance of the mixed map} (Paper~I, Lemma~9.7). Let $D$ be a domain of DFL type for a pair of perturbed compression-body Lagrangians, transverse or clean as in Paper~I, with the end types of the special quintuple
and regular invariant data, whose metric is conformal to a product near each seam and on each mixed
end, and whose almost-complex structure on the section is $*_\Sigma$ near the seams. Then the
index-zero count of $D$ is chain homotopic over $\FF$ to the mixed map of that pair. The
translation-invariant data on the mixed ends may differ from those of the quintuple.
\item\label{I2} \emph{The closed cut} (Paper~I, Lemma~10.18). The joined torus domain $W^J_\lambda$
contains a closed hypersurface $Z_{\rm cut}$, identified with $Z=D_0\cup(-D_1)$, which separates it
into the reverse excision cobordism and a domain of DFL type for the folded pair $(A_0,A_1)$ with
gauge input $Z$.
\item\label{I3} \emph{Torus completion} (Paper~I, Theorem~10.32, with Propositions~10.27
and~10.30). For large collar length and width, gluing gives bijections between the index-zero
solutions on the completed torus domains and those on the collared and joined domains. The
linearized operators at the preglued configurations are invertible with uniform bounds, and each
glued solution is the unique zero of the equation in a ball of fixed radius in the gluing chart.
\item\label{I4} \emph{The folded triangle modulo two} (Paper~I, Theorem~11.8). One has
$N_Z\circ\mu_{\mathscr W}\simeq\mu_{\mathbb X}\circ(N_0\otimes N_1)$ over $\FF$, where
$\mu_{\mathscr W}$ is the gauge triangle of the pieces $D_0$, $C$, $D_1$ and $\mu_{\mathbb X}$ the
holomorphic triangle map in $\mathbb X=\Msf_h^-\times\Msf_g$; here $N_0,N_1$ are the mixed maps of the pairs $(A_0,L_C)$ and $(L_C,A_1)$, and $N_Z$ that of $(A_0,A_1)$.
\item\label{I5} \emph{Admissible neighbourhoods} (Paper~I, Definition~11.12). For each sufficiently
small height $t>0$ there is a $C^1$-neighbourhood $\mathcal N(t)$ of the pair of compression-body
Lagrangians, and of the reflected pair, such that for the product compression body ($k=0$) the
unfolding and grading statements, the mod-two folded triangle and the mod-two cup formula of
Paper~I hold for every lower pair in $\mathcal N(t)$ and every height Lagrangian at height $t$
satisfying the incidence conditions.
\item\label{I6} \emph{Uniform stationary gap} (Paper~I, Proposition~10.7). The stationary operators
on the slices of the mixed ends of the torus domains are self-adjoint with kernel the tangent space
of the critical manifold, and their nonzero spectrum avoids a fixed interval about zero, uniformly
in the collar length and, on the joined slices, in the width $\lambda\ge\lambda_*$.
\item\label{I7} \emph{Neck gluing and families} (Paper~I, Theorems~5.1 and~5.4). For regular
pieces joined along closed or ordinary ends, the index-zero solutions at large neck length are the
gluings of matched index-zero solutions of the pieces, and the count of the joined problem is the
composite of the counts of the pieces; the same holds with one index-one trajectory or one index
$-1$ member of a family. A family of data on one domain, with fixed ends and critical data, a
compactly supported parameter derivative and endpoints regular through index one, gives after a
small generic perturbation invariant under the lifting group a chain homotopy between the endpoint
counts, counted by its index $-1$ solutions, with one boundary branch for each such solution and
each index-one trajectory at an end.
\item\label{I8} \emph{Collapse inverse and contraction} (Paper~I, Propositions~7.15, 8.1 and~8.3).
Let $V$ be a regular index-zero solution of the limiting problem. For small collapse scale
$\epsilon$ the linearization of the mixed equation at the preglued configuration
$W_\epsilon=W_\epsilon(V)$ is invertible, with inverse bounded by a constant times
$1+\log(1/\epsilon)$, and in the strong norms of Paper~I, Section~8.1, by a constant times
$(1+\log(1/\epsilon))^{m+1}$. In the matching chart the nonlinear part of the equation has
derivative bounded by a constant times the strong norm on a ball of fixed radius. The contraction
gives a unique regular solution $W_\epsilon+z_*$ in that ball, with $\|z_*\|$ bounded by a power of
$1+\log(1/\epsilon)$ times the pregluing error $e_\epsilon$, which tends to zero as a positive power
of $\epsilon$.
\item\label{I9} \emph{Weighted parameter column} (Paper~I, Lemma~10.15). The conclusions of the
families statement in~\ref{I7} hold for families whose variation is translation invariant on the
mixed ends rather than compactly supported, provided the critical set on each mixed end is
connected and, with a family of stationary references solving the equation, independent of the
parameter, the variation vanishes on the gauge and ordinary ends, and the stationary operators keep
a uniform gap and an energy decay at a rate larger than the weight.
\item\label{I10} \emph{Invariant regularity} (Paper~I, Theorem~5.6). Under the compactness and
gluing hypotheses of~\ref{I7}, generic small holonomy perturbations invariant under the lifting
group, from a finite-dimensional space of directions supported near the fixed perturbation
supports, with loops of zero lifting class joined to an original end whose limit is flat on a collar
of an odd surface, and with supports and loop regions in a fixed compact set, make all moduli spaces
of index at most one regular, relative to strata that are already regular.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Complexes, graded signs and determinant lines}\label{sec:algebra}

\subsection{The homotopy category}
A complex is a finite free $\ZZ/2$-graded abelian group
$C=C^0\oplus C^1$ with an odd differential $d_C$. For a homogeneous
map $F:C\to D$ of degree $f$, set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hom-differential}
 \delta F=d_DF-(-1)^fFd_C.
\end{equation}
A closed map satisfies $\delta F=0$. Thus an odd closed map
anticommutes with the differentials. We allow sums of the two
degrees when discussing homotopies of the composites below.
The category $\cK(\ZZ)$ has these complexes as objects and closed
maps modulo boundaries as morphisms. In particular
\[
 F\simeq G\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad F-G=\delta K.
\]
For homogeneous $F,G$ of degree $f$, the homotopy $K$ can be
chosen of degree $f+1$. The notation $F\simeq\pm G$ means that
one of $F-G,F+G$ is a boundary, with a single choice of sign.

If $A$ and $B$ are closed and $B$ has degree $b$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:compose-homotopy}
 (\delta K)A=\delta(KA),\qquad
 B\delta K=(-1)^b\delta(BK).
\end{equation}
These identities justify composition of every homotopy used below.
They also show that projectivizing by the relation $[F]=[-F]$
is compatible with composition.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:acyclic}
An acyclic finite free $\ZZ/2$-graded complex over $\ZZ$ is
contractible. An even quasi-isomorphism between finite free
complexes is a chain homotopy equivalence.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $B^i=\im(d:C^{i-1}\to C^i)$. Each $B^i$ is free as a
subgroup of a finitely generated free group. If the complex is
acyclic, the exact sequence
$0\to B^i\to C^i\xrightarrow{d}B^{i+1}\to0$ splits. Relative
to these splittings the differential identifies the complementary
summand of $C^i$ with $B^{i+1}$. Its inverse on this summand,
and zero on the complement, defines a contracting homotopy, denoted
$s$, with $ds+sd=1$.
Apply this to the mapping cone of a quasi-isomorphism to obtain
the second assertion.
\end{proof}

Over $\ZZ$ the lemma is applied only to acyclic complexes and to
quasi-isomorphisms; a map that induces zero on homology need not be
null-homotopic.

\subsection{The tensor convention}
The orientation lines of the generators precede the determinant
lines of trajectories and cobordisms. With this ordering the tensor
differential is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tensor}
 d(x\otimes y)=(-1)^{|y|}dx\otimes y+x\otimes dy.
\end{equation}
For homogeneous maps $F,G$ we use
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tensor-maps}
 (F\otimes^{\mathrm r} G)(x\otimes y)
   =(-1)^{|F||y|}Fx\otimes Gy.
\end{equation}
The superscript in $\otimes^{\mathrm r}$ records that signs arise from passing the right-hand factor, as for $F^{\mathrm r}$ and $\Theta^{\mathrm r}$. Closed maps tensor to a closed map of degree $|F|+|G|$ with
this rule. The usual left tensor differential is related to
\eqref{eq:tensor} by the even chain isomorphism
\[
 T(x\otimes y)=(-1)^{|x||y|}x\otimes y.
\]
Thus a formula imported in a left tensor convention is conjugated
by $T$ at each tensor end before it is used here.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:tensor-geometric}
For a disconnected gauge end $Y_1\sqcup Y_2$, with the orientation
lines of the generators ordered as $L(a_1)\otimes L(a_2)$, gluing index-one
trajectories on the two components gives~\eqref{eq:tensor}.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A trajectory on $Y_1$ has an odd determinant line. To place it
next to $L(a_1)$, move it past $L(a_2)$; this gives
$(-1)^{|a_2|}$. A trajectory on $Y_2$ is already adjacent to
the orientation line of its generator. Summing the two contributions gives the
formula.
\end{proof}

Put $\eps_Cx=(-1)^{|x|}x$. If a homogeneous map $F$ of degree
$f$ commutes strictly with the differentials, the map
$F^{\mathrm r}=F\eps_C^f$ is closed. For strictly commuting
maps $F,G$ of degrees $f,g$, respectively,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:right-conversion}
 (FG)^{\mathrm r}=(-1)^{fg}F^{\mathrm r}G^{\mathrm r}.
\end{equation}
The discrepancy is one sign per composite. By contrast the
factor $\eps_C$ is not generally an overall sign.

For example, on $C^0=C^1=\ZZ$ with zero differential, the map
interchanging the two generators and its product with $\eps_C$
are not equal up to sign. The difference persists on homology.
This elementary example explains why an integral comparison
must specify the convention for odd cobordism maps.

\subsection{Duals, evaluation and tensors of maps}
The graded dual has
\[
 d^\vee f=-(-1)^{|f|}f\circ d,
 \qquad F^\vee(f)=(-1)^{|F||f|}f\circ F.
\]
The canonical even double-dual map is
\[
 \ev_C:C\longrightarrow C^{\vee\vee},\qquad
 \ev_C(x)(f)=(-1)^{|x||f|}f(x).
\]
The sign is necessary: the unsigned double-dual identification
does not commute with the differential in this convention.
There is an unsigned chain isomorphism
\begin{equation}\label{eq:beta}
 \beta:C^\vee\otimes^{\mathrm r} D\longrightarrow\Hom(C,D),\qquad
 \beta(f\otimes y)(x)=f(x)y.
\end{equation}
Indeed, substituting the dual differential in~\eqref{eq:tensor}
gives exactly~\eqref{eq:hom-differential} after evaluation on a
homogeneous $x$. For a tensor in $C\otimes^{\mathrm r} D$ the \emph{bending map} is
\[
 \operatorname{bend}_C=\beta\circ(\ev_C\otimes^{\mathrm r}1):
 C\otimes^{\mathrm r} D\longrightarrow\Hom(C^\vee,D).
\]
If $e_i$ is a homogeneous basis of $C$, the tensor corresponding
to a homogeneous map $F:C\to D$ is
\[
 \ten(F)=\sum_i e_i^\vee\otimes F(e_i).
\]
This expression is independent of the chosen basis. It is a
cycle if and only if $F$ is closed. For $F=1$, it is the
coevaluation tensor in the present convention.

\subsection{Determinant lines}
For a real Fredholm operator $D:X\to Y$ set
\[
 \det D=\Lambda^{\max}\ker D\otimes
       (\Lambda^{\max}\coker D)^*,\qquad
 |\det D|=\ind D\pmod2.
\]
We use the graded determinant convention of
\cite{Zinger}. Stabilization by a finite-dimensional map
$V\to Y$ with $D\oplus V$ surjective gives the local
trivializations. Enlarging the stabilization gives compatible
transition maps. This construction applies to continuous
families of Fredholm operators without requiring constant
kernel dimension.

For direct sums the determinant lines are ordered in the
order of the summands. Interchanging two summands gives
$(-1)^{\ind D_1\ind D_2}$. The dual of a tensor reverses
the order of its factors and uses nested evaluation. An
invertible operator has the canonical determinant orientation
of $\RR$; a continuous path of invertible operators preserves
this orientation.

\begin{lemma}[Ordinary gluing]\label{lem:line-gluing}
Suppose two Fredholm problems have matching ordinary ends with
invertible asymptotic operator. Gluing with a sufficiently long
neck gives a determinant-line isomorphism
\[
 \det D_1\otimes\det D_2\longrightarrow\det(D_1\#D_2)
\]
well defined up to a positive scalar. It is continuous in
compact parameter families, natural under bundle
automorphisms, and associative for disjoint necks. On
invertible operators it is the canonical identification.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Choose finite-dimensional stabilizations supported away from
the ends so that the two stabilized operators are surjective.
Patch their right inverses using cutoffs. The error tends to
zero with the neck length, so a Neumann series corrects the
patched operator to a right inverse. Projection of the patched
kernels to the kernel of the glued stabilized operator gives
an isomorphism for long necks. Its top exterior power and the
stabilization isomorphisms define the asserted map.

Enlarging stabilizations commutes with this construction.
Changing cutoffs, projections or neck length gives a path of
kernel isomorphisms, so the sign does not change. For two
disjoint necks use stabilizations supported away from both;
the two successive patching maps are homotopic to the direct
three-piece patching map. This proves associativity, with
only the graded signs caused by reordering the factors. When
both operators are invertible, no stabilization is needed
and the map is $\RR\otimes\RR\to\RR$. The gauge version is
the determinant-line construction accompanying ordinary
Floer gluing~\cite[Proposition~5.11]{Donaldson-book}; the strip
version uses the same stabilization argument for real
Cauchy--Riemann operators as for the cylinders of~\cite{Floer-Hofer}, with the gluing of
determinant lines along strip-like ends of \cite[Proposition~2.4.3]{WW-orientations}.
\end{proof}

Gluing a constant cylinder is the positive identity: its
operator is invertible, and the patched kernel map converges
to the identity as the neck grows. Weighted gluing is treated
by conjugating with the exponential weight. The resulting
asymptotic operator must be invertible, and matched ends must
use compatible weights. At a mixed end, ordinary gluing is
applied together with the stationary coordinates, which are kept
as separate finite-dimensional factors.

\section{The integral gauge convention}\label{sec:gauge}

\subsection{Almost-complex orientations with free ends}
Choose an $S^1$-invariant almost-complex germ $J_T$ near
$S^1\times N(T_0)$, with $T_0$ an almost-complex torus in its
specified orientation. On an object, such a structure is equivalently
specified by a nonvanishing vector field on $M^\#$, normalized near
$T_0$. Choose such a structure $J^0_M$ for each object, called the
\emph{reference almost-complex structure} of $M$. On a cobordism require
$J_W$ to agree with the fixed germ near $N(T_0)\times[0,1]$ and to be
translation invariant on its end collars. Its end restrictions
$J'_0,J'_1$ are not prescribed in advance.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:almost-complex}
Every decorated cobordism admits an almost-complex structure of
this kind.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The fibre of the bundle of compatible oriented almost-complex
structures is $\SO(4)/\U(2)\cong S^2$. Let
$A=N(T_0)\times[0,1]$. The primary relative obstruction lies in
$H^3(W^\#,A;\ZZ)$ and maps to $W_3(W^\#)=0$. Here an oriented
four-manifold admits a spin$^c$ structure; equivalently its second
Stiefel--Whitney class has an integral lift. The remaining relative
class is in the image of the connecting map from $H^2(A;\ZZ)$.
The restriction $H^2(W^\#;\ZZ)\to H^2(A;\ZZ)$ is surjective:
the torus summand supplies a class pairing once with $T_0$, and
this class extends by the Mayer--Vietoris sequence across
$S^2\times[0,1]$, along which the tube is attached, since it
restricts to zero on the boundary sphere of the punctured torus
summand. Thus the connecting map vanishes.

The secondary obstruction lies in $H^4(W^\#,A;\ZZ)$.
Since $A\simeq T^2$, its third cohomology vanishes; since
$W^\#$ is connected with nonempty boundary, $H^4(W^\#;\ZZ)=0$.
The relative group therefore vanishes. Homotopy extension makes
the structure equal to the prescribed germ, and a collar
reparametrization makes it translation invariant near the ends.
\end{proof}

The end restrictions are left free in
Lemma~\ref{lem:almost-complex}, since prescribing both of them would
change the obstruction problem. The passage to the reference
structures of the objects is made by the conversion maps of
Section~\ref{subsec:conversion}.

The almost-complex deformation of the anti-self-duality operator
has a complex-linear endpoint. The complex orientation of its
kernel and cokernel orients its determinant line. This
construction is invariant under the full group of bundle
automorphisms, which act complex linearly at that endpoint
\cite[Section~5.1]{KM-unknot}. Ordinary determinant gluing preserves
these orientations. In particular the action of $\phi_M$ on the
orientation lines has no additional sign twist.

\subsection{Comparison of two almost-complex orientations}
Let $v,v'$ be the normalized vector fields corresponding to
$J,J'$ on $S^1\times Y$, where $Y=M^\#$. Their difference class
$\delta(v,v')\in H^2(Y;\ZZ)$ satisfies
\[
 2\delta(v,v')=c_1(\xi_{v'})-c_1(\xi_v).
\]
The difference class is the obstruction to homotoping $v$ to $v'$
over the two-skeleton. When $H^2(Y;\ZZ)$ has two-torsion, it is not
determined by the displayed Chern-class difference.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:character-gauge}
If $\alpha\in H^1(Y;\ZZ/2)$ is the reduction of an integral
class, the comparison of the $J$- and $J'$-orientations changes
around a loop with lifting class $\alpha$ by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gauge-character}
 \xi_{J,J'}(\alpha)
   =(-1)^{\langle\delta(v,v'),\PD\alpha\rangle}.
\end{equation}
In particular it is $1$ on $\phi_M$ when $J$ and $J'$ agree
near $T_0$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Represent the loop by a path of connections whose endpoints
are identified by a bundle automorphism of class $\alpha$.
Closing up gives a bundle $P_\alpha$ on $S^1\times Y$ with
\[
 w_2(P_\alpha)=\operatorname{pr}_Y^*w_2(P)
                  +[dt]\smile\alpha.
\]
The determinant-line comparison for this closed operator is
divided by the comparison for the constant loop. The
$P$-independent orientation factor therefore cancels.

For completeness, the bundle-dependent comparison can be
computed on a reducible model $\RR\oplus L$. The anti-self-dual
operator on the $L$-summand has both the complex structure
induced by $J$ and that of $L$. On the summand where these
structures are opposite, conjugation changes the determinant
orientation by $(-1)^{\ind_{\CC}}$. The index formula gives,
for two almost-complex structures, the ratio
\[
 (-1)^{\langle c_1(L)\smile\delta(v,v'),[S^1\times Y]\rangle}.
\]
This is the relative form of the usual almost-complex
orientation calculation~\cite[Section~3(c) and the proof of Proposition~3.25]{Donaldson87}.
Changing the instanton number does not change the ratio:
instanton addition preserves both complex orientations.

Because $\alpha$ has an integral lift, so does the displayed
Stiefel--Whitney class. The reducible model therefore computes
the desired ratio. Only the term $[dt]\smile\alpha$ survives
after division by the constant-loop comparison; integration
over the circle gives~\eqref{eq:gauge-character}. The
closing-up identification is made in pair form
$\det D\otimes(\det D_{\rm const})^*$, so it introduces no
choice of orientation at the closed end. It is the real
excision identification, independent of $J$ and $J'$.
Finally $\alpha_M$ is dual to $T_0$, and the difference class
restricts to zero there when the structures have the same germ.
\end{proof}

We call $\xi_{J,J'}$ the \emph{lifting-class sign} of the conversion
from $J$ to $J'$ (Section~\ref{subsec:conversion}): it is the sign by
which the orientation of the interpolating operator changes around a
loop with lifting class $\alpha$. The lifting classes of the framed
objects are reductions of integral classes, so
Proposition~\ref{prop:character-gauge} applies to every lifting class
used below.

\subsection{Conversion maps between almost-complex structures}
\label{subsec:conversion}
A \emph{conversion} is the isomorphism $C_G(Y,J)\to C_G(Y,J')$
between the gauge complexes oriented by two almost-complex
structures, obtained by gluing to the orientation line of each
generator an oriented operator that interpolates the two
complex-linear operators; it compares the orientation lines
of~\cite[Section~5.1]{KM-unknot} for $J$ and $J'$. For a critical
point $a$, let $Q_a$ interpolate the complex operators for $J$ and
$J'$. Choose a weight for which this operator is Fredholm and choose
an orientation $\vartheta_a$ of its determinant line. Transport
$\vartheta_a$ along paths of connections. Since $J$ and $J'$ agree
near $T_0$, the lifting-class sign $\xi_{J,J'}$ is trivial on $\phi_M$
by Proposition~\ref{prop:character-gauge}, so this transport is
consistent on the framed quotient. It is unique up to one
sign after a choice at one reference connection.

Let $D_{J,a}$ be the operator on $\RR\times Y$ that interpolates the
complex-linear operator for $J$ and the operator at $a$;
by~\cite[Section~5.1]{KM-unknot}, the generator orientation line $L(a)$
of $a$ in $C_G(Y,J)$ is $\det D_{J,a}$. Let
\[
 \gamma:\det D_{J,a}\otimes\det Q_a\longrightarrow\det D_{J',a}
\]
be the determinant-line gluing of Lemma~\ref{lem:line-gluing} along the
complex-linear end, with the factors in the displayed order. We place
$\vartheta_a$ after the generator orientation line and obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Theta}
 \Theta^{\mathrm r}_{J\to J'}:
 C_G(Y,J)\longrightarrow C_G(Y,J'),\qquad
 x\longmapsto\gamma(x\otimes\vartheta_a).
\end{equation}
Its parity $t=\ind Q_a\pmod2$ is independent of $a$. Moving
the odd trajectory line past $\vartheta_a$ shows that
\[
 d\Theta^{\mathrm r}=(-1)^t\Theta^{\mathrm r}d.
\]
Thus it is a closed isomorphism of degree $t$. If
$\Theta^{\mathrm l}$ denotes the convention with $\vartheta_a$ placed
before the generator orientation line, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:theta-grading}
 \Theta^{\mathrm r}=\Theta^{\mathrm l}\eps^t.
\end{equation}
Composition of conversion maps agrees with the direct
conversion up to one sign. The sign is the same for all
generators, because the orientations $\vartheta_a$ are transported
over the connected space of connections.

\begin{definition}\label{def:gauge-map}
For $(W,E,\nu)$ choose a structure $J_W$ as in Lemma~\ref{lem:almost-complex}, with end
restrictions $J'_0,J'_1$. Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gauge-map}
 \IG(W)=\Theta^{\mathrm r}_{J'_1\to J^0_{M_1}}
        I(W^\#,E^\#,J_W)^{\phi_W}
        \Theta^{\mathrm r}_{J^0_{M_0}\to J'_0}.
\end{equation}
At the induced end structures the middle map uses the
almost-complex orientations and the stated ordering of its
determinant line. Tensor ends use~\eqref{eq:tensor-maps}.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:gauge-functor}
The homotopy class of~\eqref{eq:gauge-map} is independent of
$J_W$ up to one sign. These classes define a projective gauge
functor, and their reduction modulo two is the framed gauge
functor of Paper~I.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Compare two choices of $J_W$ after inserting their endpoint
conversions. They orient the same real determinant line over
the space of connections with fixed endpoint limits. Their
ratio is continuous and takes values in $\{1,-1\}$, so it is
constant on that space. Gluing a path at either endpoint
shows that the ratio does not change when the limiting
critical point is changed. All structures involved have the fixed
germ near the odd torus, so the lifting-class sign of
Proposition~\ref{prop:character-gauge} is trivial on the lifting
groups of the ends; this makes the assertion invariant under changing
the lift of an end critical point. Thus the ratio is one sign for all entries
of the map.

Change the metric and the secondary perturbations on $W^\#$
along a path, varying $J_W$ continuously among compatible
structures with the fixed germ. The almost-complex orientations
then orient the parametrized moduli spaces
of~\cite[Section~3.9]{KM-unknot} continuously, and the oriented
boundary of the one-dimensional family gives a chain homotopy
between the two maps, up to one sign. A change of the Floer data
on an object is the case of a product cobordism.

For composition choose an almost-complex structure on the composite as in Lemma~\ref{lem:almost-complex}, and make it cylindrical at the intermediate
cut. The restrictions then have identical structures there,
and the ordinary gauge composition theorem applies
\cite[Proposition~5.4]{KM-unknot}. Its hypothesis is that the
subgroup of $H^1(W;\ZZ/2)$ used on the composite contains the image
of the Mayer--Vietoris connecting map $H^0(Y;\ZZ/2)\to H^1(W;\ZZ/2)$
of the cut $Y$. This image vanishes when $Y$ is connected, and
composition in $\mathbf B$ takes place at connected cuts. Insert the
conversions to the reference structures of the objects. The two right-placed
conversions at the common end are inverse conversions between the
same two structures, so their composite is $\pm1$. Independence of $J_W$ gives the result
for the original choices. The product cobordism gives the
identity class. All orientation signs disappear modulo two.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Reflection, transposition and other orientation conventions}
Choose the reference structures of $M$ and $-M$ in pairs:
\[
 J^0_{-M}=\Psi_*\rho_*J^0_M.
\]
Here $\rho$ reverses the cylindrical direction and $\Psi$
is the fixed identification of the reflected torus-summed
presentation with $(-M)^\#$. Take $T_0=\{\theta_0\}\times T^2$
and the product germ there; require $\Psi$ to preserve the
odd torus, its germ, and its lifting class, and choose the pairing
involutive, so that it returns $J^0_M$ when applied to $J^0_{-M}$.
Define $\Phi_{G,M}$ by the bent product cylinder: the product
cobordism $[0,1]\times M^\#$, with both ends bent to incoming ends, is
a cobordism from $(-M)^\#\sqcup M^\#$ to the empty set. Since
$H^1([0,1]\times M^\#;\ZZ/2)=H^1(M^\#;\ZZ/2)$, its lifting group,
generated by the class of the torus tube, restricts to the diagonal
subgroup of $\phi_{-M}\times\phi_M$. Its count on
$C_G(-M)\otimes C_G(M)$ is therefore the orbit
count~\eqref{eq:orbit-count}, summed over all lifts of the two end
orbits. Its gauge map,
with the ordered determinant gluing and the almost-complex orientations
of the paired reference structures, is a pairing
$\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_M:C_G(-M)\otimes C_G(M)\to\ZZ$, and we put
$\Phi_{G,M}(y)(x)=(-1)^{|y|}\langle y,x\rangle_M$. The sign makes
$\Phi_{G,M}$ a chain map: the pairing is closed for the right tensor
differential~\eqref{eq:tensor}, so
$\langle y,dx\rangle_M=-(-1)^{|x|}\langle dy,x\rangle_M$, and with the dual
differential this gives $d^\vee\Phi_{G,M}=\Phi_{G,M}d$. Its index-zero solutions are
the constant ones, which pair a critical point with its reflection;
their operators are invertible and their end lines even. Over a pair
of orbits $([\bar a],[a])$ the two diagonal classes are those of
$(\bar a,a)$ and $(\bar a,\alpha_Ma)$. Since $\phi_M$ acts freely on
the critical points and commutes with reflection, only the first
contains a constant solution. In the bases of generators, matched by
reflection, each entry of the pairing is therefore $0$ or $\pm1$, with
exactly one nonzero entry in each row and column. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gauge-duality}
 \Phi_{G,M}:C_G(-M)\longrightarrow C_G(M)^\vee,
 \qquad \Phi_{G,-M}=\Phi_{G,M}^\vee\ev,
\end{equation}
is an even isomorphism. For the identity, the bent cylinder of $-M$ is
that of $M$ with its two incoming ends exchanged, since the pairing of
reference structures is involutive; exchanging the input lines of $y$ and $x$ costs
$(-1)^{|x||y|}$, so $\langle x,y\rangle_{-M}=(-1)^{|x||y|}\langle
y,x\rangle_M$. Since the pairing vanishes unless $|x|=|y|$, the two signs
$(-1)^{|x|}$ and $(-1)^{|y|}$ in the definitions cancel, and this is the
displayed identity.

For a morphism $(W,E,\nu):M_0\to M_1$, the \emph{reversed morphism}
$\overline W:-M_1\to-M_0$ is the same oriented decorated manifold, read
in the opposite direction.

\begin{lemma}[Gauge transpose]\label{lem:gauge-transpose}
For every morphism $(W,E,\nu):M_0\to M_1$,
\[
 \IG(W)^\vee\,\Phi_{G,M_1}\simeq\pm\,\Phi_{G,M_0}\,\IG(\overline W)
 \quad\text{in }\cK(\ZZ).
\]
The same identity holds for gauge continuation maps, with $W$ the
product cobordism carrying the two sets of data.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{prop:gauge-functor}, $\IG(\overline W)$ may be
computed with the same $J_W$. Its end restrictions are then
$\Psi_*\rho_*J'_1$ and $\Psi_*\rho_*J'_0$, and its conversions are the
reflections of those of $W$. Reflection carries each operator $Q_a$ to
an operator of the same index, so the conversions of $\overline W$ have
the same parities $t_0,t_1$, and $\IG(W)$ and $\IG(\overline W)$ both
have degree $t_0+t_1$.

Let $y\in C_G(-M_1)$ and $x\in C_G(M_0)$. Gluing the bent
cylinder of $M_1$ to $1\otimes^{\mathrm r}\IG(W)$, and that of $M_0$ to
$\IG(\overline W)\otimes^{\mathrm r}1$, gives the same bent cobordism. It
carries $J_W$, the paired reference structures at its ends, and a
conversion at each end. The first composite orders the lines as
$L(y),L(x),\vartheta_0,\det,\vartheta_1$ and the second as
$L(y),L(x),\bar\vartheta_1,\det,\bar\vartheta_0$, where $\det$ is the
line of the cobordism operator, even at the counted solutions, and
$\vartheta_i,\bar\vartheta_i$ are the orientations of the conversion
lines. The two orders differ by the constant $(-1)^{t_0t_1}$, and each
$\bar\vartheta_i$ differs from the transported $\vartheta_i$ by one
fixed sign; the constant
solutions of the bent cylinders have even lines. The
rule~\eqref{eq:tensor-maps} contributes $(-1)^{(t_0+t_1)|x|}$ to
$(\IG(\overline W)\otimes^{\mathrm r}1)(y\otimes x)$ and no sign to
$(1\otimes^{\mathrm r}\IG(W))(y\otimes x)$. Hence
\[
 \langle y,\IG(W)x\rangle_{M_1}
  =\pm(-1)^{(t_0+t_1)|x|}\langle\IG(\overline W)y,x\rangle_{M_0},
\]
with one sign for all $x$ and $y$. Both sides vanish unless
$|x|=|y|+t_0+t_1$. Evaluated on $y$ and then on $x$, the two sides
of the identity in the lemma give
$(-1)^{(t_0+t_1)|y|+|y|}\langle y,\IG(W)x\rangle_{M_1}$ and
$(-1)^{|y|+t_0+t_1}\langle\IG(\overline W)y,x\rangle_{M_0}$. These agree
up to the one sign, because $(t_0+t_1)(|x|+|y|)\equiv t_0+t_1$ whenever
the pairing is nonzero. Thus the degree-dependent reordering of an odd
conversion line is absorbed by the graded dual and by the
rule~\eqref{eq:tensor-maps}, and no grading operator remains. The
product cobordism with varying data gives the identity for gauge
continuation.
\end{proof}

If the conversion lines are instead placed on the left, put
$I_{\mathrm l}(W)=\Theta^{\mathrm l}_1I(W)\Theta^{\mathrm l}_0$.
In the induced end gradings the unconverted gauge map $I(W)$ is
even. If the two conversions have parities $t_0,t_1$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gauge-dictionary}
 \IG(W)=(-1)^{t_0t_1}I_{\mathrm l}(W)\eps^{t_0+t_1}.
\end{equation}
This is an identity of maps in the stated conventions. The factor
$\eps^{t_0+t_1}$ depends on the degree, so on an object whose
homology has both parities the two maps differ by a grading
operator and not by an overall sign.

A $\ZZ/4$ grading compatible with all the orientation data
would provide a further regrading
$\eta(x)=(-1)^{g(x)(g(x)-1)/2}x$. The elementary identity
\[
 \eta(g+d)\eta(g)^{-1}
   =(-1)^{dg+d(d-1)/2}
\]
explains how an odd grading operator can then be converted
into a conjugation and one sign per cobordism map. The
comparison of such a lift with the grading data of
Scaduto~\cite{Scaduto} is left open. Theorem~\ref{thm:A} is
therefore stated for $\IG$, and the mod-two equality of the two
gauge functors is not used to identify the integral maps.

\section{Determinant lines on mixed configuration spaces}
\label{sec:determinants}

This section orients the mixed moduli spaces over the integers. The
orientation of a configuration is obtained by deforming it, with the
shifting of Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting}, to a gauge path followed
by an invertible constant pair, and transporting back the
almost-complex orientation of the path tensored with the canonical
orientation of the constant pair. Proposition~\ref{prop:transport}
shows that this is independent of the deformation for domains with one
gauge input; Definition~\ref{def:mixed-orientation} gives the
corresponding construction for triangles and joined torus domains.
The first two subsections fix the operators whose determinant lines
are oriented and show that the orientations of solutions do not depend
on the choices made in realizing them, including the end weights.

\subsection{Realizations of the linearized mixed operator}
Let $c$ be a smooth configuration on a mixed domain, with
its specified limits. Denote the mixed equation by
$\mathcal F(c)=0$. A chart $\Phi$ centred at $c$ incorporates
the exact matching condition and the normal seam condition.
Its differential identifies a fixed linear boundary problem
with the tangent space to the configuration space.

\begin{definition}\label{def:realization}
A permitted realization of the linearized problem at $c$
is an operator of the form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:realization}
 L_c=\bigl(\mathcal S\,d\mathcal F_c,
              \lambda^2d_{A'}^{*,g'}\pi_A\bigr)\,d\Phi_0.
\end{equation}
It acts on the prescribed weighted spaces with stationary
mixed-end coordinates included. Here $\pi_A$ denotes the connection
component of a tangent vector to the configuration space. At mixed
ends we use spatially Coulomb stationary tangent representatives and
matching lifts whose remaining divergence decays, so that the scalar
row takes values in the weighted target. Both rows are transported
by the chart differential. A change of stationary lifts likewise
transports their finite scalar columns as well as the equation
columns. The map $\mathcal S$ is a continuously chosen invertible
target identification, equal to the identity outside a compact set.
The slice centre $A'$ is a smooth connection differing from the
connection in $c$ only on a compact core and sufficiently little in
$C^0$. The metric is $g'=f^2g$, with $f=1$ off the collapse region
and $f$ depending only on the coordinates of the planar factor near a
seam, and $\lambda>0$ equals one outside a compact set. The chart has
the same end and seam conventions as the original mixed problem.
Changes of all these choices are taken inside their fixed components.
In a comparison of weights, the definition also requires that the full
stationary and parameter columns belong continuously to every
intervening weighted target.
\end{definition}

The last condition is part of the definition because a configuration
which is not a solution may decay more slowly than the weights of a
spectral gap allow. The restriction on the target identification
prevents an arbitrary reversal of orientation from being included as
a change of realization. The reference identification is the one
induced by the geometric charts, and its permitted component is fixed
