before orientations are transported.

For the collapse contraction we also use the extended realization
of Proposition~\ref{prop:affine-square}. Its centre and geometric
target identification can have stationary limits different from
those of the configuration and the identity, respectively. Its
source representatives are Coulomb for the varying centre, both
rows are transported, and differentiation of the moving equation
target contributes the residual term specified there. These extended
realizations form a separate family at a fixed positive scale; they
are not compactly supported changes in the sense of
Definition~\ref{def:realization}.

\begin{lemma}[Realization comparison]\label{lem:realization}
Under the Fredholm and continuity statements of
Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(a), permitted realizations
at a fixed configuration have canonically identified
orientation double covers. The identification is continuous
in compact families and commutes with ordinary gluing. At a
regular zero of index zero it preserves the canonical determinant
orientation.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Changes of charts act on the entire operator in
\eqref{eq:realization}. Together with target identifications,
they are bounded invertible changes of source and target and induce
the standard naturality maps on determinant lines. An interval of
weights permitted by Definition~\ref{def:realization} gives a continuous Fredholm path after conjugation
to fixed spaces, and hence an identification of orientation covers.
At a solution, improved asymptotic decay identifies the kernels,
cokernels and common stabilized determinant lines throughout the
permitted gap. For a change of slice centre at a solution, split the
tangent space into infinitesimal gauge directions and a
fixed complement. Gauge invariance gives
$d\mathcal F_c\,d_{A_c}=0$. The gauge-fixed operator is then
block triangular. Its gauge block is invertible by the
Poincar\'e estimate and the smallness of $A'-A_c$; its other
block is the intrinsic linearized equation on the quotient.
Thus invertibility at a regular zero of index zero is unchanged.

Conformal changes in the specified unperturbed region change
neither the anti-self-duality equation in four dimensions nor
the surface Cauchy--Riemann equation or the normal seam subspace.
The same block argument applies to the gauge row. Positive row
multiplication preserves orientations. These paths give the
comparison. A homotopy between two such paths gives an orientation
cover over a square, so its endpoint identification is unchanged.
The gluing statement follows by choosing the changes away
from the ordinary gluing collars and then using continuity.
At a regular zero of index zero every operator in these paths is
invertible, so the canonical orientation is preserved.
\end{proof}

The lemma uses the Fredholm families of
Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(a), including those on the completed
domains. Two situations are treated separately. At a configuration
which is not a solution, the block-triangular argument above does not
apply to a change of Coulomb centre; where such a configuration occurs,
in the collapse argument, the two-parameter determinant-line comparison
of Proposition~\ref{prop:affine-square} is used instead. A reference
path in a small weighted space may fail to lie in a larger weighted
space. For weight comparisons of its endpoint orientations, truncate
its decaying coordinates on a common far end while retaining the exact
stationary family. The truncation homotopy is taken at the initial
small weight. The truncated path, together with the endpoint truncation
segments at solutions or stationary references, admits the larger
weights. Determinant transport around this homotopy gives the
comparison; the decay of the original path is left unchanged.

\subsection{Independence of the end weights}
\label{subsec:weights}
Reverse incoming ends to outward coordinates. Write an autonomous
end operator as $\partial_s-\mathcal A_e$. Its stationary kernel
is the primary tangent space on a mixed end; at an ordinary end
it is zero. Choose $\gamma_e,\gamma_e^{\rm opp}>0$ so that the
negative spectrum is at most $-\gamma_e$ and the positive spectrum
is at least $\gamma_e^{\rm opp}$; they need not be equal. The
permitted positive primal weights satisfy $r_e<\gamma_e$, and the
formal adjoint is paired at the dual weight $-r_e$. Mixed-end
stationary coordinates remain a separate finite summand.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:weights}
Consider the mixed domains and ordinary strips used here, with a
weight in the open gap above at each autonomous end, or the weight
zero at an ordinary end. Solutions, the gauge transformations between
them with prescribed stationary limits, and the determinant lines of
the linearized operators at solutions do not depend on the choice of
weights. The identifications are transitive, respect the lifting
actions and ordered gluing, and give the same oriented counts and
homotopies whenever their small-weight compactifications have been
established.
\end{proposition}
The proof, in Appendix~\ref{app:weights}, compares decay rates,
kernels, cokernels and stabilized determinant lines across the weights
by the standard arguments for asymptotically translation-invariant
operators with a spectral gap. Two points are specific to mixed ends.
The component of a solution in the stationary kernel of
$\mathcal A_e$ is integrated from infinity and uses the prescribed
limit in the stationary critical family, which is carried as a
separate finite summand at every weight. The component in
the positive spectral subspace is also integrated from infinity, so
decay at every rate below $\gamma_e$ holds without any restriction from
the opposite gap $\gamma_e^{\rm opp}$.

Proposition~\ref{prop:weights} concerns solutions. A configuration
which is not a solution and decays slowly need not lie in a space with
a larger weight; reference paths are compared by the truncation
described after Lemma~\ref{lem:realization}.

\subsection{Orientations transported from constant pairs}
A constant pair $C_x$ joins the gauge critical point $a_x$
to the symplectic intersection point $x$. Its operator is
invertible. Let $L_S(x)=L_G(a_x)$. More generally, a constant pair
$C^{c}$ may be built from any representative $c$ of the critical
point over $x$ (Appendix~\ref{app:shifting}). It is equivalent to
$C_x$ under the lifting group, by a gauge transformation when $c$
lies in the orbit of $a_x$. Its operator is invertible at the data in
use, which are small, by the invertibility clause of
Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(b). Its positive element corresponds
to that of $C_x$ under conjugation, and the almost-complex orientation
identifies $L_G(c)$ with $L_G(a_x)$, compatibly with transport
(Appendix~\ref{app:shifting}). For a mixed configuration
$c$ with gauge input $a$ and symplectic output $x$, shifting
and gluing identify it, through a path of configurations,
with a configuration of the form $A\# C^{c'}$, where $A$ is a
gauge path from $a$ to a representative $c'$. The configurations of
this form make up the \emph{terminal subspace} $\mathcal T_x$ of
Appendix~\ref{app:shifting}: they agree with a constant pair outside
the gauge input end.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:transport}
Let $c$ be a configuration on a one-input domain of
Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting}(a), with gauge input $a$ and output
$x$. There is a relative orientation isomorphism
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Gamma}
 \Gamma_c:L_G(a)\otimes\det D_c\longrightarrow L_S(x),
\end{equation}
defined up to a positive scalar, which at a configuration $A\#C^{c'}$
of the terminal subspace is the almost-complex orientation of $A$
followed by the canonical orientation of the invertible constant pair.
It is independent of the shifting path, natural under the lifting
group, and coherent under ordinary gluing of gauge paths at the input
and of strips at the output.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The construction follows \cite[Section~4.7]{DFL}, where the orientation
is fixed by gluing to an invertible constant pair. Here it is carried
out on configurations rather than gauge classes, relative to the
terminal subspace, using Propositions~\ref{prop:foundations}
and~\ref{prop:shifting}. On $\mathcal T_x$ a common gluing
length identifies the determinant line of $A\#C^{c}$ with the gauge
line of $A$ tensored with the determinant line of the invertible
constant pair. The gauge factor is oriented by the almost-complex
construction and the second factor canonically, so this terminal
orientation is continuous on compact families in $\mathcal T_x$.

Let $p_1,p_2$ be two shifting paths from $c$, ending at terminal
configurations $t_1,t_2$. Apply the relative family shifting of
Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting}(a) to the compact one-parameter family
$p_1^{-1}p_2$ from $t_1$ to $t_2$. Its relative clause keeps the tracks
of the two endpoints in the terminal subspace, and the family ends in
it. This gives a square whose top edge and two sides lie in the
terminal subspace, where the terminal orientation is continuous. Since
the square is contractible, transport along $p_1$ and along $p_2$ gives
the same orientation at $c$. In particular $t_1$ and $t_2$ are joined in
the terminal subspace. For a loop $\ell$ at $c$, taking $p_2=\ell\cdot p_1$
shows that the orientation monodromy of a loop with fixed limits is
trivial. This proves path independence of the orientation transported
from the constant reference.

For a strip attached at the output, first shift the original
mixed configuration to $A\#C^{c}$. Then shift $C^{c}$ together
with the attached strip to another gauge path followed by
$C^{c'}$, with $c'$ a representative over $y$. Associativity of
determinant gluing identifies both orders of this construction with
gauge gluing at the final reference. Continuity transports that
equality back to the original configuration. The gauge-end statement
is the same argument with the extra path attached before shifting.

The lifting automorphism preserves gauge orientations,
conjugates the constant-pair operator, and commutes with
gluing. Conjugation preserves the canonical orientation of
an invertible operator. Hence the construction descends to
the framed quotient. If shifting reaches the other lift of
$a_x$, the two end lines are identified by this same action.
\end{proof}

The pure strip orientation is defined by applying
\eqref{eq:Gamma} to $C_x\#u$ and cancelling the constant
pair. This is the transported orientation construction
of~\cite[Section~4.7]{DFL}.

Domains with two gauge inputs are oriented in the same way, after the
output region has been separated from the inputs by a closed cut. For
a mixed triangle, the closed three-manifold that separates the two
gauge inputs from the output (Paper~I, Lemma~9.11) writes its gauge
region as an ordinary two-input cobordism followed by a truncated
output DFL domain. For the folded triangle the cut is $Z$ and the
compression-body arms of the output domain are $D_0,D_1$
(Section~\ref{subsec:handle-geometry}). A configuration is not required
to be critical at this finite cut. On a compact family at positive
collapse scales, the cut first receives a product collar. This is done
by a change of metric at fixed configuration, on compact ranges of
positive scales, which raises the compression scale near the cut to one
and makes the metric a product there (Paper~I, Lemma~9.12, and the
corresponding path for the folded triangle in Paper~I, Theorem~11.8).

The finite-width joined torus domains $W^J_\lambda$ of
Section~\ref{subsec:handle-geometry} have the same structure, with no
collapse scale. By~\ref{I2}, the closed cut $Z_{\rm cut}\cong Z$
decomposes $W^J_\lambda$ into the reverse excision cobordism, which is
the ordinary two-input cobordism, and the closed-cut piece $M_\lambda$,
a truncated output DFL region for the pair $(A_0,A_1)$ with
compression-body arms $D_{0,\lambda},D_{1,\lambda}$. After the
compactly supported metric change of Paper~I, Lemma~10.18(v), near the
arcs of the cut away from the bridge ends and the matching lines, the
cut has a product collar. The configuration spaces do not depend on
the metric (Appendix~\ref{app:shift-reduction}), so the change is a
transport along a path of data. The construction below applies
verbatim with the output region $M_\lambda$ and the cut $Z_{\rm cut}$,
on compact ranges of widths.

Prepare the output DFL region and extend it positively, as in the
proof of \cite[Lemma~4.62]{DFL}. Then shift the output region far along
the cut collar until it becomes a constant mixed pair, leaving the
two-input gauge cobordism unchanged; Appendix~\ref{app:shift-local}
gives the formulas. We call this deformation the \emph{localized
shift}. It ends at a configuration $A_E\#C^{c}$: a constant mixed pair
$C^c$, with $c$ a representative over the output label $y$, glued at a
parallel copy of the cut inside its product collar to a configuration
$A_E$ on the ordinary two-input cobordism with the original inputs.

At the end of the deformation remove the compact nonprimary terms
whose dependence or output crosses the cut. On the constant mixed
factor use its zero-secondary reference datum, retaining the fixed
compatible end structures. These are homotopies of Fredholm data
supported in a compact set, used only to transport orientations.
Ordinary determinant gluing then gives the ordinary cobordism line
tensored with the canonically oriented constant-pair line.

\begin{definition}\label{def:mixed-orientation}
Let $c$ be a configuration on a mixed triangle or a folded triangle
at a positive collapse scale, or on a finite-width joined torus domain
$W^J_\lambda$. A configuration is \emph{terminal} if it agrees with a
constant pair $C^{c'}$ beyond a parallel copy of the cut in its product
collar and on a two-sided collar of that copy; the configurations
$A_E\#C^{c'}$ above are terminal. The \emph{reference orientation} at
a terminal configuration is the almost-complex orientation of the
ordinary cobordism line of $A_E$, with the input lines in cyclic
order, tensored with the canonical orientation of the invertible
constant-pair line through ordinary determinant gluing at the copy of
the cut, after the compact data homotopy above. The \emph{orientation
at positive scale} of $c$ is the isomorphism obtained by
transporting the reference orientation back to $c$ along the
localized shift. Its source is the tensor product, in this order, of
the input lines, the conversion line at the cut when one is present,
and $\det D_c$; this placement of the conversion line is
Convention~\ref{conv:interior-line}, stated at the beginning of
Section~\ref{sec:excision}. Its target is the output line. On the joined torus domains the reference orientation is
given explicitly by~\eqref{eq:G-terminal}.
\end{definition}

The relative form of the localized shift
(Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting}(b)) keeps terminal configurations
terminal. The square argument in the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:transport} therefore shows that the orientation
of Definition~\ref{def:mixed-orientation} is independent of the
deformation and coherent with ordinary gluing. Associativity is
applied before any factor is reordered.

\subsection{Boundary orientations of one-parameter families}
A parametrized family is oriented by placing the oriented
parameter space before the determinant line of the vertical
operator. At a regular point this induces the usual
orientation of the finite-dimensional zero set. To see
continuity when the vertical kernel dimension changes,
choose one stabilization on a neighbourhood in the parameter
space. The orientation is then the exterior-power
orientation of the stabilized kernel with the fixed
stabilization factor cancelled. Transition maps between
such neighbourhoods are the determinant-bundle transition
maps, so no basis of the moving kernel is chosen.

For a one-parameter regular family, the boundary orientation
is outward normal first. At a strip break the translation
vector is the positive vector $\partial_su$. Moving its
odd line past the preceding generator factors gives
exactly the signs in~\eqref{eq:tensor}. At a parameter
endpoint the two outward normals are opposite. It follows
that a compactified one-dimensional family yields an
identity of the form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:family-boundary}
 F_1-F_0=\delta K,
\end{equation}
with the Koszul signs already included in $\delta$.
If an orientation for the whole family has been chosen
only up to sign, the corresponding endpoint identity
has one overall sign, not independently chosen signs for
its separate matrix entries.

The compatibility of a geometric boundary collar with
this determinant-line orientation rests on ordinary
linear gluing at the end where the break occurs
(Lemma~\ref{lem:line-gluing}) and on the direction of the
translation vector along the gluing collar; the computation is
carried out in Lemma~\ref{lem:family-signs}. This comparison of
collars, and not only an orientation of the interior of the moduli
space, is what \eqref{eq:family-boundary} requires.

\section{The mixed isomorphism over the integers}\label{sec:vertex}

\subsection{Differentials and the mixed map}
For an index-one gauge trajectory or holomorphic strip,
its sign is positive when the relative orientation agrees
with its translation vector. For a regular index-zero
mixed solution, its sign is the comparison of
\eqref{eq:Gamma} with the canonical element of its
invertible determinant line. Set
\begin{align}
 d_Go_a&=\sum_b\sum_{[A]\in\mathcal M_G^0(a,b)}
                   \operatorname{sign}(A)o_b,\notag\\
 d_So_x&=\sum_y\sum_{[u]\in\mathcal M_S^0(x,y)}
                   \operatorname{sign}(u)o_y,\label{eq:differentials}\\
 N_po_a&=\sum_x\sum_{[c]\in\mathcal M_N^0(a,x)}
                   \operatorname{sign}(c)o_x.\notag
\end{align}
The superscript $0$ denotes dimension after quotienting
translation when that action is present. All sums are
finite by the compactness and energy-index bounds of
Paper~I.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:N-chain}
The transported orientations give
$d_S^2=0$ and $d_SN_p=N_pd_G$ over $\ZZ$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The compactified one-dimensional strip space has two-level
broken strips as its boundary. Coherence in
Proposition~\ref{prop:transport} identifies their boundary
orientation with the product of the two zero-dimensional
signs, with the sign from the translation factor. The
oriented boundary count is zero, yielding $d_S^2=0$.
For the mixed domain defining $N_p$, the two types of boundary are a
gauge trajectory followed by a mixed solution and a mixed
solution followed by a strip. The outward-normal
orientations of the two ends give opposite signs. Their
sum is $d_SN_p-N_pd_G$. The index and energy restrictions
exclude additional boundary strata in this dimension.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The unit diagonal}
The filtration used in Paper~I orders the finite generator
set so that every index-zero mixed solution goes from a
generator to a generator of no larger filtration. At equal
filtration the only solutions are the unique small
deformations of the constant pairs. This is a statement
about the set of solutions, before orienting it.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:triangular}
Let $F$ be an integral endomorphism of a finite filtered
free module. (For the mixed map, the source and target bases are
identified through $L_S(x)=L_G(a_x)$.) Suppose its associated graded map is diagonal
with each diagonal entry in $\{1,-1\}$. Then $F$ is an
integral isomorphism. Its inverse preserves the filtration.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write $F=D(1+U)$, with $D$ the diagonal sign matrix and
$U$ strictly filtration-lowering. If there are $r$
filtration steps then $U^r=0$, and
\[
 F^{-1}=(1-U+U^2-\cdots+(-1)^{r-1}U^{r-1})D^{-1}.
\]
All coefficients are integral.
\end{proof}

Each diagonal solution is one regular point and contributes $1$ or
$-1$, so Lemma~\ref{lem:triangular} already gives the invertibility
of $N_p$. With the normalization by constant pairs the diagonal
entries are $+1$.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:N-isomorphism}
The map $N_p$ is a chain isomorphism. Its diagonal entries
are $+1$ in the constant-pair normalization. Its homotopy
class is independent of regularizing data, after the
corresponding gauge continuation is inserted; for a change of the
ordinary strip structure the symplectic continuation map is inserted
at the output.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Take the segment from zero regularizing data to the
chosen small data. At a constant pair the linearization
is invertible. The chart implicit-function theorem
therefore supplies a nearby branch of regular solutions.
The uniqueness statement in the triangularity argument
identifies this branch with the diagonal solution. The charts and
the continuous Fredholm families of
Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(a) make the branch, which is
continuous in the chart norm, continuous for determinant-line
transport; the constant pair is invertible by
Lemma~\ref{lem:constant-pairs}. Its sign is locally
constant and begins at the canonical sign $+1$.

The filtration now gives an inverse integral matrix.
Since $N_p$ commutes with the differentials, its inverse
does too. At fixed ordinary ends, a regular compact-data homotopy gives
\eqref{eq:family-boundary}. A change of the gauge-end function
is treated by the inserted-cylinder comparison in
Proposition~\ref{prop:end-data}(i), which retains the ordinary
continuation map, and a change of the ordinary strip structure by
part~(ii) of that proposition. This proves the stated independence.
\end{proof}

The normalization uses two properties of the segment. The secondary
perturbations are supported away from the strip end, so the strip
operator is constant along it. The segment lies in a star-shaped
neighbourhood of zero on which all reference constant-pair operators
are invertible. A general path of perturbations has neither property,
which is why the segment is used.

\subsection{Changes of the ordinary end data}
\label{subsec:end-data}
A change of a far-end equation is not compactly supported on the
mixed domain. It is compared by inserting the ordinary continuation
domain.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:end-data}
Fix primary data and the mixed domain defining a mixed map, such as
the domain defining $N_p$, or the domain of the pair $(A_0,A_1)$ with
gauge input $Z$ at a fixed positive width.
Its mixed maps satisfy the following integral chain homotopies.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item If $f_0,f_1$ are two sufficiently small invariant gauge
trajectory perturbations vanishing near the whole primary critical
set, with the same symplectic end datum, then
\[
 N_1\cont_G(f_0,f_1)-N_0=d_S H_G+H_Gd_{G,0}.
\]
\item If the gauge end is fixed and $J_0,J_1$ are regular ordinary
strip structures on the same ordered primary pair, then
\[
 \cont_S(J_0,J_1)N_0-N_1=d_{S,1}H_S+H_Sd_G.
\]
For the pair $(A_0,A_1)$ the output pair is the product pair and the
structures and continuation are split.
\end{enumerate}
The homotopies $H_G$ and $H_S$ count the isolated points of
parametrized moduli spaces of vertical index $-1$.
\end{proposition}

The proposition concerns changes of the ordinary end data at fixed
primary data. Changes of the primary data, for presentations in one
small ball, are treated in
Proposition~\ref{prop:primary-naturality}.

\begin{proof}
This is the usual neck-stretching argument for continuation maps; we
record the points specific to the mixed equation. The argument uses
Propositions~\ref{prop:foundations} and~\ref{prop:shifting}, and the
neck gluing and families results recalled in~\ref{I7}.

\emph{The stretched domains.} For part~(i), let $\beta$ rise from zero
to one on $[-1,1]$, and let $\chi$ be the cutoff of the end
perturbation in the mixed equation, equal to one for $s\leq-3$ and
zero for $s\geq-2$. On the incoming gauge end use the end perturbation
\[
 q_T(s)=\chi(s)\bigl(f_0+\beta(s+T)(f_1-f_0)\bigr),
 \qquad T\geq5.
\]
It equals $f_0$ on the far end and $f_1$ on the long cylinder between
the transition band and the mixed piece. Translate a fixed regular
ordinary continuation perturbation with the $\beta$ band, and keep the
secondary data of $N_1$ on the mixed piece. For large $T$ the supports
of these two perturbations, and the regions on which they depend, are
disjoint. The continuation piece is then a fixed nonautonomous
cylinder, so a rigid continuation trajectory has index zero and is not
divided by translation. For part~(ii), choose a continuation structure
$J_{01}(\sigma,t)$ from $J_0$ to $J_1$, with zero Hamiltonian and
constant end collars. On the outgoing strip of $N_0$, keep its
original structure until $s=T-1$ and use $J_{01}(s-T,t)$ afterwards.
The far output equation is $J_1$ for every finite $T$, and the gauge
end and the stationary mixed ends do not change. In both parts the
far-end equations are fixed, and the derivative of the data in $T$ is
supported in the translated transition band. In coordinates in which
the two pieces are fixed, the stretched domain is their gluing along a
neck of length about $T$ that carries the fixed autonomous equation;
this is the setting of~\ref{I7}.

\emph{Step 1: energy and critical points.} The smallness neighbourhood for
$f_0,f_1$ is chosen so that no new critical points occur: otherwise
compactness of the perturbed critical equation would give a critical
point of the functional with the primary perturbation outside the
neighbourhood of the critical set on which both functions vanish. The two end perturbations therefore have the
same critical points and Hessians. In part~(i) the energy identity for
the modified curvature is
\[
 E_T=8\pi^2\kappa+2\|\eta_T(A)\|^2
                 +2\int\partial_s q_T(s)(A_s)\,ds,
 \qquad
 \partial_s q_T=\beta'(s+T)(f_1-f_0)+\chi'(s)f_1.
\]
where $\kappa$ is the topological energy and $\eta_T$ the secondary perturbation of the stretched domain. The last integral is bounded in absolute value by
$\|f_1-f_0\|_\infty+\|f_1\|_\infty$, and the translated secondary
perturbations have uniformly bounded norm. The gradient of the
autonomous function $f_1$ on the growing middle cylinder is part of
the modified curvature, so it is not estimated as a compactly
supported source. In part~(ii) the Hamiltonian is zero, so the energy
of a section is twice its symplectic area even for domain-dependent
$J$, with no term involving the derivative of $J$, and the transition
adds no curvature or boundary term. In both parts, configurations with
the same intermediate critical lift have cancelling actions at the
neck, and ordinary determinant gluing makes the index additive. The
relation between index and topological energy therefore gives an
energy bound and a lower bound for the index that do not depend on
$T$.

\emph{Step 2: transversality.} Regularity is needed for the stretched
domains and for the interpolating families used below. In these
families the gauge perturbations vary with fixed compactly supported
dependence and output, and the strip structures vary on a compact set.
The far ends and the collars of the genus matching surfaces stay fixed. Coefficients at an ordinary
Lagrangian boundary component vary through a compact family of
compatible structures with the same totally real boundary condition,
and on a product target the structures stay split. A change of the
principal coefficients of the section operator on a compact set is not
a relatively compact perturbation. Instead, local elliptic estimates, a
finite cover of the parameter space and the fixed parametrices on the
ends give Fredholm estimates with a compact remainder. The local
estimates for the operator and for its weak adjoint vary continuously
with the coefficients, and both rows and all stationary columns of the
operator are transported.

For mixed solutions, with the strip structure prescribed, invariant
holonomy perturbations supported in a fixed compact set detect the
whole cokernel, as in Paper~I, Theorem~5.6(ii). The scalar component of a cokernel
element vanishes because the scalar Neumann operator is invertible.
The gauge component vanishes on the support region, because it pairs to zero with the holonomy perturbations along loops of zero lifting class supported there. Every perturbation term has its output in that region, so the adjoint equation is local outside it, and unique continuation on the connected gauge region gives vanishing everywhere. The section
component vanishes by Green's formula on a genus matching arc,
extension by zero and unique continuation, applied on each component
of the section. This uses that every component of the section meets a
genus matching arc and that the gauge region is connected, which holds
for the domains of the proposition.

For continuation strips with domain-dependent $J$ in the class above,
let $u$ be a nonconstant solution. The set where $u_t\neq0$ is dense in
any region where $J$ may vary: if $u_t$ vanished on an open set, so
would $u_s$, and unique continuation would make $u$ constant. If a
cokernel element of the parametrized operator is nonzero, choose a point of that set at which it is also nonzero (its zero set has empty interior, by unique continuation for the adjoint equation). There the variations
$Y\mapsto Yu_t$ of compatible structures span the tangent space of the
target, contradicting the pairing with the cokernel element. Because
the variations are localized in the domain, the strip need not be
injective. At a constant solution, the energy identity for the
linearized operator on the fixed tangent Lagrangians makes the
decaying kernel zero. Its index is zero by deformation to an
autonomous constant strip, during which the asymptotic operators stay
invertible by transversality. For product targets use independent
variations on each nonconstant factor; each constant factor is
invertible. This transversality argument uses only the linearized
strip operator and no part of Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}; it is
the input for continuation strips cited in the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(c), so that citation is not
circular.

We choose perturbations inductively in the index, starting from the
lowest index allowed by the energy bound. At each stage the solutions of smaller index are already regular, those of negative index, counting the parameter, are absent, and outside a neighbourhood of the already regular broken configurations the solutions of the next index form a compact set, and a
finite-dimensional space of perturbations makes it regular. This space
is then fixed, and later choices are small enough to preserve the
earlier regularity and the end collars. The Sard--Smale theorem for
parametrized families then gives data that are regular for
parametrized families through index one, with the data parameter
counted once. In the parametrized operator
$(v,\tau)\mapsto D_bv+\tau C_b$ the single column $C_b$ contains
$(\partial_bJ_b)u_t$, the transport of the target and the variation
of the gauge perturbations; ordinary gluing copies this column once,
onto the piece that carries it. Monotonicity and the exclusion of bubbles, each of which costs index four, make the regular continuation spaces compact up to breaking through index one.

\emph{Step 3: compactness and gluing.} For large $T$, the results
of~\ref{I7} apply to the two pieces: the continuation piece and the
mixed piece of $N_1$ in part~(i), and the mixed piece of $N_0$ and the
continuation strip in part~(ii). A limit of solutions of index $d$
consists of the two pieces and broken trajectories $W_j$ along the
neck, with
\[
 d-I_1-I_2-\sum_j\ind D_{W_j}=4n,\qquad
 E_{\rm lost}=4\pi^2 n,\qquad n\geq0,
\]
where $I_1,I_2$ are the indices on the two pieces; the continuation
piece contributes its full index. Both pieces are regular, so their
indices are nonnegative, and every nonconstant trajectory on the
neck has positive index. Hence for large $T$ there are no solutions of
negative index, simultaneously for all labels by the uniform lower
index bound. At $d=0$ both pieces have index zero, there are no broken
trajectories and $n=0$. The continuation pieces keep their full
transition data in the limit, and no energy term involving the
derivative of $J$ arises. By~\ref{I7}, gluing is then a bijection from the matched
pairs of rigid solutions on the two pieces onto the rigid solutions of
the stretched domain, with one length threshold for the finitely many
pairs.

The quotient by the lifting group is kept in the gauge construction.
The connected cylinder adds no lifting class, since the connecting image from $H^0$ of the connected cut vanishes~\cite[Proposition~5.4]{KM-unknot}; its bundle
automorphisms identify the lifting groups of its two ends, and the
fibre product over the common critical orbit gives exactly the
multiplicity of matrix composition. A strip adds no gauge region and
no lifting factor. In both parts the orientations are compared by
ordinary determinant gluing (Lemma~\ref{lem:line-gluing}) before the
line of the intermediate generator is cancelled. For a nonautonomous
continuation, the full stabilized operator is kept on its compact part,
with its two prescribed end models, and the autonomous inverse is used
only on the matched tail. The gluing exact sequence and its
independence of the cutoffs then apply with both far ends fixed,
without replacing the continuation by an autonomous trajectory.
Relative mixed shifting (Propositions~\ref{prop:shifting}
and~\ref{prop:transport}) and associativity preserve the
constant-pair normalization, and the constant strip carries its
canonical orientation. All maps here are even. Thus for large $T$ the count $F_T$ of rigid solutions on the stretched domain is
\[
 F_T=N_1\cont_G(f_0,f_1)\quad\text{in (i)},\qquad
 F_T=\cont_S(J_0,J_1)N_0\quad\text{in (ii)},
\]
with no independent sign for a component or a matrix entry.

\emph{The homotopy.} Fix such a length $T_*$. If necessary, make an
arbitrarily small compactly supported perturbation of the endpoint
data that makes index one regular; by compactness and local uniqueness
it does not change the regular rigid count. In part~(i), interpolate
the inserted data to those of $N_0$ by replacing $\beta$ with
$b\beta$, $b\in[0,1]$, and interpolating the compactly supported
perturbations; the far input stays $f_0$. In part~(ii), interpolate to
$N_1$ through compactly supported changes of the strip structure and
of the gauge perturbations, with far output $J_1$. Along either path
the far ends are fixed and the data change in a compact set, so the
parametrized moduli spaces are regular through index one by the
transversality above. Compactness holds with the coefficient family on
the compact part, with index loss in nonnegative multiples of four and
positive indices of ordinary breaks. As in~\ref{I7}, the compactified
one-dimensional parametrized space has as boundary only the two
endpoint counts and breaks along index-one trajectories at ordinary
ends, with collars given by gluing at the ordinary end. With the
parameter oriented first, \eqref{eq:family-boundary} gives the
displayed identities, with $H_G$ or $H_S$ the count of parametrized
solutions of vertical index $-1$; this holds also when the two data
differ in the strip structure on a compact set. Finally
Proposition~\ref{prop:weights} extends these counts from their common
small weight to all permitted weights; slow reference paths are
prepared before their weights are compared.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Orientations in families of data}
The same reasoning applies to compact parameter families
of primary and geometric data whenever their asymptotic
intersections remain transverse. Locally choose
fixed-centre charts and stabilizations. A nearby regular
solution has small coordinates in such a chart. The
line orientation pulled back from
Proposition~\ref{prop:transport} varies continuously, by
Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting} for families with varying data,
while the canonical orientation of an invertible
operator is continuous on the invertible locus. Their
ratio is consequently locally constant. This fact is used in the
collapse and closed-cut comparisons; it is a local statement in the
parameter and uses only these charts.

\section{Triangles and orientations under collapse}\label{sec:collapse}

\subsection{Two-handle data and the triangle product}\label{subsec:two-handle-data}
Let $W_2\colon M_0\to M_1$ be a two-handle cobordism, attached along a
framed link with $n$ components, with the bundle trivial at both ends and
a filling frame as in Paper~I, Section~4.4. Following Paper~I,
Section~9.1, choose a Heegaard triple $(\Sigma;H_0,H_1,H_2)$ of genus $g$
subordinate to the link. Then $H_0\cup H_1$ and $H_0\cup H_2$ are
Heegaard splittings of $M_0$ and $M_1$, and $Y_{12}=H_1\cup H_2$ is a
connected sum of $r=g-n$ copies of $S^1\times S^2$. Let $C_i$ be the
torus compression body of $H_i$ (Section~\ref{sec:geometry}), so that
$h=g+1$, and write $Y_{ij}^\#=C_i\cup(-C_j)$; thus $Y_{01}^\#=M_0^\#$ and
$Y_{02}^\#=M_1^\#$. The three Lagrangians in $\Msf_h$ are
\[
 P_0,\qquad P_1=L(C_1),\qquad P_2=L_{\mathfrak h_2}(C_2),
\]
where $P_0$ is the image of $L_{\mathfrak h_0}(C_0)$ under the
symplectomorphism of $\Msf_h$ induced by the filling frame. The primary
perturbation of $C_1$ is zero. The Lagrangians $L(C_1)$ and $L(C_2)$ meet
cleanly in $Q\cong\SU(2)^r$, and $\mathfrak h_2$ is a small positive
multiple of the height function $\zeta_Q$ of Paper~I, Section~9.2, a Morse
function on $Q$ with a unique maximum $q_*$. The perturbation
$\mathfrak h_0$ is small, with $P_0$ transverse to $P_1$ and $P_2$ and
disjoint from $P_1\cap P_2$. The point $q_*$ is the top generator of
$\CF(P_1,P_2)$, and the corresponding critical point of $Y_{12}^\#$ is
again written $q_*$.

Write $N_{ij}$ for the mixed map of the pair $(P_i,P_j)$, from the gauge
complex of $Y_{ij}^\#$ to $C_{ij}=\CF(P_i,P_j)$. Let $F_r$ be the filling
of $Y_{12}^\#$ by the boundary sum of $r$ copies of $S^1\times D^3$ and one
copy of $T^2\times D^2$. Its relative invariant $\theta_G$ is the signed
count of index-zero solutions on $F_r$ with cylindrical end $Y_{12}^\#$,
projected to the framed complex; it is a cycle, equal to $\pm o_{q_*}$
(Paper~I, Proposition~9.4, and Lemma~\ref{lem:gauge-triangle-Z}). Put
$\theta_S=N_{12}\theta_G$. The \emph{torus-summed triangle} $X^\#$ is the
union of $\Delta\times\Sigma_h$ and $\Delta\times T^2$, for a triangle
$\Delta$ with edges $e_0,e_1,e_2$, with the pieces $e_i\times C_i$ attached
along $e_i\times(\Sigma_h\sqcup T^2)$. Its ends are $Y_{01}^\#$,
$Y_{12}^\#$ and $Y_{02}^\#$, and $W_2^\#=X^\#\cup_{Y_{12}^\#}F_r$
(Paper~I, Section~9.3). Its lifting group $\phi_X$ is the quotient of
$(\ZZ/2)^3$, with one coordinate for each compression piece, by the
diagonal; the class it restricts to on $Y_{ij}^\#$ is the sum of the
$i$-th and $j$-th coordinates, written $s_{ij}$. Let
$\mu_G\colon C_G(Y_{01}^\#)\otimes^{\mathrm r} C_G(Y_{12}^\#)\to C_G(Y_{02}^\#)$ be
the gauge map of $X^\#$ with lifting group $\phi_X$. The symplectic map of the
two-handle is
\[
 S_{W_2}=\mu_S(\,\cdot\otimes\theta_S),
\]
where $\mu_S$ is the triangle product of $(P_0,P_1,P_2)$ defined below,
composed on both sides with the frame transports of Paper~I,
Theorem~9.13, which we suppress from the notation.
Theorem~\ref{thm:two-handle} compares $S_{W_2}$ with the gauge map
of $W_2$.

Order three Lagrangians $L_0,L_1,L_2$ by the boundary
orientation of the triangle. Write $C_{ij}=\CF(L_i,L_j)$.
Use the end gradings induced by its chosen almost-complex gauge
triangle. Holomorphic triangles carry the limiting triangle orientation
of Definition~\ref{def:limit-orientation}: the mixed triangle at small
positive collapse scale is oriented by
Definition~\ref{def:mixed-orientation}, this orientation is pulled back
along collapse, and the lines of the corner pieces are then cancelled.
The \emph{corner pieces} of the zero-scale limit domain are the one-input
mixed pieces $\mathcal V_{01},\mathcal V_{12}$ attached at its incoming
ends along ordinary strip necks (Section~\ref{subsec:vertex-id}). Put all generator orientation lines before the determinant line of the
triangle.
The resulting product
\[
 \mu:C_{01}\otimes^{\mathrm r} C_{12}\longrightarrow C_{02}
\]
satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:leibniz}
 d\mu(a,b)=(-1)^{|b|}\mu(da,b)+\mu(a,db).
\end{equation}
Indeed the first strip break requires its translation
line to pass the second input generator; the second
break does not. This is the boundary calculation for
the one-dimensional triangle space.

If $\theta\in C_{12}$ is a homogeneous cycle, then
$R_\theta(a)=\mu(a,\theta)$ is a closed map of degree
$|\theta|$. The product itself is even in this convention.
For a change of reference almost-complex structure at any of the
three ends of the triangle,
the corresponding symplectic conversion is diagonal on
the same generator orientation lines. Applying the conversions with
\eqref{eq:tensor-maps} gives the product for the new reference structures.
If the three conversions have parities $t_{01},t_{12},t_{02}$,
the converted product has degree $m=t_{01}+t_{12}+t_{02}$ and satisfies
\[
d\mu(a,b)=(-1)^m\bigl((-1)^{|b|}\mu(da,b)+\mu(a,db)\bigr).
\]
Inserting a cycle $\theta$ then gives a closed map of degree
$m+|\theta|$. All subsequent changes of reference structure use this converted
formula, with the right tensor convention.

\subsection{Collapse preserves the transported orientations}
This subsection proves Theorem~\ref{thm:B}. The decisive step is
Proposition~\ref{prop:collapse-sign}, which rests on
Lemma~\ref{lem:square}: if a continuous family of Fredholm operators over
a square is invertible on three edges, then transport along the fourth
edge carries the canonical orientation to the canonical orientation.
Proposition~\ref{prop:affine-square} supplies such a square at a fixed
small collapse scale $\epsilon$, for a regular index-zero limiting
solution $V$. Its edge $s=0$ is constant, equal to the linearized
operator at the preglue $W_\epsilon(V)$, which is invertible, with
inverse bounded by a fixed power of $1+|\log\epsilon|$, by the attachment
inverse of~\ref{I8}. Its edge $t=0$ is the derivative of the contraction
equation of~\ref{I8} along the chart
segment from $W_\epsilon(V)$ to the solution $\mathcal G_\epsilon(V)$; it
is invertible because the inverse bound at the preglue dominates the
quadratic bound on the contraction ball. Its edge $s=1$ is the operator
at $\mathcal G_\epsilon(V)$ with its slice centre moved from
$W_\epsilon(V)$ to $\mathcal G_\epsilon(V)$; it is block triangular, with
the invertible linearization on the quotient by gauge directions and an
invertible scalar block. Its edge $t=1$ is the path of linearized mixed
operators along the segment, which carries the transported orientation.
Lemma~\ref{lem:square} therefore transports the canonical orientation at
the preglue to the canonical orientation at the solution, and
Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family} identifies the former with the canonical
orientation at $V$.

The bijection of solutions is Paper~I's; what is new here is the
comparison of determinant lines. The estimates below serve two purposes.
Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family} extends the linear analysis of Paper~I
from regular solutions to compact families of limiting configurations,
whose operators may have kernel and cokernel; this is needed because
orientations live on configuration spaces.
Proposition~\ref{prop:affine-square} constructs the square; it is needed
because the preglue is not a solution, so the block-triangular comparison
of Lemma~\ref{lem:realization} does not apply along the segment.

The comparison must be defined on compact families of limiting
configurations, where kernels and cokernels may occur. We first give
that construction. Stationary mixed-end coordinates are included
as finite-dimensional domain factors throughout.

\begin{lemma}[Collapse of stabilized families]\label{lem:collapse-family}
Let $\{V_b\}_{b\in B}$ be a compact smooth family of limiting
configurations with the specified autonomous end models and
separated stationary coordinates. Assume that their remainders
have a common exponential rate $\beta$ at every required derivative
order, and choose the comparison weights in common gaps strictly
below that rate. Assume that the collapse datum is unperturbed, as
in Paper~I: the primary perturbation vanishes on the inserted
compression body, and the dependence region and the output of every
other perturbation avoid a fixed neighbourhood of the collapse.
Primary data on the remaining pieces may vary in a compact
regular family, and the length of the collapse arc may vary in a compact
range. For sufficiently small
$\epsilon>0$, pregluing gives a continuous isomorphism of
orientation lines
\begin{equation}\label{eq:collapse-det}
 \det D_{V_b}\longrightarrow\det L_{W_\epsilon(V_b)}.
\end{equation}
It is independent, up to positive homotopy, of the stabilization
and cutoff choices, and it commutes with the lifting actions. It also
commutes with ordinary gauge and strip gluing at an original end, in
the following sense. There are constants $C$ and $T_0$, and a threshold
$\epsilon_0$ independent of the neck length $T\ge T_0$, such that the
square formed by~\eqref{eq:collapse-det} and the gluing isomorphisms of
Lemma~\ref{lem:line-gluing} commutes in the \emph{cofinal neck-and-scale
regime}
\[
 T\ge C\log(1+|\log\epsilon|)+T_0,\qquad 0<\epsilon<\epsilon_0 ,
\]
and hence, by continuity, for all $T\ge T_0$ and
$0<\epsilon<\epsilon_0$. At a regular index-zero solution both
operators are invertible and~\eqref{eq:collapse-det} carries the
canonical element to the canonical element.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\emph{Stabilization.} Choose a finite-dimensional real space $F$
and smooth compactly supported target columns
$j_b:F\to Y_b$ so that
$\widehat D_b=(D_{V_b},j_b):X_b\oplus F\to Y_b$ is onto.
Such columns exist by smoothness of the cokernel on the limit domain
(Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(a),(b), which includes the
zero-scale limit domains) and density of
compactly supported tests; a finite cover of $B$ and parameter
cutoffs give one $F$. Write $K_b=\ker\widehat D_b$. These kernels
form a vector bundle. Choose projections $\Gamma_b:X_b\oplus F\to K_b$
whose restriction to $K_b$ is the identity. They can be formed
from compactly supported pairings, together with the stationary
coordinate projections at original ends and the coordinate tests
on $F$: a nonzero
kernel element cannot vanish in all three sets of tests. The $F$
tests also detect redundant or inactive stabilization columns.
A finite selection works over each compact parameter patch. A family of
arc lengths in a compact range is identified with a family of metrics on
one domain by diffeomorphisms supported along the arc; the constants of
Paper~I, Sections~7.1--7.7, are uniform on such families.
Any free stationary coordinate at an artificial exterior end is
also detected by compact pairings on $K_b$, after enlarging the
compact set. Choose those pairings instead of an artificial-end
limit projection: the artificial end will disappear on attachment.
Then $(\widehat D_b,\Gamma_b)$ is an isomorphism, with uniformly
bounded inverse over $B$.

Transport the columns to the preglued domain, away from the
transition annuli, and denote them by $j_{\epsilon,b}$. Patch
kernel fields by the harmonic lift on the collapsed compression
and by the usual cutoffs elsewhere. Call this map $J_{\epsilon,b}$.
Transport the compact pairings in $\Gamma_b$ in the same manner,
retaining their finite-dimensional factors in the original order.
This defines
\[
 A_{\epsilon,b}=(L_{W_\epsilon(V_b)},j_{\epsilon,b},
                         \Gamma_{\epsilon,b}):
 X_{\epsilon,b}\oplus F\longrightarrow Y_{\epsilon,b}\oplus K_b.
\]

\emph{Four estimates.} Put $\mathsf A=1+|\log\epsilon|$, and fix a weight
$\delta_*<\min(\beta,\gamma/2)$, where $\gamma$ is the transition gap of
Paper~I, Proposition~7.9. The arguments of Paper~I, Sections~7.1--7.7,
which are written for a regular index-zero solution, give the following
four estimates for the stabilized family, with constants $C_B$, $N$ and
$\sigma>0$ independent of $\epsilon$ and $b$. They are proved in
Appendix~\ref{app:collapse-family}.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item (Decay.) The stationary columns of the auxiliary exterior operator
of Paper~I, Section~7.7, built from the truncation of $V_b$ at radius
$\epsilon^{1/4}$, differ from those of $D_{V_b}$ by
$O(\epsilon^{1/4})+O(\epsilon^{(\beta-\delta_*)/4})$, uniformly on $B$.
The common rate $\beta$ replaces the decay of solutions.
\item (Exterior inverse.) The auxiliary exterior operator, augmented by
$j_b$ and $\Gamma_b$, is invertible, with inverse bounded uniformly in
$\epsilon$ and $b$ at the end weight $\delta_*$, and by $C_B\mathsf A^N$
at the end weight $\delta_L\asymp\mathsf A^{-1}$ on the new ends. This
replaces Paper~I, Proposition~7.13.
\item (Attachment inverse.) $\|A_{\epsilon,b}^{-1}\|\le C_B\mathsf A^N$.
This extends the attachment inverse of~\ref{I8} to the stabilized
family.
\item (Kernel patching.) For $k\in K_b$,
$\|A_{\epsilon,b}J_{\epsilon,b}k-(0,k)\|\le C_B\epsilon^\sigma\mathsf A^N\|k\|$.
This carries the kernel estimate of Paper~I, Proposition~7.8, from the
model strip to the glued domain.
\end{enumerate}
By (iii) and (iv),
$\|A_{\epsilon,b}^{-1}(0,k)-J_{\epsilon,b}k\|\le C_B^2\epsilon^\sigma\mathsf A^{2N}\|k\|$,
which tends to zero. Hence $k\mapsto A_{\epsilon,b}^{-1}(0,k)$ identifies
$K_b$ with $\ker(L_{W_\epsilon(V_b)},j_{\epsilon,b})$ and is asymptotic to
the patched kernel map. Cancel $\det F$ in the two identities
$\det D=\det\ker(D,j)\otimes(\det F)^*$ to obtain
\eqref{eq:collapse-det}. Its independence of the stabilization and cutoff
choices, and its compatibility with the lifting actions and with gauge
and strip gluing, are proved in Appendix~\ref{app:collapse-family}.

At a regular index-zero solution the stabilization may be taken to
be zero. Then $K_b=0$, the realized operator is invertible, and the
estimate above makes the preglued operator invertible, so
\eqref{eq:collapse-det} carries the canonical element to the canonical
element; by stabilization independence the same holds for every
stabilization. This proves all the assertions.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Limiting orientation]\label{def:limit-orientation}
Let $V$ be a configuration on the zero-scale limit domain of the
triangle, with gauge inputs $a_{01},a_{12}$ and output $x_{02}$. For
$0<\epsilon<\epsilon_0$, with $\epsilon_0$ as in
Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family} for a compact family containing $V$, the
\emph{limiting orientation} of $V$ is the map
\[
 L_G(a_{01})\otimes L_G(a_{12})\otimes\det D_V\longrightarrow L_S(x_{02})
\]
obtained from the orientation of Definition~\ref{def:mixed-orientation}
at the preglue $W_\epsilon(V)$ through~\eqref{eq:collapse-det}. Let
$\mathcal V_{01}$, $\mathcal V_{12}$ be the corner pieces of the zero-scale
domain (Section~\ref{subsec:two-handle-data}).
For a configuration $u$ on the triangle domain with inputs
$x_{01},x_{12}$ and output $x_{02}$, let $V=C_{x_{01}}\#C_{x_{12}}\#u$ be
obtained by gluing the constant pairs on the corner pieces to the
inputs of $u$. The \emph{limiting triangle orientation} of $u$ is the map
\[
 L_S(x_{01})\otimes L_S(x_{12})\otimes\det D_u\longrightarrow L_S(x_{02})
\]
obtained from the limiting orientation of $V$ by cancelling, in cyclic
order, the canonically oriented lines of the two invertible constant
pairs, with $L_G(a_{x_{ij}})=L_S(x_{ij})$. The folded triangle is treated
in the same way, with the pieces of the pairs $(A_0,L_C)$ and
$(L_C,A_1)$.
\end{definition}

The orientation of Definition~\ref{def:mixed-orientation} at scale
$\epsilon$ is formed after moving the metric, with the configuration
fixed, along stages (A) and (B) of the path of Paper~I, Lemma~9.12 (for
the folded triangle, the corresponding stages in the proof of Paper~I,
Theorem~11.8), to a metric with a product collar at the cut. The
configuration spaces do not depend on the metric
(Appendix~\ref{app:shift-reduction}), so this is transport along a path
of data, continuous in $\epsilon$. By part~(V1) of
Lemma~\ref{lem:vertex-id} below, the strip actions of the corner
pieces are those of $C_{01}$ and $C_{12}$. A limiting triangle need not
admit a shift to a constant triangle, because the triple intersection of
its boundary labels can be empty; the orientation is therefore defined by
collapse rather than by shifting.

The limiting orientations are independent of $\epsilon$ and of the
auxiliary choices, and they are coherent at breaks, by the following
arguments.

Continuity for $0<\epsilon<\epsilon_0$ on a compact configuration
family compares sufficiently small scales. On overlaps choose a
common smaller scale. Stabilization independence and ordinary
gluing compare the auxiliary columns, corner pieces and necks.
For a symplectic strip at an incoming end, apply the one-input
comparison $C_x\#u\sim A\#C_y$ in its corner piece, away from
the central collapse neighbourhood, and absorb $A$ into the ordinary
cobordism factor. Thus the full end-gluing diagram commutes before
the lines of the corner pieces are cancelled.

The same comparison applies with nonconstant configurations on the
corner pieces. If $c_{01},c_{12}$ are such configurations, for instance
solutions or the preglued configurations $C_x\#w$ used below, shift each to
$A_{ij}\#C^{c'_{ij}}$ inside its domain defining the mixed map
(Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting}(a); Appendix~\ref{app:shifting} covers
the domains defining the mixed maps). This is a compact family on a fixed limiting
domain, of the kind allowed in Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family}. Its
compatibility with gauge gluing at the original inputs, together with
the coherence of the positive-scale orientation under input gauge
gluing, absorbs $A_{ij}$. Hence at $c_{01}\#c_{12}\#u$ the pulled-back
orientation is $\Gamma_{c_{01}}\otimes\Gamma_{c_{12}}\otimes o(u)$, in
cyclic order. At an incoming break of the one-dimensional triangle space
the internal neck grows with the breaking parameter. Regroup
$C_x\#_T(w\#_{T'}u')$ as $(C_x\#w)\#_Tu'$ on a domain with a longer
internal neck. This neck runs along the collapsing arc: the output
strip of a corner piece is bounded by $P_0$ and $P_1$, and in the
limit domain the only $P_1$ boundary is the collapse arc (Paper~I, proof
of Theorem~9.10). Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family} allows compact families
of lengths of this arc, so the pulled-back orientation does not depend on
the neck length over compact ranges. This proves coherence of the
defined triangle orientation without an independent component sign.

For a nonsolution reference family supplied only in a strong
weighted topology, use a comparison weight below its remainder rate.
Solutions are used with their exponential decay, without truncation.

Now let $V$ be a regular index-zero limiting solution. Its realized
operator and the operator at its preglue are invertible. Let
$\mathcal G_\epsilon(V)$ be the nearby solution obtained from the preglue
by the contraction mapping argument of~\ref{I8}. It remains to compare
the operator at the preglue with the operator at $\mathcal G_\epsilon(V)$.
The chart used for the contraction estimate need not be the chart used
to define the determinant bundle, and the second derivative of a fixed
mixed chart need not be bounded uniformly in the collapse norm. The
comparison is therefore made around a square of operators.

\begin{lemma}[Transport around a square]\label{lem:square}
Let $D_{s,t}$ be a continuous family of Fredholm operators
on a square. Suppose the operators on three edges are
invertible. Parallel transport of orientations on the
fourth edge carries the canonical orientation at its
first endpoint to the canonical orientation at its
second endpoint.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The orientation double cover of the determinant bundle
is trivial over the square. Transport around its
boundary is therefore the identity. On each of the
three invertible edges the canonical orientation is a
continuous section, so transport is positive. The
remaining edge must have the same property at its
endpoints.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Affine centres at positive collapse scale]
\label{prop:affine-square}
Fix the collapse datum of Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family} and a
sufficiently small positive scale $\epsilon$. Let
$c_s=\Phi(sz_*)=(A_s,u_s)$, $0\leq s\leq1$, be the segment in the exact
matching chart from the preglue $c_0=W_\epsilon(V)$ to the solution
$c_1=\mathcal G_\epsilon(V)$ obtained by the contraction mapping argument
of~\ref{I8}. At a common sufficiently small weight on the original
ends there is a continuous Fredholm family $D_{s,t}$, $0\leq s,t\leq1$,
whose $t=0$ edge is the derivative of the contraction equation and whose
$t=1$ edge is the linearized mixed operator at $c_s$ with slice centre
$A_s$. It retains the finite stationary coordinates, both operator rows,
and their weak-adjoint domains. If $V$ is regular of index zero, the
other three edges are invertible.

The Fredholm construction also holds in compact smooth families of these
small matching-chart neighbourhoods, including nonsolutions, with bounds
depending on the positive scale.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The proof has three steps. Step~1 constructs the family $D_{s,t}$.
Step~2 proves that it is a continuous family of Fredholm operators.
Step~3 proves that the edges $t=0$, $s=0$ and $s=1$ are invertible when
$V$ is regular of index zero.

\emph{Step 1: construction of $D_{s,t}$.} Write $g=g_\epsilon$, $\lambda=\lambda_\epsilon$ and
$\widehat g=\lambda^{-2}g$ for the metrics and rescaling in
Paper~I, Sections~7--8. Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:affine-centers}
 C_{s,t}=(1-t)A_0+tA_s.
\end{equation}
The centre need not solve the primary equation at a mixed end.
The exact chart fixes the normal connection trace, so the
covariant Neumann condition is common to all these centres.
A single normal gauge for $A_0$, applied to the whole family,
makes this trace zero without changing the affine relation.
For each fixed $\epsilon$, the changed compression region is
compact and its minimum scale is positive. Paper~I's strong
norms are then equivalent to ordinary weighted Sobolev norms,
with the stationary coordinates adjoined; these equivalence
constants need not be uniform as $\epsilon\to0$.

At an original mixed end $e$, choose the primary representatives
$B_e(p)$ in the spatial Neumann Coulomb slice of $B_e(0)$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:affine-end-slice}
 d_{B_e(0)}^*(B_e(p)-B_e(0))=0,
 \qquad (B_e(p)-B_e(0))(n)=0.
\end{equation}
The spatial scalar inverse and implicit function theorem give
this local section of the family of primary solutions. Extend it into
the matching chart after subtracting its stationary value. This
ensures that the affine scalar equation has weighted target.
If an initial local primary section is used instead, its small
spatial gauge gives this normalization; extend that gauge with
a fixed longitudinal cutoff. The correction is quadratic in the
stationary parameters when the initial tangent is Coulomb. It
occurs on the fixed original-end geometry and preserves the
initial collapse operator and the quadratic estimates of Paper~I.
The chart segment is smooth in all stationary-subtracted norms.

We first compare scalar operators without assuming compact
support of $C_{s,t}-A_s$. With
$d\mu=\lambda^2dV_{\widehat g}=\lambda^{-2}dV_g$, the operator
$P_{C,A}=\lambda^2d_C^{*,g}d_A$ has weak form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:affine-scalar-form}
 \langle\xi,P_{C,A}\eta\rangle_\mu
 =\int\langle d_C\xi,d_A\eta\rangle_{\widehat g}\,d\mu.
\end{equation}
The two factors on the right are generally different. Let $C_P$
be the Poincar\'e constant for $A$, and let $a$ bound the bracket
with $C-A$ in the rescaled metric. Then
\[
 \operatorname{Re}\langle\xi,P_{C,A}\xi\rangle_\mu
 \geq(1-aC_P)\|d_A\xi\|_\mu^2.
\]
If $aC_P<1$, the bounded coercive form gives a weak inverse.
Conjugating by an original-end weight $w=e^\omega$ adds form
terms controlled by $\|d\omega\|_\infty$ and its square. For a
common sufficiently small weight they are absorbed. Neumann
elliptic regularity gives the higher Sobolev inverses at this
fixed scale. The identical spatial argument makes $d_C^*d_B$
invertible when $B$ is the perturbed-flat limit of $c_s$ at a mixed end
and $C$ is sufficiently close to it, whether or not $C$ is perturbed
flat.

We next specify all stationary columns. Let $\varphi_{s,0,e}(p)$ be
the connection value of the contraction-chart column at the
limit $B_e(s)$ of $c_s$ at the end $e$, and let $C=C_{s,t,e}$ be the corresponding
limit of the centre. Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:affine-columns}
 \psi_{s,t,e}(p)=(d_C^*d_{B_e(s)})^{-1}d_C^*\varphi_{s,0,e}(p),
 \qquad \varphi_{s,t,e}=\varphi_{s,0,e}-d_{B_e(s)}\psi_{s,t,e}.
\end{equation}
The new value is $C$-Coulomb and has the same primary boundary
class. At $t=0$ the correction vanishes by
\eqref{eq:affine-end-slice}. Extend $\psi$ by a fixed end cutoff
to $\Xi_{s,t}(p)$ and put
\[
 H_{s,t}(p)=H_{s,0}(p)-G_s\Xi_{s,t}(p),
 \qquad G_s\xi=(d_{A_s}\xi,0),\qquad
 J_{s,t}(w,p)=J_s^{\rm dec}w+H_{s,t}(p).
\]
Here $J_s^{\rm dec}$ is the decaying derivative of the exact
chart. Reading the primary classes at infinity and subtracting
$H_{s,t}$ proves that $J_{s,t}$ is a source isomorphism. It is
the derivative of a matching chart: solve the nonlinear
spatial $C$-Coulomb equation based at $B_e(s)$ and extend its
small gauge with the same cutoff. This leaves the configuration
$c_s$ fixed and differentiates to the displayed formula.
The scalar stationary image vanishes, and all remaining scalar
columns belong to the weighted target. Equivariance gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:affine-residual-gauge}
 \nabla\mathcal F_{c_s}G_s\Xi
       =\rho_*(\Xi)\mathcal F(c_s).
\end{equation}
This weighted equation column is retained; it need not vanish
at a nonsolution.

There is also a target derivative. On the section factor choose
$S_c$ by pointwise parallel transport from $u(z)$ to $u_0(z)$
along the short geodesic in the target. The gauge target is
fixed. Shrinking the chart gives uniform normal neighbourhoods;
$S_{c_0}=1$ and $\nabla S|_{c_0}=0$. Pointwise multiplication
estimates preserve Paper~I's quadratic bound and initial
operator. Differentiating gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:affine-target-residual}
 d(S\mathcal F)_c[v]=S_c\nabla_v\mathcal F+R_cv,
 \qquad R_cv=(\nabla_v S)_c\mathcal F(c).
\end{equation}
The coefficient of $R_c$ tends to zero on the original ends,
where the residual tends to zero. It is compact from the
weighted decaying $H^1$ domain to $L^2$, by compact truncation
and Rellich, and its stationary columns are weighted finite
rank. Since $\nabla S|_{c_0}=0$, the formula
\eqref{eq:affine-target-residual} gives $R_{c_0}=0$ even though the
preglue is not a solution; also $R_{c_1}=0$ since $c_1$ is a solution. The target
map itself may have a nonidentity stationary limit.

The required square is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:full-affine-square}
 D_{s,t}=\left(S_{c_s}\nabla\mathcal F_{c_s}+(1-t)R_{c_s},
       \lambda^2d_{C_{s,t}}^{*,g}\pi_A\right)J_{s,t}.
\end{equation}
At $t=1$ the residual term is absent and the centre is $A_s$,
so this edge is the path of linearized mixed operators at
$c_s$.

\emph{Step 2: Fredholm property and continuity.} The family
\eqref{eq:full-affine-square} is a continuous family of Fredholm
operators on the whole square. On the changed compact core, which is
smooth at this fixed scale, this follows from the local estimates
of~\cite{DFL,DFL-mixed}. On each mixed end the operator differs from the
translation-invariant end operator at the stationary limit by a
zeroth-order term bounded by a constant times the $C^0$ distance between
the centre and that limit; when this distance is small, a Neumann series
gives the end inverse and the inverse of its adjoint. The estimates are
given in Appendix~\ref{app:affine-fredholm}. The smallness conditions used in Steps~1 and~2 follow from the
contraction bounds of~\ref{I8}.
On the unchanged original ends the spatial gaps and cylinder
constants are fixed. The stationary correction is bounded by
$C(1+|\log\epsilon|)^{m+1}e_\epsilon\to0$, with $e_\epsilon$
the pregluing error of~\ref{I8}. The strong-control estimate of
Paper~I, (8.1), bounds the variation $sz_*$ on unit patches of the
rescaled metric by its strong norm, so Sobolev embedding gives
$\|A_s-A_0\|_{C^0(\widehat g)}\to0$. The temporal component
in this norm is the rescaled component, with no additional
$\epsilon^{-1}$ factor. The preglue's uniform scalar Poincar\'e
estimate therefore persists, and both scalar and end
smallness conditions hold for sufficiently small $\epsilon$.

\emph{Step 3: the three invertible edges.} The $t=0$ edge of \eqref{eq:full-affine-square} is exactly the derivative of the
contraction equation, including the affine scalar row and
\eqref{eq:affine-target-residual}. By~\ref{I8} the operator at the preglue is invertible in the strong
norms, with inverse bounded by a fixed power of $1+|\log\epsilon|$. The
derivative of the nonlinear part of the contraction equation along the
segment is bounded by a constant times $\|z_*\|$, which is at most a
fixed power of $1+|\log\epsilon|$ times the pregluing error, a positive
power of $\epsilon$. Hence this entire edge is invertible. Strong invertibility
implies first-order invertibility: kernels regularize into the
strong domain, and a weak cokernel would annihilate every smooth
target reached by the strong inverse. The first-order operator has closed
range, being Fredholm by Step~2, and the smooth targets reached by the
strong inverse are dense in its target, so such a cokernel element
vanishes. At $s=0$, the centres and source maps
are constant and $R=0$, giving the invertible preglued operator
used in \eqref{eq:collapse-det}.

At $s=1$, equivariance gives $\nabla\mathcal F_{c_1}G_1=0$.
The scalar inverse splits the source into horizontal vectors
and decaying gauge directions. The resulting blocks are the
intrinsic quotient linearization and $P_{C_{1,t},A_1}$. Changing
\eqref{eq:affine-columns} changes only the representative of
that quotient tangent; \eqref{eq:affine-residual-gauge} now
vanishes. Regularity and index zero make the quotient block
invertible, and scalar coercivity makes the second block
invertible. This proves invertibility of the third edge.

Compact parameter families use finite local covers; the transition
gauge transformations transport both rows and all stationary columns.
Ordinary gluing at fixed positive scale commutes with these
identifications by the bordered determinant construction, with
corrections placed on the mixed factors and fixed ordinary end models.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:collapse-sign}
The map~\eqref{eq:collapse-det}, followed by transport
from $W_\epsilon(V)$ to $\mathcal G_\epsilon(V)$, preserves
the sign of the zero-dimensional solution.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Use the family \eqref{eq:full-affine-square} of
Proposition~\ref{prop:affine-square}. Its contraction edge is
invertible by the nonlinear estimate, its preglue edge is the
initial invertible collapse operator, and its solution edge is
invertible by the quotient and scalar block decomposition.
Lemma~\ref{lem:square} identifies the canonical orientations
along the remaining edge, the path of linearized mixed operators at
$c_s$. Composing this
identification with \eqref{eq:collapse-det} proves the claim.

That path, the edge $t=1$, is an extended realization, not an
permitted one in the sense of Definition~\ref{def:realization}: its
target map $S_{c_s}$ may have a nonidentity stationary limit, and its
source is the chart at $c_0$. It differs from a permitted realization
$L_{c_s}$ along the same configurations by bounded source and target
isomorphisms depending continuously on $s$. At $s=0$ these are the
identity, since $S_{c_0}=1$ and the chart is centred at $c_0$. The
naturality maps of determinant lines under these isomorphisms commute
with transport, and at $s=1$ both operators are invertible, so the
naturality map carries the canonical element to the canonical element.
Hence the identification along the edge $t=1$ is the one in the
determinant bundle of the permitted realizations along $c_s$, which carries the
transported orientation.
\end{proof}

If the conformal metric in the collapse estimate is
changed within the unperturbed collar class, the zero
set of the mixed equation is unchanged. At a solution
Lemma~\ref{lem:realization} compares the gauge rows by
invertible paths, so the sign is unchanged. This permits
use of the metric of Paper~I without introducing a new
orientation convention.

The construction is compatible with compact parameter
families because its stabilizations and operator
bounds may be chosen locally uniformly. Associativity
of ordinary gluing identifies the determinant maps at
broken ends. This proves Theorem~\ref{thm:B}. Together with
Lemmas~\ref{lem:vertex-id} and~\ref{lem:gauge-triangle-Z} below, it gives
the two-handle square, Theorem~\ref{thm:two-handle}.

\subsection{Mixed maps of the degeneration pieces and the two-handle map}\label{subsec:vertex-id}
The degenerations of the mixed triangle contain one-input mixed pieces
which are not the special quintuples on which the mixed maps are
defined. At the collapsed end these are the two DFL pieces
$\mathcal V_{01},\mathcal V_{12}$ of the zero-scale domain, cut off along
ordinary strip ends; they are the corner pieces of
Section~\ref{subsec:two-handle-data}. Each carries an original mixed
end and a new mixed end of Paper~I, Proposition~6.9. At the end with the
long cut collar the piece is the DFL piece $\mathcal V_{02}$ of the pair
$(P_0,P_2)$ beyond $Y_{02}^\#$. The folded triangle of
Lemma~\ref{lem:folded-triangle} has the corresponding pieces for the
pairs $(A_0,L_C)$, $(L_C,A_1)$ and $(A_0,A_1)$. Each such piece
$\mathcal V$ is a domain of DFL type in the class of~\ref{I1} (Paper~I,
the paragraph following Lemma~9.7, and the proof of Theorem~11.8). By~\ref{I1}
its count is chain homotopic over $\FF$ to the mixed map of the pair;
Lemma~\ref{lem:vertex-id} gives the integral statement.

Orient the configurations on $\mathcal V$ by the map $\Gamma_{\mathcal V}$
of Proposition~\ref{prop:transport}; Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting}(a)
applies to $\mathcal V$ by Appendix~\ref{app:shift-reduction}. Let
$\mathcal V^{\rm st}$ be the special quintuple of the same pair, carrying
the data of its mixed map $N$. Suppose that $\mathcal V$ has the same
ordinary end data, including the almost-complex structure on the gauge
end collar; otherwise compose with the continuations of
Proposition~\ref{prop:end-data} and the conversions of
Section~\ref{sec:gauge}. Let $N_{\mathcal V}$ be the count of regular
index-zero solutions on $\mathcal V$, signed by $\Gamma_{\mathcal V}$. For
a regular strip $u$ from $x$ to $y$ at the output, the action
$R_{\mathcal V}(u):L_S(x)\otimes\det D_u\to L_S(y)$ is defined as in
Section~\ref{subsec:torus-maps}: glue $u$ to the constant pair $C_x$ on
$\mathcal V$ and evaluate $\Gamma_{\mathcal V}$ on the positive element of
the invertible constant-pair operator.

\begin{lemma}[Integral identification of the degeneration pieces]\label{lem:vertex-id}
Let $\mathcal V$ be a domain of DFL type in the class of~\ref{I1}, for a
transverse pair whose mixed map $N$ is defined on the special quintuple
$\mathcal V^{\rm st}$ of the pair, and joined to $\mathcal V^{\rm st}$ by the
path of domains and data in the proof of~\ref{I1}. Suppose that
$\mathcal V$ and $\mathcal V^{\rm st}$ have the same ordinary end data,
including the almost-complex structure on the gauge end collar. Let
$N_{\mathcal V}$ be the count of regular index-zero solutions on
$\mathcal V$ signed by the orientation map $\Gamma_{\mathcal V}$ of
Proposition~\ref{prop:transport}, and let $R_{\mathcal V}(u)$ be the strip
action defined above. The degeneration pieces of this section and the
closed-cut piece $M_{\lambda_0}$ of Proposition~\ref{prop:closed-neck} are
domains of this kind.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(V\arabic*)]
\item $R_{\mathcal V}(u)=R_{\mathcal V^{\rm st}}(u)$ for every regular
strip $u$ at the output. Thus the strip orientations transported through
$\mathcal V$ are those of the target complex of $N$.
\item $N_{\mathcal V}$ is closed and even, and
$N_{\mathcal V}-N=\delta H_{\mathcal V}$ over $\ZZ$. Here
$H_{\mathcal V}$ is the signed count of the isolated parametrized
solutions of vertical index $-1$ along the path of domains and data from
$\mathcal V^{\rm st}$ to $\mathcal V$ used in the proof of~\ref{I1}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The proof of Paper~I, Lemma~9.7, joins $\mathcal V^{\rm st}$ to
$\mathcal V$ by a path $\mathcal V_b$, $b\in[0,1]$, of domains and data
with fixed end types and fixed ordinary end data, which stays in the
class of that lemma. It consists of compactly supported changes of the
conformal structure of the section region, of the seam placement, of the
metrics near the mixed ends and of the neck lengths, followed by a change
of the translation-invariant data on the mixed ends. The configuration
spaces along the path are identified by the decorated diffeomorphisms of
Appendix~\ref{app:shift-reduction}, and the data vary as permitted in
Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}. Hence $\Gamma_{\mathcal V_b}$ varies
continuously with $b$, by Propositions~\ref{prop:shifting}
and~\ref{prop:transport}, as for the parametric orientations of
Section~\ref{sec:vertex}. With no secondary or inhomogeneous data the
constant pair $C_x$ is a solution on every $\mathcal V_b$, with
invertible linearized operator (Lemma~\ref{lem:constant-pairs}); by
openness, uniformly on the compact path, the operator remains invertible
for the small data used here. Glue $u$ at the output with one long neck
along the whole path (Lemma~\ref{lem:line-gluing}). Then
$R_{\mathcal V_b}(u)$ is a continuous family of isomorphisms between the
fixed lines $L_S(x)\otimes\det D_u$ and $L_S(y)$, hence constant. This
proves (V1); it is the argument of Corollary~\ref{cor:strip-actions}.

For (V2), choose small invariant secondary perturbations along the path,
supported in the cores and regular as a parametrized family through
index one (Paper~I, Theorems~5.4 and~5.6(iii)). At $b=0$ they are the
regular data of $N$. At $b=1$ they are the data of $\mathcal V$, after an
arbitrarily small compactly supported change which makes index one
regular; by compactness and local uniqueness this change leaves the
index-zero count $N_{\mathcal V}$ unchanged, as in the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:end-data}. On the compactly
supported part of the path the parameter column is compactly supported;
on the change of mixed-end data it is the weighted column of Paper~I,
Lemma~10.15, whose hypothesis of uniform energy decay is verified in the
proof of Paper~I, Lemma~9.7. This is the mixed-end family of
Section~\ref{subsec:torus-families}, now on $\mathcal V$, with
$\Omega=\Gamma_{\mathcal V_b}$ and $f=0$. The proof of
Lemma~\ref{lem:family-signs} uses only three properties of $\Omega$: its
compatibility with gauge paths at the input, its compatibility with
strips at the output, and the constancy of the output differential along
the family. For $\Gamma_{\mathcal V_b}$ the first two are
Proposition~\ref{prop:transport} and the third is (V1). That proof
therefore gives $N_{\mathcal V}-N_{\mathcal V^{\rm st}}=\delta H_{\mathcal V}$,
and its last assertion gives closedness at both ends. By construction
$N_{\mathcal V^{\rm st}}=N$, and all these counts are even because their
solutions have index zero. This is the construction which produces $H_M$
in Proposition~\ref{prop:closed-neck}.
\end{proof}

The piece counts are replaced by the mixed maps through the following
identity. If $F,F',G,G'$ are closed even maps and $F'-F=\delta H$,
$G'-G=\delta K$ with $H,K$ odd, then
\[
 F'\otimes^{\mathrm r}G'-F\otimes^{\mathrm r}G=\delta\bigl(H\otimes^{\mathrm r}G'+F\otimes^{\mathrm r}K\bigr),
\]
since \eqref{eq:tensor} and \eqref{eq:tensor-maps} give
$\delta(H\otimes^{\mathrm r}G')=\delta H\otimes^{\mathrm r}G'$ and
$\delta(F\otimes^{\mathrm r}K)=F\otimes^{\mathrm r}\delta K$. Composition with closed maps
preserves such identities by~\eqref{eq:compose-homotopy}.

Consider the cycles $\theta_G$ and $\theta_S=N_{12}\theta_G$ of
Section~\ref{subsec:two-handle-data}. The unit diagonal of $N_{12}$ and the
filtration of $C_{12}$ by the Morse index of $\zeta_Q$ identify $\theta_S$
with the top generator $o_{q_*}$, up to the chosen sign. The mixed triangle
family has one endpoint equal to the gauge triangle followed by the
count $N_{\mathcal V_{02}}$ on the DFL piece beyond the cut, and the other
endpoint is the symplectic triangle with the two counts
$N_{\mathcal V_{01}}$, $N_{\mathcal V_{12}}$ of the zero-scale domain as
inputs. Proposition~\ref{prop:collapse-sign} compares the
counts at the collapse endpoint with the transported orientations,
and the two endpoints of the family are related by its oriented
boundary~\eqref{eq:family-boundary}. By part~(V1) of Lemma~\ref{lem:vertex-id}, the input strip orientations entering the limiting triangle orientation are those of $C_{01}$ and $C_{12}$, and the output strip action at the long-collar end is that of $C_{02}$. Along the family the output strip action is defined through the terminal constant pairs on the truncated output regions, which are invertible (Lemma~\ref{lem:constant-pairs}); it is therefore constant, by the argument of Corollary~\ref{cor:strip-actions}, and the output differential is that of $C_{02}$ throughout. The broken
boundary terms give a homotopy
$N_{\mathcal V_{02}}\mu_G\simeq\pm\mu_S(N_{\mathcal V_{01}}\otimes^{\mathrm r}
N_{\mathcal V_{12}})$. Since $\mu_G$ and $\mu_S$ are closed and even,
part~(V2) of Lemma~\ref{lem:vertex-id}, the identity for tensors of homotopies above
and~\eqref{eq:compose-homotopy} replace the three piece counts by
$N_{01}$, $N_{12}$ and $N_{02}$, hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:triangle-square}
 N_{02}\mu_G\simeq
 \pm\mu_S(N_{01}\otimes^{\mathrm r} N_{12}).
\end{equation}
The gauge two-handle map is itself a triangle map over the integers.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:gauge-triangle-Z}
With the almost-complex orientations, the ordering of
Lemma~\ref{lem:tensor-geometric} and the conversions inserted as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:gauge-functor},
\[
 \IG(W_2)\simeq\pm\mu_G(\,\cdot\otimes\theta_G),
\]
where $\mu_G$ is the framed gauge triangle map of the torus-summed
triangle (Paper~I, Sections~9.3 and~9.4) and $\theta_G=\pm o_{q_*}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Paper~I, Proposition~9.5, proves the identity modulo two, and its
argument is integral once three points are checked. First,
$W_2^\#=X^\#\circ W'$ is cut along $Y_{01}^\#\sqcup Y_{12}^\#$, and the
connecting image in \cite[Proposition~5.4]{KM-unknot} vanishes by the
$H^0$ computation in the proof of Paper~I, Proposition~9.5. That proposition and the K\"unneth isomorphism
of \cite[Section~5.2]{KM-unknot} hold over $\ZZ$; the latter is written
in the left convention, and its transfer to the ordering of
Lemma~\ref{lem:tensor-geometric} is trivial on $a\otimes\widetilde\theta_G$,
because the invariant $\widetilde\theta_G$ of the filling is even in its
induced grading. Second, an element of
the lifting group with $s_{12}=1$ is realized by an automorphism
supported on the torus region. The almost-complex orientations are
invariant under all bundle automorphisms (Section~\ref{sec:gauge}), so
it maps solutions to solutions preserving their signs, and the orbit
count of Paper~I gives $\mu^{\{s_{12}=0\}}(a\otimes b)=\mu^{\phi_X}(a\otimes\pi b)$ over $\ZZ$, with no factor two. The lifting-class sign of the conversion is trivial on these classes by Proposition~\ref{prop:character-gauge}, because
$J_W$ equals the fixed germ on the torus region. Third, the
