gluing fibre (Lemma~\ref{lem:gluing-multiplicity}). The stretched cut
decomposes the domain into the triangle cobordism of $D_0$, $C$, $D_1$,
glued at $Z$ to the piece $\mathcal V_Z$. Lemma~\ref{lem:G-properties} is
stated for $W^J_\lambda$, but its proof uses only a decomposition at a
closed cut, the localized shift, which
Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting}(b) supplies on folded triangles, and
Convention~\ref{conv:interior-line}. The same proof gives parts (i)--(iii)
for this decomposition, and part (iii) identifies the orientation with that
of the gauge cobordism followed by the conversion and the mixed map; this
gives $N_Z\Theta^{\mathrm r}\mu_{\mathscr W}$. Parts (i) and (ii) give the
compatibility at the input and output breaks. The family between the two
ends is a compact one-parameter family with compactly supported parameter
derivative. Its boundary strata are the two endpoints and ordinary breaks
at the two gauge inputs, ordered by Lemma~\ref{lem:tensor-geometric} as in
the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:family-signs}, and at the output strip; the
collapse-scale stage is a family of the same kind. Lemma~\ref{lem:family-signs},
with $f=t$, turns its compactified index-zero space into the stated
homotopy. The overall sign
reflects the fixed orientation choices at the two ends.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Cup formula]\label{prop:cup-formula}
Let $p$ be lower data and $q=C\circ p$ the upper height presentation at
a height $t$, as in Definition~\ref{def:fold-pair}(b), and let
$W_k:M\to M^+$ be the cobordism of the $k$ one-handles. With $n_p$ as
in~\eqref{eq:n-p},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cup-formula}
 \check U_C\simeq\pm N_q\IG(W_k)n_p.
\end{equation}
In particular, for a small fold pair
$(p,r)$ (Definition~\ref{def:fold-pair}),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cup-zero}
 B(p,r)\simeq\pm N_r\cont_G(p,r)n_p.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:folded-triangle} to
the two distinguished input cycles
$q_i^*=\pm N_i\theta_i$. Since the gauge
filling $B_k$ is the triangle cobordism
glued to the two auxiliary fillings,
$\psi_{B_k}$ is homologous to
$\pm\mu_{\mathscr W}(\theta_0\otimes\theta_1)$
by gauge composition. Hence the
folded triangle output is, up to a
boundary and one sign,
$N_Z\Theta^{\mathrm r}\psi_{B_k}$ with the
conversion of Lemma~\ref{lem:folded-triangle},
hence $N_Z\Theta^{\mathrm r}\Theta'\psi_{B_k}$
with the conversion of \eqref{eq:excision-cycle}.
Apply \eqref{eq:excision-cycle} to the
homogeneous cycle $\Theta'\psi_{B_k}$. With the
convention for $\mathsf E\psi_{B_k}$ stated
above, the result is
\[
 [z_C]=\pm[(N_{\bar p}\otimes^{\mathrm r} N_q)
                          \mathsf E\psi_{B_k}].
\]
Equation~\eqref{eq:handle-state} identifies
this with the tensor of $N_q\IG(W_k)n_p$.
The identification follows directly from
\eqref{eq:beta} and the graded double-dual
map in~\eqref{eq:n-p}. Since the difference
of the tensors is a boundary, their images
under $\operatorname{bend}_{\CF(\bar p)}$ differ by $\delta K$.
This proves~\eqref{eq:cup-formula}. The same
calculation with $k=0$ gives
\eqref{eq:cup-zero}.
\end{proof}

This proves a formula for the cup tensor
without selecting the reflection pairing
needed for the source of Paper~I's map.
The next sections construct that pairing
and show that it gives the ordinary
symplectic continuation when $k=0$.

\section{Sphere characters under small holonomy perturbations}
\label{sec:characters}

The sphere character of a coherent strip orientation system is the
sign by which the orientation changes when a sphere in $\Msf_h$ is
attached to a strip (Definition~\ref{def:sphere-character}). It
decides whether coherent continuation orientations with prescribed
ends exist (Lemma~\ref{lem:rigidity}). Theorem~\ref{thm:character-constancy}
shows that it is constant on the ball $\mathfrak B$ of primary
perturbations at an object with nonzero integral gauge homology.
Nonvanishing is used only to find an intersection point that
persists through each birth--death singularity of a generic path
(Lemma~\ref{lem:persistent}); the character is read off at that
point.

\subsection{The character of an orientation system}
For a transverse pair $P=(P_0,P_1)$ let
$\mathcal P(P_0,P_1)$ be the path space of paths in
$\Msf_h$ starting on $P_0$ and ending on $P_1$.
For $h\ge2$, the compression-body Lagrangians are
simply connected and have vanishing second homotopy
group (Paper~I, Section~3.2). The relative homotopy classes of strip
configurations with fixed ends are consequently a
torsor for $\pi_2(\Msf_h)\cong\ZZ$ in the lift
convention of~\cite[Section~2]{DFL}. Fix its positive
generator $S_h$; attaching it changes the index by
four. For $h=1$ the moduli space is a point and the
sphere-character assertions below are empty.

\begin{definition}\label{def:sphere-character}
Let $p$ be a presentation whose Lagrangians $P=(P_0,P_1)$ intersect transversely (its regularizing data need not be regular), and
let $\Gamma^p$ be the transported coherent orientation system on its
strip configurations (Section~\ref{sec:vertex}). The determinant line
of a sphere operator, together with the inverse determinant line of
the tangent space of $\Msf_h$ at its node, carries its complex
orientation. Attach a sphere to a strip configuration at an interior
node. The orientation given by $\Gamma^p$ on the glued configuration
differs from the node-glued orientation, formed from $\Gamma^p$ and
this complex orientation, by a sign. The sign depends only on the
class of the sphere, and it defines the \emph{sphere character}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sphere-character}
 \chi^p:\pi_2(\Msf_h)\longrightarrow\{1,-1\}.
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

Associativity of node gluing makes $\chi^p$ a homomorphism to
$\{\pm1\}$ (a character).
Multiplying generator orientation lines by signs does not change
it: the two endpoint factors occur in both sides of
the comparison. Multiplying them by their grading
signs has the same property. Thus the character is
information about the orientations of the determinant
lines over all strip configurations, beyond the signs
of the differential. The relation of $\chi^p$ to the
orientation systems induced by relative spin structures in
quilted Floer theory~\cite{WW-orientations} is not studied
here; only the constancy of $\chi^p$ and its invariance under
reflection are used.

At a transverse point $x$, a second description is
available. Attach a fixed representative sphere to
the constant mixed pair $C_x$ and compare its
transported mixed orientation with the product of
the canonical orientation of $C_x$ and the complex
node orientation. Denote the ratio, the \emph{pointed
character}, by $\chi(\mathfrak h,x;d)$, where $\mathfrak h$ is the
primary perturbation and $d$ contains the
regularizing and almost-complex data.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:pointed-character}
If the Lagrangians of the presentation $p$ with data $(\mathfrak h,d)$ intersect transversely, then $\chi(\mathfrak h,x;d)=\chi^p(S_h)$ for every
intersection point $x$. The ratio is constant along
any compact smooth family of primary data carrying
a persistent transverse intersection point. It is
also independent of small regularizing data at
fixed primary data and fixed reference
almost-complex structure.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Glue a strip to the constant pair and attach the
sphere away from the ordinary gluing collar; moving
the sphere from the constant pair to the strip keeps
the node in the interior of a pure strip, where
Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(c) applies. The
two possible orders of these operations induce
the same determinant-line isomorphism. The sphere
and node factors are complex, hence even as real
graded lines, so commuting them with the strip
factor gives no sign. Cancelling the strip
orientation gives equality of the pointed and
strip definitions. A strip configuration exists
between any two intersection points, in some
relative homotopy class; it need not solve the
holomorphic equation. Applying the same argument
to that configuration shows independence of $x$.

For a family with a persistent point, the
constant-pair operators and the node-gluing map vary
continuously by Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}, and the
transported orientation of Proposition~\ref{prop:transport} by
Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting}.
The ratio is a continuous function to
$\{1,-1\}$ and is therefore constant. To vary
regularizing data, use the segment to zero data.
The perturbations are supported off the strip
end, and the constant-pair operators stay
invertible. Transporting their canonical
orientations along the segment proves the last
assertion. Compatible almost-complex structures
can be varied through the same argument, with
the reference almost-complex structure held fixed.
\end{proof}

The persistent point need only remain transverse
at that point. Other intersections of the two
Lagrangians may become degenerate. The lemma is
about determinant lines over configurations and
therefore does not require a regular moduli
space along the family.

\subsection{A common ball of primary perturbations}
Let $\mathcal C_i$ be the finite real span of the
sign-sensitive holonomy functions on $C_i^\circ$,
modulo constants. Use the norm
\begin{align}
 \|\mathfrak h_i\|_i={}&\sup_B\|\nabla_B\mathfrak h_i\|_{L^\infty}
  +\sup_{B\ne B'}
   \frac{\|\nabla_B\mathfrak h_i-\nabla_{B'}\mathfrak h_i\|_{L^2}}
        {\|B-B'\|_{L^2}},\label{eq:primary-norm}\\
 \|\mathfrak h\|={}&\max(\|\mathfrak h_0\|_0,\|\mathfrak h_1\|_1).\notag
\end{align}
The suprema are taken over smooth connections in a
fixed determinant bundle. Cylinder functions have
finite norm by the holonomy estimates of
\cite[Proposition~6.1]{DFL}. If the norm vanishes,
the gradient vanishes on the affine connection
space, so the function is constant; thus this is
a norm modulo constants.

Choose a finite trace family $F_0$ whose restrictions
to the compact flat-connection moduli spaces give
embedding coordinates. In the model
$L(C_i)\cong\SU(2)^{h-1}$, quaternion coordinates
can be obtained from sign-sensitive traces using
the fixed holonomies on the odd torus. Such traces
distinguish central sign changes; passing to
unsigned $\SO(3)$ traces would lose that property.
For a finite family $F$ containing $F_0$, put
\[
 \mathfrak B(F)=\{\mathfrak h\in\operatorname{span}F:\|\mathfrak h\|<r_*\},
 \qquad \mathfrak B=\bigcup_F\mathfrak B(F).
\]
The radius is independent of the additional
functions in $F$, for the fixed splitting and
metric data. These sets are nested under
inclusion of finite families and are convex.
They differ from the sets $\mathcal U(F)$ of Paper~I,
Definition~11.12. The small ball of
Section~\ref{subsec:admissible} is defined in the same way with a smaller radius $r_\chi$ and with $F$ subject to the conditions of Paper~I, Definition~3.3, chosen so that its
elements are also admissible in the sense of Paper~I.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:small-ball}
Fix the splitting and the metric data. The radius
$r_*>0$ in the definition of $\mathfrak B$ can be
chosen, independently of the finite family
$F\supset F_0$, so that every primary perturbation
$\mathfrak h$ with $\|\mathfrak h\|<r_*$ has the
following properties.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item The perturbed flat moduli space of each
compression body is a smooth compact manifold
identified with its unperturbed moduli space;
its boundary restriction is an embedded
Lagrangian close to the unperturbed one.
\item The connection representatives and their
first derivatives in the moduli parameters
are close in $L^2_1$ and $L^2$, respectively,
uniformly as $\|\mathfrak h\|\to0$.
\item The functions from $F_0$ remain embedding
coordinates on these perturbed moduli spaces.
\item If the two boundary Lagrangians intersect
transversely, the corresponding closed gauge
critical points are nondegenerate and are
exactly the lifts of those intersections.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The norm gives
$\|F_B\|_{L^\infty}\le\|\mathfrak h_i\|_i$ for an
$\mathfrak h_i$-flat connection. If a sequence with norm
tending to zero escaped every gauge neighbourhood
of the flat moduli space, Uhlenbeck compactness
on the compact compression body would give a
subsequence weakly converging in $W^{1,4}$,
after gauge, to a flat connection. The
$W^{1,4}$ embedding in $C^0$ is compact in
three dimensions. A Coulomb--Neumann slice
relative to the flat limit and the elliptic
estimate for $(d_B^*,d_B)$ then improve this
to convergence in $L^2_1$, a contradiction.
The boundary slice and compactness statements
are the versions for a manifold with boundary
in~\cite{Wehrheim-book}.

In a slice at a flat connection split into the
finite-dimensional harmonic space and its
complement. The linearized perturbed-flat
operator on the complement has a bounded
right inverse. The second term of
\eqref{eq:primary-norm} makes the holonomy
part a small Lipschitz perturbation in
$L^2$, so the contraction gives a graph over
the harmonic space. Differentiating its
fixed-point equation in the finite-dimensional
parameter gives the asserted first-derivative
control. Hodge decomposition and boundary
elliptic regularity identify these solutions
with the smooth perturbed-flat solutions;
the exact regularity inputs are recorded in
Appendix~\ref{app:inputs}. Compactness of the
flat moduli space makes the thresholds
uniform over finitely many slice charts.

For a trace function $\tau$, its bounded
Lipschitz gradient extends the identity
\[
 \tau(B')-\tau(B)=\int_0^1
 \langle\nabla_{B+s(B'-B)}\tau,B'-B\rangle\,ds
\]
to $L^2$ connections. Differentiating on the
graph family shows that the trace-coordinate
map remains $C^1$-close to its unperturbed
embedding. Openness of embeddings of a
compact manifold proves (iii). The matching
exact sequence for the two compression-body
boundary problems identifies the kernel
of the closed Hessian with the tangent
intersection of the Lagrangians, using the
restriction and deformation sequences of
\cite[Propositions~2.10 and~2.16]{DFL}, as in \cite[Lemmas~2.45 and~2.47]{DFL}; hence
transversality gives (iv).
\end{proof}

The norm controls exactly the two estimates
used in this proposition. On a fixed
finite-dimensional perturbation family all
higher smooth seminorms are finite and are
handled separately in compact-family
regularity statements.

\subsection{Paths with only birth--death singularities}
Enlarge $F$ to include the monomials of degree at
most five in the trace coordinates $F_0$.
These remain cylinder functions: a monomial
is a smooth invariant function of the joint
finite holonomy family.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:generic-path}
Two regular primary data in $\mathfrak B$
can be joined within a common $\mathfrak B(F)$
by a smooth path with finitely many singular
parameters. At each singular parameter
there is one quadratic tangency, with
versal normal form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:birth-death}
 S_t(x,y)=x^3+\sigma(t-t_*)x+Q(y),
 \qquad\sigma\in\{1,-1\},
\end{equation}
where $Q$ is nondegenerate. All other
intersections are transverse.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Start with the segment from $\mathfrak h_a$ to $\mathfrak h_b$
and perturb it to
\[
 \mathfrak h_t=(1-t)\mathfrak h_a+t\mathfrak h_b+\beta(t)c,
\]
where $\beta$ vanishes near the endpoints
and is positive on the interior where
singularities may occur. The segment is
compact in the open convex ball, so small
$c$ keeps the path in the ball.

Variation of the holonomy function induces
an exact Lagrangian deformation; its
Hamiltonian on the perturbed Lagrangian is
the restriction of that function to the
connection moduli space, by the Lagrangian
cobordism of \cite[Proposition~2.18]{DFL}. The trace
coordinates remain embedding coordinates
by Proposition~\ref{prop:small-ball}.
Polynomials of degree at most three in
embedding coordinates realize arbitrary
third jets at one point. Polynomials of
degree at most five realize arbitrary
second jets at two distinct points. For
the latter assertion, choose an affine function $\ell$ with
$\ell(a)=0$ and $\ell(b)=1$. Given a second jet at $b$, multiply
$\ell^3$ by the quadratic Taylor polynomial at $b$ of that jet
divided by $\ell^3$. The product has degree at most five, realizes
the prescribed jet at $b$, and vanishes to order three at $a$.
Use $(1-\ell)^3$ in the same way for the jet at $a$, then add
the two polynomials. Restriction of ambient jets
to an embedded submanifold is surjective.

In a Weinstein chart, varying one
Lagrangian changes the relative generating
function by this Hamiltonian to first
order. The map on its jets is triangular
with identity diagonal. Parametric
transversality therefore excludes higher
singularities, two simultaneous tangencies,
and tangencies with zero crossing speed:
these strata have codimension at least two.
A generic one-parameter path meets only
the codimension-one birth--death stratum,
transversely. Its parameter-dependent
splitting normal form is
\eqref{eq:birth-death}.

The singular-parameter set is closed by
compactness of the Lagrangians. Each
singular parameter is isolated by the
normal form, and the endpoint neighbourhoods
are regular. It is consequently finite.
No uniform separation of the two points
in the two-point jet argument is required;
transversality is applied on the open
configuration space of distinct pairs.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:persistent}
Suppose $H(C_G(M))\ne0$. The path in
Lemma~\ref{lem:generic-path} has a finite
subdivision with regular subdivision
points such that each subinterval carries
a continuous transverse intersection
point throughout the subinterval.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Every regular primary datum has at least
one intersection. Otherwise its gauge
complex would have no generators by
Proposition~\ref{prop:small-ball}(iv),
contradicting invariance and nonvanishing
of the gauge homology.

At a birth--death time the local pair
in~\eqref{eq:birth-death} is absent on
one side. If it were the only intersection
at the singular time, compactness would
force the nearby regular intersection set
on that side to be empty. Thus another
intersection exists at that time and is
transverse. The implicit function theorem
continues it across the wall.

On a closed interval with no walls, the
incidence set of intersections is a
compact one-manifold projecting locally
diffeomorphically to the interval. It is
a finite covering, hence a disjoint union
of sections. Extend the chosen transverse
point from each wall to neighbouring
regular subdivision times. This gives
the required finite collection of
persistent sections.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Constancy of the sphere character]
\label{thm:character-constancy}
Fix a splitting, metric data and a reference
almost-complex structure, and suppose
$H(C_G(M))\ne0$. Then the sphere character
$\chi^p(S_h)$ takes the same value at every
regular presentation $p$ with these data whose
primary data lie in $\mathfrak B$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
First remove the regularizing data at
both endpoints using
Lemma~\ref{lem:pointed-character}. Join
the primary data by the subdivided path
of Lemma~\ref{lem:persistent}. On each
subinterval the pointed character is
constant along its persistent transverse
point. At a regular subdivision time the
pointed character equals the global
character for any choice of point, so
one may switch to the persistent point
for the next subinterval. Chaining these
equalities gives constancy along the
whole path. Restore the endpoint
regularizing data. The same argument
allows variation of the compatible almost-complex structure on $\Msf_h$, with the reference almost-complex structure of the object fixed.
\end{proof}

The argument uses nonvanishing only to
find a point persisting through each
birth--death. It does not assume that
individual generators persist along an
arbitrary path, or that the regular
locus of the perturbation ball is
connected.

\section{Reflection of the transported orientations}
\label{sec:reflection}

This section constructs the reflection pairing
$\Phi_p:\CF(\bar p)\to\CF(p)^\vee$ (Definition~\ref{def:kappa} and
Proposition~\ref{prop:reflection-pairing}). Its existence rests on
Corollary~\ref{cor:reflection-character}: the transported systems of
$p$ and $\bar p$ have the same sphere character. The proof compares
the characters across the diagonal fold
(Proposition~\ref{prop:fold-character}), moving a sphere through the
unfolded triangle at zero collapse scale.

\subsection{Reflection of strip orientation systems}
The reflected presentation $\bar p$
interchanges the two compression bodies.
As in Paper~I, Section~11.2, its ordinary
strip data are the reflections of those of
$p$, namely $J(-s,1-t)$ and $-H(-s,1-t)$ with
the full one-form transformation, so that
reflection is a bijection between the strip
moduli spaces of $p$ and $\bar p$. This
convention is used in
Proposition~\ref{prop:reflection-pairing},
which asserts a chain isomorphism.
A strip is reflected by
\[
 \bar u(s,t)=u(-s,1-t).
\]
This map preserves the orientation of the
strip domain and keeps the surface
almost-complex structure. It reverses
the translation direction. The target
symplectic manifold retains the original
orientation; the reversed order of the
Lagrangians supplies the dual complex.
The formula reflects strips only; the
transported system of $\bar p$ is
obtained through the mixed domain of
$\bar p$.

For a strip from $x$ to $y$, the
reflection of its coherent orientation
has differential sign
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reflection-sign}
 \operatorname{sign}_{\rm dual}(\bar u)
   =-(-1)^{|y|}\operatorname{sign}(u).
\end{equation}
The minus sign is the reversed
translation vector; the other sign is
the graded transpose. In general the
\emph{reflection transpose} from $p$ to
$\bar p$ assigns to the reflection of a
strip of index $n$ on $p$ the sign
$(-1)^{n|w|}$, relative to the
identification of determinant lines
induced by the reflection, where $w$ is
the target of the strip on $p$. Thus this
reflected system is the one for which the
paired generator bases give the graded
dual differential. The reflection
transpose from $\bar p$ to $p$ is the same
construction with $p$ and $\bar p$
exchanged; its sign is read on the target
of the strip on $\bar p$. The two
reflection transposes are not inverse to
each other: on a strip of index $n$ their
composite is $(-1)^n$, the graded
evaluation sign in
Proposition~\ref{prop:reflection-pairing}.
The inverse of the reflection transpose
from $p$ to $\bar p$, whose sign is again
read on the target of the strip on $p$, is
called the \emph{unfolding transpose}; it
occurs in Lemma~\ref{lem:fold-coherence}.

Attaching $S_h$ changes the index by four.
The determinant factor at a sphere node
is complex and even. The additional
transpose sign is $(-1)^{4|y|}=1$.
Consequently reflection of a coherent
strip system preserves its sphere
character. What remains is to compare
this reflected system with the system
transported through the reflected mixed
domain.

\subsection{Capped folded triangles: strip gluing and unfolding}
We record the end-gluing comparison before using it to compare
sphere characters. For a capped folded configuration $c$ with
outputs $x$ on $\bar p$ and $y$ on $r$, write $F_c$ for its
determinant line after contracting the two normalized auxiliary
cap factors. The conversion line at $Z$ is retained in its fixed
position. Transport from the mixed configuration gives
$\Gamma_c:F_c\to L_{\bar p}(x)\otimes^{\mathrm r} L_r(y)$.
Thus this is a tensor with two outgoing ends. Bending the first
end gives a map from $L_{\bar p}(x)^\vee$, not from
$L_{\bar p}(x)$.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:fold-coherence}
Let $u$ be any smooth outgoing strip from $x$ to $x'$ on $\bar p$,
and $w$ any smooth outgoing strip from $y$ to $y'$ on $r$.
Capping commutes with their determinant gluing. For $k=0$,
unfolding along the diagonal commutes with the same gluing,
with the first outgoing strip reflected into an incoming strip
by the unfolding transpose. These
statements hold for arbitrary strip indices and configuration
classes.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Put $d_u=\det D_u$ and $d_w=\det D_w$. The assertion about the
end systems is the commutative diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
 F_c\otimes d_u\otimes d_w
 &\xrightarrow{\quad(\Gamma_u\otimes^{\mathrm r}\Gamma_w)\Gamma_c\quad}&
 L_{\bar p}(x')\otimes^{\mathrm r} L_r(y')\\
 \big\downarrow G&&\big\Vert\\
 F_{c\#(u,w)}&\xrightarrow{\qquad\Gamma_{c\#(u,w)}\qquad}&
 L_{\bar p}(x')\otimes^{\mathrm r} L_r(y').
\end{array}
\]
In the upper arrow the strip determinant factors are inserted
next to their corresponding generator factors using the right
tensor convention. In particular moving $d_u$ past $L_r(y)$
contributes $(-1)^{\ind(u)|y|}$.

Here $G$ is ordered determinant gluing, including the cap
contractions. Before contraction take the order
\[
 \mathscr I,\quad\det\Theta,\quad
 \det D_c,\quad\det D_{a_0},\quad\det D_{a_1},\quad d_u,\quad d_w,
\]
where $\mathscr I$ is the complete generator input line in cyclic
triangle order and $a_0,a_1$ are the two caps. If a sphere is
attached, append its complex node factor last. Both routes use
this order. Associativity regroups factors without interchanging
them; any permutation needed for an end convention is performed
on both routes with its graded sign. In particular the conversion
line is never carried through the moving configuration determinant.
The unit cap maps contract their paired generator and determinant
factors by even evaluation maps. No assumption that the individual
cap generator has even parity is needed.

First consider the completed mixed triangle at positive scale.
Its localized shift retains the ordinary two-input cobordism and
ends at that cobordism followed by a constant mixed pair. There
ordinary determinant associativity gives the end-gluing diagram,
with the conversion line and the fixed unit cap maps in the order
above. The relative family construction transports it over the
positive-scale configuration families.

Pull back the entire ordered diagram through
Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family}. Its ordinary-gluing compatibility
applies to the strip and cap operators at their ends in the cofinal
neck-and-scale regime defined in the statement of
Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family}. Only after this diagram commutes do
we cancel the lines of the corner pieces and contract the two cap
factors.
The fixed-width closed-cut line comparison retains the conversion
factor; its completion is expressed through the separate output
lines through the reference comparison $K_{0,x}$ of
Proposition~\ref{prop:reference-comparison}, not by completing the
fixed closed-$Z$ input itself. This proves the first diagram for
the limiting configurations. The limiting triangle is reached by
collapse, not by a shift to a constant triangle, which does not exist
because the triple intersection of its boundary labels is empty.

For unfolding with $k=0$, first choose doubling data retaining the
Hodge almost-complex structure and zero inhomogeneous germ in the
collapse neighbourhood, the compatible ordinary ends and the fixed
cap models. Choose a folded
configuration obtained from a smooth configuration on the unfolded
strip. The two coefficient systems are then restrictions of one
smooth system. At the $W^{1,p}\to L^p$ realization, $p>2$, equal
traces along the diagonal are exactly the condition for the two
sections to join to a $W^{1,p}$ section after reflecting the lower
side. Restriction is the inverse, and the same identification holds
for $L^p$ target sections. This is an operator identification.
At higher Sobolev orders equal traces alone would not suffice.
Instead identify the higher-order determinant lines through their
common smooth kernels and cokernels, using smooth stabilization
columns. Elliptic regularity makes these identifications commute
with gluing and parameter variation.

The comparison is first made on this doubling locus. Any finite
set of smooth configuration classes has representatives there:
smooth the joined maps in a small collar of the diagonal and keep
the end data fixed. The smoothing is a relative homotopy and
changes no sphere class. Interpolate the auxiliary almost-complex
and perturbation data to doubling data within the admitted data
space, preserving these germs and end values;
Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(a) gives the continuous
Fredholm families. Determinant transport extends the resulting
comparison back to the original finite configuration families.
The first gluing diagram and path independence in
Proposition~\ref{prop:transport} make it independent of these
homotopies. With stabilizations off the joining regions the
operator identification on the doubling locus gives
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
 F_c\otimes d_u\otimes d_w&\xrightarrow{G}&F_{c\#(u,w)}\\
 \big\downarrow U_{\rm ord}&&\big\downarrow U\\
 d_{Ru}\otimes\det D_{Uc}\otimes d_w
 &\xrightarrow{G}&\det D_{Ru\#Uc\#w}.
\end{array}
\]
Here $Ru$ runs from $x'$ to $x$ on $p$, so it attaches to the
incoming end of $Uc$, which runs from $x$ to $y$. The left arrow
$U_{\rm ord}$ applies unfolding and reflection and then interchanges
$F_c$ and $d_u$, with sign $(-1)^{|F_c|\ind(u)}$. At the
operator level the maps are restriction, reflection and the same
ordinary-end cutoff patching, whose supports are disjoint from
the diagonal. Their two composites agree. This is a diagram on
the zero-scale section problem: unfolding is applied only to
zero-scale operators, after collapse. The
displayed graded interchange expresses that agreement in the
chosen tensor order. Cancelling the ordered
stabilizations proves the displayed statement. The diagonal
normal factor has its complex orientation and is even.
Reflection of the strip $u$ of index $n$ moves its index line
across the paired endpoint line $L_{\bar p}(x)$ and contributes the sign
$(-1)^{n|x|}$. This is the unfolding transpose defined
after~\eqref{eq:reflection-sign}: the inverse of the reflection transpose
from $p$ to $\bar p$, applied to the strip $Ru$, which runs from $x'$ to
$x$ on $p$, so that the sign is read on the target $x$ of $Ru$ on $p$. It
is not the reflection transpose from $\bar p$ to $p$, whose sign
$(-1)^{n|x'|}$ is read on the target $x'$ of $u$ on $\bar p$ and differs
from $(-1)^{n|x|}$ by $(-1)^n$. At index four both signs equal $1$, as
for $(-1)^{4|y|}$ above.
For a strip counted modulo translation the reversed translation
vector adds the minus sign in \eqref{eq:reflection-sign}.
To check the total sign, put $a=|x|$, $b=|y|$ and
$f=|F_c|=a+b$. The outgoing gluing sign is $(-1)^{nb}$.
On bending, the reflected input sign $(-1)^{na}$ and the swap
$(-1)^{nf}$ have product $(-1)^{n(a+f)}=(-1)^{nb}$.
Thus the diagrams give the same sign at every index.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Sphere characters across the diagonal fold}
Fix one small fold pair with lower
primary datum $\widetilde p$ and upper
height datum $r$, both in the small
ball. Choose the height after the radius
of that ball, as in
Section~\ref{sec:choices}. Double the
folded triangle and attach its auxiliary
caps. Before bending, the resulting disk has two outgoing
strip ends modelled on $\bar{\widetilde p}$ and $r$.
By Lemma~\ref{lem:fold-coherence}, its transported orientation
is coherent with the independently transported strip system on
$\bar{\widetilde p}$ and the transported outgoing system on $r$.
The comparison with the dual system on $p$ is made only after
the sphere characters have been identified.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:fold-character}
The sphere character of the reflected lower presentation equals that
of the upper height presentation:
\[
 \chi^{\bar{\widetilde p}}(S_h)=\chi^r(S_h).
\]
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Choose a smooth reference configuration
$c_0$ on the doubled disk. Insert a representative sphere on
the first outgoing strip end at a fixed small positive plumbing
parameter. Move this smooth inserted configuration through the
unfolded disk to the second outgoing end, then refold. The two
resulting configurations lie in the same relative class. Under diagonal
unfolding the two sphere
classes are identified with the same generator of the relative
sphere group. In the product description this moves the class
between the two factors modulo the diagonal class. A reversal of
its representative replaces the generator by its negative and
does not change the sphere character, whose values are $\pm1$.

There is a continuous orientation on
the determinant bundle along this path.
Its construction on the folded domain
uses the collapse transport of
Section~\ref{sec:collapse}, the
closed-cut conversion, and the
reference comparison of
Proposition~\ref{prop:reference-comparison}. For the endpoint comparisons place
the sphere in an ordinary strip factor attached away from the
collapse neighbourhood. At the fixed positive plumbing parameter,
Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family} commutes with gluing that entire
strip. Its interior-node determinant comparison is the usual
punctured Cauchy--Riemann bordering and complex evaluation map;
the gauge rows are absent on this pure strip. Ordinary-gluing
associativity thus gives the endpoint comparison with the complex
even node factor appended last. The collapse scale and ordinary
neck can be chosen after this plumbing parameter, so the node and the
collapse never degenerate together. At the two ends,
the first diagram of Lemma~\ref{lem:fold-coherence}
identifies the transported orientation with both specified
strip-node gluing orientations.
No Koszul sign is introduced by moving
the complex node factor.

It remains to carry the node-glued orientation along the path, on
which the node crosses the image of the diagonal. Carry out the move at
zero collapse scale, on the doubling locus, with the doubling data of
Lemma~\ref{lem:fold-coherence}: the Hodge almost-complex structure and
zero inhomogeneous germ in the collapse neighbourhood. There the folded
operator is identified with the unfolded pure real Cauchy--Riemann
operator, by the $W^{1,p}\to L^p$ identification in the proof of that
lemma, and in the unfolded picture the image of the diagonal is an
interior line, not a matching or boundary collar. Along the whole path
the node lies in a disc separated from the matching and boundary
collars, so Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(c) makes the node gluing
a continuous isomorphism of line bundles along the path. Hence transport
carries the node-glued orientation at the start to the node-glued
orientation at the end. The transported orientation is continuous along
the same zero-scale path by Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family}: the path is
a compact smooth family of limiting configurations, and the collapse
datum is unperturbed near the diagonal. The ratios at the two ends are
therefore equal, giving the assertion.

This comparison uses arbitrary smooth
configurations, not only holomorphic
solutions. To justify coherence where
the fold is attached to prescribed
strip configurations, select one
reference configuration for each of the
finitely many generator pairs and their
required sphere classes. With the
height already fixed, choose the
ordinary neck lengths and then the
collapse scale so that ordinary gluing
applies to this finite set, in the order
of choices of Section~\ref{sec:choices}. The determinant-line construction
then extends over each configuration
component by path independence.
Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations} and
Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting} are
used on all the domains in this
finite construction, not only at the
constant solution.
\end{proof}

Changing the reference almost-complex
structure on the closed cut changes the output generator
lines by a fixed diagonal conversion.
That conversion cancels in a sphere
ratio. Thus the character comparison is
unchanged by the conversion required in
Section~\ref{sec:excision}, although that
conversion remains essential for the
cup identity as a chain-level formula.

\begin{corollary}[Reflection invariance]\label{cor:reflection-character}
Let $p$ be a small presentation of an object
with nonzero integral gauge homology. Then $p$ and
its reflection have the same sphere character:
\[
 \chi^{\bar p}(S_h)=\chi^p(S_h).
\]
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:character-constancy}
on $-M$ to compare $\bar p$ with
$\bar{\widetilde p}$. Apply
Proposition~\ref{prop:fold-character}
to compare the latter with $r$.
Apply character constancy on $M$ to
compare $r$ with $p$. The reflected
ball is the same ball under the
chosen reflection identification.
Gauge duality makes nonvanishing
for $M$ and $-M$ equivalent.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The reflection pairing}
\begin{definition}\label{def:kappa}
Let $p$ be a small presentation of an object with nonzero
integral gauge homology. On the strip configurations of $\bar p$
compare two orientation systems: the transported system of $\bar p$,
and the dual system of~\eqref{eq:reflection-sign}, which is the
reflection transpose from $p$ to $\bar p$ of the transported system
of $p$. Fix a reference intersection point $x_0$. For an
intersection point $x$, let $\kappa_p(x)\in\{1,-1\}$ be the ratio of
the two systems on a strip configuration of $\bar p$ from $x_0$ to
$x$. The \emph{reflection pairing} is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Phi-S}
 \Phi_p:\CF(\bar p)\longrightarrow\CF(p)^\vee,
 \qquad o_x^{\bar p}\longmapsto
                \kappa_p(x)(o_x^p)^\vee .
\end{equation}
Thus $\Phi_p$ is the integral form, for transported orientations, of
the standard duality $\CF(L_1,L_0)\cong\CF(L_0,L_1)^\vee$ given by
reversing strips, here with $(L_0,L_1)=(P_0,P_1)$.
\end{definition}

The ratio of the two systems is constant on smooth
configuration components and multiplicative under
concatenation. Changing the configuration from $x_0$
to $x$ changes its class by a sphere;
Corollary~\ref{cor:reflection-character} says that
the ratio on this sphere is one. Hence $\kappa_p(x)$
is well defined, and the ratio on a strip from $x$ to
$y$ is $\kappa_p(x)\kappa_p(y)$, independent of the
strip class.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:reflection-pairing}
The map $\Phi_p$ of~\eqref{eq:Phi-S}
is an even chain isomorphism. The
comparison of the orientations of the
determinant lines over all strip
configurations determines it up to one
sign. The
pairings may be chosen so that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Phi-symmetry}
 \Phi_{\bar p}=\Phi_p^\vee\ev.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Equation~\eqref{eq:reflection-sign}
and the ratio just computed give
exactly the dual differential under
\eqref{eq:Phi-S}. The paired reference
almost-complex structures make the sum of the two
endpoint parities zero, so the map
is even. Any two comparisons have
constant ratio: configurations
exist between every pair of
intersection points, and the ratio
on all those configurations fixes
the relative generator signs.
Only the initial reference sign
is free.

Apply to a strip of index $n$ with
endpoints $x,y$ the reflection
transpose from $p$ to $\bar p$ and then
the one from $\bar p$ to $p$, which
defines $\kappa_{\bar p}$. Their signs
are read on the two different endpoints
and multiply to
$(-1)^{n(|x|+|y|)}=(-1)^n$.
The corresponding change of the
paired basis is the sign in the
graded evaluation map. Thus the
pairing obtained by the second
reflection is exactly
$\Phi_p^\vee\ev$, after the common
reference sign is fixed. This
proves~\eqref{eq:Phi-symmetry} for the
orientations over all strip configurations.
\end{proof}

A chain-map condition alone would
not determine $\kappa_p$ up to
one sign. If the differential
splits into two subcomplexes,
one can change the sign on one
summand and still obtain a chain
map. The preceding construction
uses all strip configurations,
including those which are not
solutions, to exclude that extra
freedom.

Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hat-Phi}
 \widehat\Phi_p=\Phi_p^\vee\ev:
   \CF(p)\longrightarrow\CF(\bar p)^\vee.
\end{equation}
This is the reflection pairing
which will be composed with the
cup map~\eqref{eq:check-cup}.

\section{Continuation and the diagonal compression cup}
\label{sec:continuation}

\subsection{Coherent orientations with prescribed ends}
A continuation datum from $p$ to $q$ has a strip-like
surface with Lagrangian boundary conditions varying
on a compact interval and equal to those of $p,q$
at its ends. The almost-complex and Hamiltonian
data are likewise constant outside that interval.
Throughout this section the primary paths lie in the common convex
ball of Proposition~\ref{prop:small-ball}; after Hamiltonian
identification, their exact-graph primitives lie in a fixed convex
neighbourhood. Homotopies have fixed endpoints and remain in this
neighbourhood. Almost-complex and Hamiltonian continuation data are
varied with these fixed boundary identifications. All statements of
independence below concern this class of data.
For generators $x,y$, let $\mathcal B_{p,q}(x,y)$
be its smooth configuration space, with the
prescribed exponential limits. A coherent
orientation system assigns relative orientations
to the determinant lines over these spaces and
commutes with attaching strips at either end.

The space of configurations in a fixed relative
homotopy class is path connected. One may first
choose a continuous relative homotopy, smooth it
relative to the fixed ends and labels, and then
make it constant beyond common end cutoffs. Local
charts are given by an exponential map for a
metric making the boundary labels totally
geodesic. The topology in
Section~\ref{sec:geometry} makes these paths
continuous. The relative classes are a torsor
for the same sphere group as fixed-label strips,
and the index changes by four under its generator.
In particular there is at most one index-zero
component for fixed endpoints.

\begin{lemma}[Rigidity of coherent systems]\label{lem:rigidity}
Fix a continuation datum from $p$ to $q$ in the
class described at the start of this subsection.
Two coherent continuation orientation systems
with the same end systems differ by one sign.
Such a system exists if and only if the sphere
characters at the ends agree.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The ratio of two systems is constant on each
configuration component. If a strip is attached
at either end, the common end orientation cancels,
so the ratio does not change. Choose one
reference configuration from $x_0$ to $y_0$.
For any $x,y$, choose strips from $x$ to $x_0$
and from $y_0$ to $y$. Attaching these strips
transports the ratio to a configuration with
ends $x,y$. Any difference in relative class
can be supplied by a sphere class on either
strip. Since the end systems are the same,
this also leaves the ratio unchanged. Thus
there is only one sign for all configurations.

For existence choose a reference orientation
and extend it by the same gluing procedure.
The determinant bundle on each configuration
component is orientable: the boundary
Lagrangians are the parallelizable compression-
body Lagrangians \cite[Theorem~5.1.6]{WW-orientations}, and the remaining sphere
factors have complex orientation. The only
compatibility left when the gluing paths are
changed is moving a sphere class from the
incoming end to the outgoing end. The two
extensions agree precisely when the two sphere
characters agree. This also proves necessity
by moving the node of one sphere through a
continuation configuration. The construction
is continuous because the determinant line
is a line bundle over the smooth
configuration space.
\end{proof}

In an index-zero comparison a finite form of
the lemma suffices. Choose one strip for each
input generator and one for each output
generator, in the classes needed to reach
the index-zero component. Coherence on that
finite collection fixes the ratios of all
zero-dimensional counts. This form allows
the neck lengths and collapse scales to be
chosen after a finite set of configurations has
been fixed, so that estimates are needed only on
that finite set.

At an object with nonzero integral gauge homology, Theorem~\ref{thm:character-constancy} supplies existence whenever $p$ and $q$ have the same
splitting, metric data and reference almost-complex structure, and primary
data in the ball $\mathfrak B$ of
Section~\ref{sec:characters}. This includes the
auxiliary upper data of
Section~\ref{subsec:admissible}, which lie in
$\mathfrak B$ but in general not in the small
ball. For a lower datum and an auxiliary upper
height datum, the folded configuration itself
also supplies a coherent system and hence the
required character equality; moving the node through
the unfolded configuration crosses the image of the
diagonal, and this is justified as in the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:fold-character}.

\subsection{Parity and continuation maps}
For a continuation configuration $c$ from $x$
to $y$, the \emph{continuation parity}
\[
 \operatorname{par}(p,q)=|y|-|x|-\ind D_c\pmod2
\]
is independent of the configuration and its
end generators. It is additive under
composition of continuation data. The
configuration description by sphere classes, together with a
homotopy in the specified small family, shows its independence of
the chosen datum in that family.
For the paired reference almost-complex structures of this paper,
$\operatorname{par}(p,q)=0$ at the data used in the comparison,
at an object with nonzero integral gauge homology.
The proof uses the integral cup formula, so that
only the nonvanishing of the integral homology
enters.

Indeed, at a small fold pair $(p,r)$ the map
$B(p,r)$ is homogeneous and
\eqref{eq:cup-zero} identifies it up to
homotopy with the even homotopy equivalence
$N_r\cont_G(p,r)n_p$. On nonzero integral
homology this map cannot be both even and
odd. Thus its degree is zero. The doubled
configuration has the same index as the
continuation problem, and the gauge
reflection pairing is even; hence
$\operatorname{par}(p,r)=0$. A common upper height datum
and additivity give $\operatorname{par}(p,q)=0$. The integral cup formula used here is proved in Section~\ref{sec:cup} without symplectic continuation maps, which are introduced only below.

With a coherent system define
\[
 \cont_S(p,q):\CF(p)\longrightarrow\CF(q)
\]
by the signed zero-dimensional continuation
count. It is even and commutes with the
differential. The compactification of a
one-dimensional continuation moduli space
proves this exactly as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:N-chain}.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:continuation}
Let $p,q,r$ be regular presentations of an object
with nonzero integral gauge homology, with the
same splitting and metric data and with primary
data in the ball $\mathfrak B$ of
Section~\ref{sec:characters}, and define their
continuation maps as above. These maps satisfy,
up to sign and chain homotopy,
\begin{align}
 \cont_S(q,r)\cont_S(p,q)&\simeq
                 \pm\cont_S(p,r),\label{eq:cont-composition}\\
 \cont_S(p,p)&\simeq\pm1.\label{eq:cont-unit}
\end{align}
Their homotopy classes are independent of
the continuation datum. They are homotopy
equivalences.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For a long strip neck, ordinary gluing
identifies the zero-dimensional solutions
with pairs of continuation solutions.
The determinant gluing is coherent, so
the signed matrix is the composite matrix.
The glued orientation system has the same
end systems as the chosen direct
continuation system. Lemma~\ref{lem:rigidity}
compares them by one sign. Varying the
neck length gives the chain homotopy in
\eqref{eq:cont-composition}.

A homotopy of continuation data, constant
near the ends, gives the corresponding
homotopy by its oriented one-dimensional
boundary. The pure symplectic compactness
and gluing used here are
Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(c). For
constant data the zero-dimensional
solutions are constant strips, with
canonical positive orientation. This
normalizes the unit. Apply composition
to a datum and its reverse to obtain a
homotopy inverse.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Continuation maps under reflection}
There is no translation quotient for a
continuation configuration. Reflecting it
therefore has the reflection transpose sign of Section~\ref{sec:reflection}
but no additional translation sign.
Using the pairings of
Proposition~\ref{prop:reflection-pairing}
gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cont-reflection}
 \cont_S(\bar p,\bar q)\simeq
 \pm\Phi_q^{-1}\cont_S(q,p)^\vee\Phi_p.
\end{equation}
To verify coherence of the reflected
system, glue an input strip, a
continuation configuration, and an
output strip, and reflect the three
pieces in reverse order. If their
indices are $n_u,n_c,n_w$, the reordering
sign is
$(-1)^{n_un_c+n_un_w+n_cn_w}$.
Combining it with the three transpose
signs leaves $(-1)^{n_u\operatorname{par}(p,q)}=1$.
The generator corrections $\kappa_p$
and $\kappa_q$ identify the reflected
end systems with the transported
systems of $\bar p$ and $\bar q$. Rigidity then gives the one
sign in~\eqref{eq:cont-reflection}.
This argument also shows why parity
zero is needed here.

\subsection{The diagonal compression cup equals continuation}
For $k=0$ the compression correspondence
is the diagonal. Double the folded
triangle, glue the two distinguished
caps, and unfold along that diagonal.
The resulting surface is a continuation
strip. This is the geometric
identification used in Paper~I. To
obtain an integral statement we compare
its coherent systems before counting.

\begin{proposition}[Diagonal cup]\label{prop:diagonal-cup}
At every small fold pair $(p,r)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:diagonal-cup}
 B(p,r)\widehat\Phi_p\simeq
                  \pm\cont_S(p,r).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Use both diagrams of Lemma~\ref{lem:fold-coherence}. After
unfolding, the incoming system is the image of the transported
system of $\bar p$ under the unfolding transpose, the inverse of the
reflection transpose from $p$ to $\bar p$ (see the paragraph
after~\eqref{eq:reflection-sign}). Its comparison with the system on
$p$ is the ratio of orientation systems over all strip configurations
that defines $\kappa_p$ (Definition~\ref{def:kappa}). Indeed, by the definition of $\kappa_p$, on every strip of
$\bar p$ between $x$ and $y$ the transported system of $\bar p$ is
$\kappa_p(x)\kappa_p(y)$ times the reflection transpose of the transported
system of $p$. Applying the inverse transpose, on every strip of $p$
from $x$ to $y$ the incoming system is $\kappa_p(x)\kappa_p(y)$ times
the transported system of $p$. These are precisely the two endpoint
corrections in $\widehat\Phi_p$. The reflection transpose from $\bar p$
to $p$, which enters the double reflection in the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:reflection-pairing}, would instead give the ratio
$(-1)^{|x|+|y|}\kappa_p(x)\kappa_p(y)$, and the comparison below would
leave the grading operator $\eps_{\CF(p)}$, which anticommutes with
the differential. On a homogeneous generator the coefficient of $\widehat\Phi_p$ is $(-1)^{|x|}\kappa_p(x)$, since the graded evaluation
map enters \eqref{eq:hat-Phi}. Bending by $\operatorname{bend}_{\CF(\bar p)}$ supplies
the second factor $(-1)^{|x|}$; the two evaluation signs cancel.
Thus precomposition by
$\widehat\Phi_p$ changes the unfolded input system into the
prescribed system on $p$. The outgoing system is already the
prescribed one on $r$. This holds for every configuration
class and every strip index, as rigidity requires.

The pulled-back capped system and the chosen continuation system
therefore have the same two end systems. Their relative sign is
constant under the two gluing operations in the diagrams. Rigidity
(Lemma~\ref{lem:rigidity}) applies and leaves one scalar sign. A
nonconstant generator-sign automorphism would change one of those
gluing maps of determinant lines over strip configurations; a grading operator would change
an odd-index gluing map. Both have been fixed before invoking
rigidity. This proves the required comparison of coherent systems.

For sufficiently long cap necks,
the gluing theorem gives a bijection
of zero-dimensional solutions and
preserves these orientations. A cap
of index zero has one regular
solution with unit coefficient, so
no extra multiplicity occurs. The
counts are therefore the same
up to that sign. Varying the cap
length and the triangle data to the
ones defining $B(p,r)$ gives a
chain homotopy through the
one-dimensional moduli spaces.
The preglue-to-solution path is
within the pure section chart,
and determinant transport on that
path preserves the canonical
orientation of a regular solution.
This is the pure symplectic gluing
input of Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(c).
\end{proof}

This completes the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:C}.
The cup in that theorem is the geometric
compression cup of Paper~I, and it is
compared directly with the geometric
continuation map.

\subsection{Objects with vanishing homology}\label{subsec:zero}
No object with vanishing integral gauge homology is known (see the remark after
Theorem~\ref{thm:A}); the convention below makes the statements unconditional.
Suppose $H(C_G(M))=0$. Lemma~\ref{lem:acyclic}
and Proposition~\ref{prop:N-isomorphism}
make both $C_G(M,p)$ and $\CF(M,p)$
contractible for every presentation.
Every closed map from or to a
contractible complex is a boundary:
compose it with a contracting
homotopy and use
\eqref{eq:compose-homotopy}. Hence
there is exactly one morphism
between it and any other object
in the homotopy category.

At such an object we take the
continuation and reflection maps
needed for the comparison to be
zero representatives of those
unique morphisms. In particular
a reflection map here is a
homotopy equivalence between zero
objects of $\cK(\ZZ)$. Character constancy
and the reflection pairing of
Definition~\ref{def:kappa} are used only at objects with
nonzero integral gauge homology. Every
identity involving an acyclic object is
interpreted in the homotopy category.

This convention also handles a
word passing through an acyclic
intermediate object: its class is
zero, as is the corresponding
gauge composite. Reduction modulo
two preserves a contracting
homotopy. Thus Paper~I's maps
incident to that object are
null-homotopic as well.

Nonzero mod-two gauge homology
implies nonzero integral gauge
homology, since an acyclic finite
free integral complex would be
contractible. Integral framed
calculations place rational homology
spheres and $\#^k(S^1\times S^2)$ in
the nonvanishing case: for a rational
homology sphere $Y$ the Euler
characteristic of $I^\#(Y)$ is
$|H_1(Y;\ZZ)|$
\cite[Corollary~1.4]{Scaduto}, and
$I^\#(\#^k(S^1\times S^2))$ is computed in
\cite[Section~7.8]{Scaduto}.
Only this implication is used. The
converse fails in general: integral
homology that is finite of odd order
gives zero homology modulo two.

\section{Small data, naturality of the mixed maps and handle squares}
\label{sec:choices}

\subsection{Order of the auxiliary choices and small data}
\label{subsec:admissible}
This subsection constructs the radius $r_\chi$ of the small ball
of Definition~\ref{def:admissible-intro}, and fixes the order in which
the remaining auxiliary data are chosen.
Items (1)--(3) below are made once for each Heegaard splitting with its metric data, together with its reflection, before any comparison is considered.
They are transported by isomorphisms of decorated data: for such an
isomorphism $\phi$, the choices for $\phi(\Sigma)$, with the
transported metric data and reference almost-complex structure, are
the transports of those for $\Sigma$. Since the norm
\eqref{eq:primary-norm} is invariant under bundle automorphisms, frame
changes preserve them. Items (4)--(8) concern the finitely many
presentations of one comparison. The order avoids requiring a
perturbation to be simultaneously small relative to a parameter chosen
after it.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
\item Fix the trace-coordinate family $F_0$ of
Section~\ref{sec:characters}, and let the primary regularity threshold
$r_H$ be the radius $r_*$ of Proposition~\ref{prop:small-ball}.
\item Choose the auxiliary height $t_*$ below the height thresholds of
Section~\ref{sec:cup} and of the admissible neighbourhoods~\ref{I5} of
Paper~I. Choose it so small that its height perturbations, together
with every generic correction in the ball $|\gamma|\le\delta(t_*)$ of
Section~\ref{sec:cup}, have norm less than $r_H$. These data are used
as upper auxiliary presentations.
\item Choose the radius $r_\chi<r_H$ with the following property, for
the splitting and for its reflection. If a primary datum lies in the
span of a finite family containing $F_0$ and has norm less than
$r_\chi$, then its Lagrangians lie in the lower-data neighbourhood
$\mathcal N_C(t_*)$ of Section~\ref{sec:cup} for the product
compression body, and in the neighbourhoods that define admissible
primary data in Paper~I, Definition~11.12 (see~\ref{I5}). Such a radius
exists because these neighbourhoods are $C^1$-neighbourhoods of the
unperturbed pair of Lagrangians, and by
Proposition~\ref{prop:small-ball}(ii),(iii) and its proof the
perturbed Lagrangians converge in $C^1$, uniformly in the finite
family, as the norm tends to zero.
\item Choose smaller heights $t''$ for the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:mixed-duality},
$t_f$ for the reflection fold and $t_C$ for the compression cup and,
for a Heegaard stabilization or destabilization, the two-handle heights
$t_2,t_2'$ and the generic perturbations $R_0',R_0''$ of $R_0$ used for
stabilization in Section~\ref{sec:descent} (Paper~I, proof of
Proposition~12.4). Choose them so that the resulting upper and
intermediate presentations are small in the sense defined below.
Choose the lower datum of a cup after $t_C$, sufficiently small
relative to that height.
\item Fix the ordinary gauge trajectory data and the symplectic
strip-end data for every required end, including the intermediate
closed cuts. Make the simultaneous regular end choices before
selecting their weights. Choose a common sufficiently small weight
below the relevant primal, adjoint and scalar thresholds. For
configuration reference paths it must also lie below their
remainder rates; relative end preparation then supplies faster
reference paths when needed. The completed-domain estimates at
these choices are Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(b).
\item Select the finite compact reference families needed for
coherence, and regularize, with these fixed ends, the limiting
problems on the mixed domains defining the maps $N_p$, on the
triangles and on the completed cores. The transport of their
primary data, their finite stabilizations and their compact
secondary supports are fixed before taking a geometric limit. In particular an
auxiliary upper datum is a full presentation with its own
thresholds for the mixed map. Keep the collapsing compression unperturbed,
including the dependence regions of the holonomy terms.
\item Choose the ordinary necks after these limiting data have
been fixed. Then choose the collapse scales from the bounds for
the compact reference families and the finite limiting index-zero
solution sets, with their decay rates and the already fixed
weights. For simultaneous determinant comparisons use the cofinal
neck-and-scale regime in Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family}. Each
threshold therefore depends only on parameters already chosen.
\item At the resulting positive geometric parameters choose any
further compactly supported secondary regularizers within the
openness thresholds for the endpoint counts, preserving every
end equation fixed in item~(5). Extend them relatively over the comparison
families. If an end datum or a limiting regularization is changed,
reselect the dependent necks and collapse scales and use the
corresponding data homotopies. Independence over the full permitted
weight range is applied only after this common small-weight
construction, as in Proposition~\ref{prop:weights}.
\end{enumerate}
Every choice has a positive threshold
once the preceding finite data have
been fixed.

With the radius $r_\chi$ of item~(3), the
small ball and the small presentations
are those of Definition~\ref{def:admissible-intro}.
The slices of the small ball by the finite
families are convex, and two of its elements lie
in a common slice. It lies in the ball
$\mathfrak B$ of Section~\ref{sec:characters},
and its elements are admissible primary data in
the sense of Paper~I (see~\ref{I5}). Small
fold pairs are those of
Definition~\ref{def:fold-pair}(c): the lower
presentation is small with Lagrangians in
$\mathcal N_C(t)$, and the primary data of the
upper height presentation lie in $\mathfrak B$.
For $k\ge1$,
\emph{compatible handle data} consist of a
small lower presentation $p$ and the upper
height presentation $q=C\circ p$ at the height
$t_C$, subject to the same conditions. The
Lagrangians of $p$ lie in $\mathcal N_C(t_C)$,
and its primary data lie in the neighbourhood
$\mathcal N_{C,R}$ of Paper~I, Lemma~11.7, on
which the grading and coefficient statements of
that lemma hold. By item~(4), $q$ is small.
A \emph{small decomposition} of a morphism,
from $(M_0,p_0)$ to $(M_1,p_1)$, is a word of
elementary moves as in Paper~I, Section~13.2:
changes of presentation, frame changes, one- and
three-handle attachments and two-handle
cobordisms. All presentations in it are
small, its maps $S_e$ are those of
Section~\ref{sec:descent}, and its auxiliary data
are chosen as in items~(4)--(8).

The auxiliary upper data of item~(2) lie in
$\mathfrak B$ but in general not in the
small ball. At an object with nonzero
integral gauge homology,
Theorem~\ref{thm:character-constancy} applies to
them, so their coherent continuation
systems exist by Lemma~\ref{lem:rigidity}. The
upper data at the heights of item~(4), and in
particular the handle targets at which
reflection or mixed duality
(Proposition~\ref{prop:mixed-duality}) is used,
are small.

\subsection{Naturality under small changes of holonomy perturbation}
The mixed maps commute, up to sign and
homotopy, with the gauge and symplectic
continuation maps for changes of primary data
within a small ball. All maps in this
subsection and the next are even.

\begin{proposition}[Primary naturality]\label{prop:primary-naturality}
For small presentations $p,q$ with the same splitting and metric data,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:primary-naturality}
 N_q\cont_G(p,q)\simeq
              \pm\cont_S(p,q)N_p.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Choose one upper height presentation $a$
of $-M$ at the height $t_*$ of item~(2)
of Section~\ref{subsec:admissible}, with
its correction in the ball
$|\gamma|\le\delta(t_*)$ generic for $\bar p$
and $\bar q$. By item~(3) there,
$(\bar p,a)$ and $(\bar q,a)$ are
small fold pairs. Apply the cup formula and
Proposition~\ref{prop:diagonal-cup} to
both fold pairs. They give
\begin{align*}
 \cont_S(\bar p,a)&\simeq
 \pm N_a\cont_G(\bar p,a)
                       n_{\bar p}\widehat\Phi_{\bar p},\\
 \cont_S(\bar q,a)&\simeq
 \pm N_a\cont_G(\bar q,a)
                       n_{\bar q}\widehat\Phi_{\bar q}.
\end{align*}
All factors being eliminated are
homotopy equivalences. Cancel the
common upper datum using continuation
composition. Thus
\begin{align*}
 \cont_S(\bar p,\bar q)\simeq\pm
 &(n_{\bar q}\widehat\Phi_{\bar q})^{-1}
       \cont_G(\bar p,\bar q)\\
 &\hspace{30mm}\cdot
       n_{\bar p}\widehat\Phi_{\bar p}.
\end{align*}
Use the reflection formula
\eqref{eq:cont-reflection} on the
symplectic side and its gauge analogue, Lemma~\ref{lem:gauge-transpose} for gauge continuation, together with~\eqref{eq:gauge-duality}. The
symmetric conventions give
\[
 \widehat\Phi_{\bar p}=\Phi_p,
 \qquad \Phi_{G,M}n_{\bar p}=N_p^\vee,
\]
and the same equalities at $q$.
Cancelling these pairings transforms
the displayed identity into
\[
 \cont_S(q,p)^\vee\simeq
 \pm\bigl(N_p\cont_G(q,p)N_q^{-1}\bigr)^\vee.
\]
Transpose and rename $p,q$ to obtain
\eqref{eq:primary-naturality}.
Every double-dual map occurs together
with its inverse. Because the maps
here are even, the cancellations
introduce no grading-dependent sign.
\end{proof}

This proof uses the same pairing in
the diagonal-cup and reflection
identities. Choosing an unrelated
chain-level duality isomorphism would
leave a diagonal automorphism after
the cancellation.

\subsection{The reflected mixed map inverts \texorpdfstring{$N_p$}{N\_p}}
Following Paper~I, write $N_p^\dagger$ for the
transpose of the mixed map of the reflected
presentation $\bar p$, read through the gauge
and symplectic reflection pairings:
\[
 N_p^\dagger=n_p\widehat\Phi_p:\CF(p)\longrightarrow C_G(M),
\]
with $n_p$ as in~\eqref{eq:n-p} and
$\widehat\Phi_p$ as in~\eqref{eq:hat-Phi}.
Modulo two it is the map $N_{\bar P}^\vee$ of
Paper~I, Proposition~11.9. The next proposition
shows that it is a homotopy inverse of $N_p$, up
to sign.

\begin{proposition}[Mixed duality]\label{prop:mixed-duality}
For every small presentation $p$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mixed-duality}
 N_pN_p^\dagger\simeq\pm1.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
First choose $p$ so that $(p,r)$ is a small fold pair at the height $t''$; by item~(4) of
Section~\ref{subsec:admissible}, $r$ is
small. The cup formula~\eqref{eq:cup-zero}
and Proposition~\ref{prop:diagonal-cup} give
\[
 \cont_S(p,r)\simeq
 \pm N_r\cont_G(p,r)n_p\widehat\Phi_p.
\]
By primary naturality the right side
is homotopic, up to sign, to
$\cont_S(p,r)N_pN_p^\dagger$.
Cancel the continuation equivalence.

For a general small presentation $q$
with the same splitting and metric data,
transpose primary naturality for the
reflected presentations. The symmetric
pairings give
\[
 N_q^\dagger\cont_S(p,q)\simeq
             \pm\cont_G(p,q)N_p^\dagger.
\]
Combine this with primary naturality
and~\eqref{eq:mixed-duality} at $p$:
\[
 N_qN_q^\dagger\cont_S(p,q)
             \simeq\pm\cont_S(p,q).
\]
Cancel the continuation once more.
The contractible case has the
interpretation of
Section~\ref{subsec:zero}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{One- and three-handle squares}
At compatible handle data
(Section~\ref{subsec:admissible}) define
the integral compression cup by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:U}
 U_C=\check U_C\widehat\Phi_p:
               \CF(p)\longrightarrow\CF(q),
 \qquad q=C\circ p.
\end{equation}
Define the cap by reflected
transposition. If $\bar C$ is the
reflected compression and
$\check U_{\bar C}:\CF(p)^\vee\to\CF(\bar q)$
is its unpaired cup, put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:V}
 \check V_C=\ev_{\CF(p)}^{-1}
                  (\check U_{\bar C})^\vee,
 \qquad V_C=\check V_C\widehat\Phi_q.
\end{equation}
Then $V_C:\CF(q)\to\CF(p)$. Equivalently
it is the transpose of the paired
reflected cup with the two pairings
inserted. These are the orientations
of the compression cup and cap counts
of Paper~I. At other presentations
they are conjugated by the geometric
continuation maps.

\begin{theorem}[One- and three-handle squares]\label{thm:handles}
Let $C=C_{h,g}$ be the compression body adding
$k$ handles, $W_k:M\to M^+$ the corresponding
one-handle cobordism, and $W_3:M^+\to M$ the
three-handle cobordism whose reversal
$\overline W_3:-M\to-M^+$ is the one-handle
cobordism of the reflected compression $\bar C$.
Let $(p,q)$ be compatible handle data, with
$U_C$ and $V_C$ as in~\eqref{eq:U}
and~\eqref{eq:V}. Then
\begin{align}
 U_CN_p&\simeq\pm N_q\IG(W_k),
                                      \label{eq:onehandle}\\
 V_CN_q&\simeq\pm N_p\IG(W_3).
                                      \label{eq:threehandle}
\end{align}
The identities hold at compatible handle
data and, with the cup and cap conjugated by
continuations, at all small endpoint
presentations with the splittings and metric
data of the handle data; other endpoints are
reached through Section~\ref{sec:descent}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The cup formula (Proposition~\ref{prop:cup-formula})
and mixed duality (Proposition~\ref{prop:mixed-duality}) give
\[
 U_CN_p\simeq
 \pm N_q\IG(W_k)n_p\widehat\Phi_pN_p
 \simeq\pm N_q\IG(W_k),
\]
which proves~\eqref{eq:onehandle}.
For the cap, transpose the cup formula
at the reflected compression. The
graded evaluation maps and
$\Phi_{G,-M}=\Phi_{G,M}^\vee\ev$
give the gauge transpose of the reversed handle map (Lemma~\ref{lem:gauge-transpose} applied to $\overline W_3$). Mixed duality
at $q$ cancels the reflected mixed
map. In detail, in the homotopy
category,
\begin{align*}
 V_CN_q
 &=\check V_C\widehat\Phi_qN_q\\
 &\simeq\pm\check V_C n_q^{-1}\\
 &\simeq\pm N_p\Phi_{G,-M}^{-1}
            \IG(\overline W_3)^\vee
            \Phi_{G,-M^+}\\
 &\simeq\pm N_p\IG(W_3).
\end{align*}
The only potentially odd map in the
transpose comparison is the handle
map; all pairings and mixed maps
are even. The last step is Lemma~\ref{lem:gauge-transpose} for $\overline W_3:-M\to-M^+$, whose reversal is $W_3$; it holds with one sign although the handle map may be odd.

Finally conjugate both handle maps
by continuations from arbitrary
small endpoints to the
compatible handle data. Primary
naturality moves the mixed maps
past these continuations, and
gauge functoriality identifies the
gauge composite with the same
cobordism. Homotopies compose by
\eqref{eq:compose-homotopy}.
\end{proof}

\section{Descent to cobordisms and proof of the main theorem}
\label{sec:descent}

\subsection{Squares for the remaining elementary moves}
The geometric presentation moves are the
ones in Paper~I. We record how the
integral orientations enter them. A change
of meridian disk systems does not change
the Lagrangians; its map is the identity.
A change of regularizing data uses
the family defining
Proposition~\ref{prop:N-isomorphism}.
A change of strip-end almost-complex
structure uses Proposition~\ref{prop:end-data}(ii),
with the outgoing continuation map of that
proposition and the fixed transported end systems.
For a change of the reference almost-complex
structure, the gauge and symplectic generator
orientation lines are converted by the same
right-placed conversion maps. The constant pair used as
reference has no further line, so its orientation
comparison commutes with this conversion.

An isomorphism of decorated data
pulls back the equation, its
linearization and the shifting
construction; the pullback of a
shift is a shift, and the
orientation maps do not depend on
the shifting path
(Proposition~\ref{prop:transport}). Choose pulled-back
stabilizations in the determinant
construction. The kernel gluing
maps then commute with the
isomorphism, and the mixed square
commutes exactly on chains at the
pulled-back data. This applies
to frame changes and diffeomorphisms
preserving the torus decoration. The
transported presentation is small,
since the choices of
Section~\ref{subsec:admissible} are natural
under these isomorphisms. For a frame
change it has the same splitting and metric
data, and a subsequent continuation
(Proposition~\ref{prop:primary-naturality})
gives the square at the originally chosen
data, up to sign and homotopy. For a
diffeomorphism the transported reference
almost-complex structure is compared with the
given one by the conversion of the preceding
paragraph, and the
subsequent change of presentation is a
composite of the moves treated in this
section.

A Heegaard stabilization is realized
by the cancelling one- and two-handle
pair of Paper~I. Let $r$ be the
stabilized presentation. The one-handle
square ends at the presentation $C\circ p$,
which carries height perturbations on
both bodies, while the two-handle square
holds from $(C\circ p)'$ to $r'$. Here
$(C\circ p)'$ is $C\circ p$ with the Lagrangian $R_0$ of its first body replaced by a generic small perturbation $R_0'$,
supported away from its critical set, and with
zero primary perturbation on its second body;
$r'$ is $r$ with $R_0'$ on its first body and
the two-handle height perturbation at height
$t_2$ on its second body (Paper~I, proof of
Proposition~12.4). As in Paper~I, insert the
continuations $\cont_S(C\circ p,(C\circ p)')$
and $\cont_S(r',r)$; their squares are
Proposition~\ref{prop:primary-naturality},
since $(C\circ p)'$ and $r'$ are
small by item~(4) of
Section~\ref{subsec:admissible}. Compose
\eqref{eq:onehandle}, these two squares
and \eqref{eq:twohandle}. The decorated
gauge cobordism is the identity,
so its map is continuation up to
sign and homotopy. The reverse
stabilization is treated by the
cancelling two- and three-handle
pair. Passing through a common
stabilization treats changes of
metric data at a fixed splitting. An
isotopy of the splitting fixed near the
ball is treated as in Paper~I,
Proposition~12.4: transport by the
isotopy, as in the preceding paragraph,
followed by a change of metric data and
a change of primary data. The order of
choices in Section~\ref{subsec:admissible}
allows
the new presentations to satisfy the
integral height and ball conditions.
Every orientation comparison in this
argument is one of the signed squares
above.

Thus every elementary move $e:p\to q$ has a
symplectic map $S_e$, constructed from the
curves of Paper~I, and a square
\begin{equation}\label{eq:edge-square}
 N_qG_e\simeq\pm S_eN_p.
\end{equation}
Here $G_e$ is the gauge map of the decorated
cobordism of $e$; for a change of presentation
it is a gauge continuation map. Changes of
presentation give even maps, and handle
attachments may give odd ones.

\subsection{Independence of the decomposition}
\begin{lemma}[Projective descent]\label{lem:descent}
Suppose that the presentations of each object
are connected by elementary moves, and that
every cobordism, with any small endpoint
presentations, is represented by a word of
elementary moves. Suppose that the gauge maps
$G_e$ compose, up to sign and homotopy, to the
gauge map of the represented cobordism. Suppose $N_p$ are
isomorphisms in a homotopy
category and the elementary
maps satisfy
\eqref{eq:edge-square}. Then
$S_d$ depends only on the
represented cobordism and the
endpoint presentations, up to
sign and homotopy.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For a word $d=e_m\cdots e_1$,
compose the squares to obtain
\[
 S_dN_{p_0}\simeq
     \pm N_{p_m}G_{e_m}\cdots G_{e_1}.
\]
Every composition of homotopies
is valid by
\eqref{eq:compose-homotopy}.
If $d'$ represents the same
cobordism, gauge functoriality
identifies the two gauge words
up to sign and homotopy. Cancel
$N_{p_0}$ to identify $S_d$
and $S_{d'}$.

For words representing the
identity cobordism this also
gives the transitive system of
comparison maps between
presentations. The identity
word is the identity map, and
concatenation of words gives
composition up to sign. Hence
these systems descend to a
projective functor and the
classes of $N_p$ give a
projective natural isomorphism.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:A}]
Proposition~\ref{prop:N-isomorphism}
gives the integral mixed
isomorphisms. Equation
\eqref{eq:twohandle},
Theorem~\ref{thm:handles},
Proposition~\ref{prop:primary-naturality}
for changes of primary data within a
small ball, and the presentation
comparisons above supply all elementary
squares. Generation and
connectivity are the geometric
presentation results of
Paper~I; the presentations in these words
may be taken small, because the slices of the
small balls are convex and contain
regular data, two primary data in one
small ball lie in a common slice,
and the balls are transported by
isomorphisms (Section~\ref{subsec:admissible}).
Proposition~\ref{prop:gauge-functor}
and Lemma~\ref{lem:descent}
therefore give
\eqref{eq:main} and the
projective functoriality.

The mixed maps preserve the
chosen object gradings. On
homology, the comparison with
the homogeneous gauge map
therefore gives the stated
parity of the symplectic map.
The parity is a property of the
homology class, whatever chain
representative of a long word is
chosen.

Modulo two all determinant
orientations give the same
counts. The conversions become
the identity on the generator
basis, the signs in the tensor
and dual formulas disappear,
and the pairing
\eqref{eq:Phi-S} becomes the
paired-basis reflection
pairing. Thus the geometric
maps reduce to those of
Paper~I at the same data. At
an acyclic object the zero
representatives reduce to
maps homotopic to those of
Paper~I by
Section~\ref{subsec:zero}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Further questions}
The scope of Theorem~\ref{thm:A}, including its relation to
homology-oriented instanton maps, to the integral map of~\cite{DFL}
and to relative-spin orientations, is described in the Scope
subsection of Section~\ref{sec:introduction}. One further point
concerns the descent itself. The symplectic maps form a projective
functor, and exact composition signs would require more than
projectivizing the maps; the regrading discussed after
\eqref{eq:gauge-dictionary} indicates the additional grading
information involved.

\appendix
\section{Analytic foundations}\label{app:inputs}

This appendix proves Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}, the four estimates in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family} (Section~\ref{app:collapse-family}), Lemma~\ref{lem:bridge-gluing} (Section~\ref{app:bridge-gluing}) and Proposition~\ref{prop:weights} (Section~\ref{app:weights}). All
references to Paper~I mean its version of 7 October 2026~\cite{PaperI}. The unsigned collapse, torus excision and generation results of that
manuscript are used as they are stated there. The exceptions are the
proofs here which repeat an argument of Paper~I for a wider class of
operators, such as stabilized families of nonsolutions, and state which
steps change; the principal case is Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family}. The arguments below
combine the local theory of \cite{DFL,DFL-mixed} with explicit end
models. They are the local-plus-ends arguments of
\cite[Lemma~4.34]{DFL}, carried out on the domains of this paper.
Theorem, proposition and remark numbers in \cite{DFL-mixed} are those
of arXiv:2109.07038v1.

\subsection{Local theory and end models}\label{app:local}
\emph{Local estimates.} On a compact region meeting the matching seam,
with a product metric and the fixed almost-complex structure near the
seam, the local estimates for the mixed operator are
\cite[Theorem~4.17]{DFL}; they are uniform along $C^\infty$-convergent
sequences of configurations by \cite[Remark~4.19]{DFL}. For
domain-dependent almost-complex structures, and for the formal adjoint,
the local estimates and weak regularity are \cite[Theorem~5]{DFL-mixed},
with \cite[Remark~4.33]{DFL}; their uniformity on neighbourhoods of the
configuration and of the data is \cite[Remarks~5.73 and~5.76]{DFL-mixed}
for the operator; applied to the adjoint
$d/d\theta+D_{(B_\theta,S^*_\theta)}$, which has the same form
\cite[Section~5, before Theorem~5.9]{DFL-mixed}, they give it for the
adjoint. Away from the seam these are
the interior and boundary estimates for the anti-self-duality operator
with the normal boundary condition and for real Cauchy--Riemann
operators with totally real boundary conditions. Holonomy perturbations
contribute zeroth-order terms with the bounds of
\cite[Proposition~6.1]{DFL}. The conformal factor of
Definition~\ref{def:realization} does not change the anti-self-duality
equation; it rescales the scalar row by $f^{-2}$ and adds zeroth-order
terms to it. A compact
family in the topology of Section~\ref{sec:geometry} has coefficients
converging in every $C^k$ norm on compact sets, so these estimates are
uniform on it.

\emph{Charts.} At a smooth configuration the matching-boundary chart is
built from \cite[Lemmas~4.1, 4.2 and~4.4]{DFL} and the construction
following Lemma~4.4, with the slice of \cite[Proposition~4.6]{DFL} and
the $L^q$ radius of Paper~I, Remark~5.2, in place of the $L^2$ radius
stated in \cite[Lemma~4.1]{DFL}. The construction is local, so
on a compact family the charts vary continuously with their centres.
Charts with a radius uniform as a torus length tends to infinity are
used only at the preglued references of Paper~I, Definition~10.23 and
Lemmas~10.28--10.29.

\emph{End models.} (O) At an ordinary gauge or strip end the operator is
translation invariant with invertible asymptotic operator, the extended
Hessian at a nondegenerate irreducible critical point or $J\partial_t$
with transverse boundary conditions. Weighted estimates hold for weights
