below the gap, by \cite[Section~3.3.1]{Donaldson-book} for gauge ends
and by the same Fourier argument for strip ends. (E) At a mixed end the
stationary operator is self-adjoint with kernel the tangent space of the
manifold of stationary solutions at that end, which is the perturbed
Lagrangian of its compression body, and it has a spectral gap
\cite[Section~4.2.1 and Proposition~4.21]{DFL}. On the torus-extended
slices $Y_{\mathsf R}$, $\mathsf R\in[0,\infty]$, the gap is uniform, and on
the joined slices $D_{i,\lambda}$ it is uniform for $\lambda\ge\lambda_*$
(Paper~I, Proposition~10.7). For every $\lambda>0$ the slice
$D_{i,\lambda}$ is compact and its stationary operator is self-adjoint, by
the argument of that proposition, so its spectrum is discrete; its kernel
is the tangent space of $P_i\times Q_i$ by the same kernel computation,
which uses only flatness on the bridge and $H^*(T;\ad\alpha)=0$ and does
not involve $\lambda_*$. Hence each $D_{i,\lambda}$ has a gap, uniform on
compact ranges of $\lambda$ by continuity, as in the proof of Paper~I,
Proposition~10.17. The moving boundary domain is transported to a fixed
one by \cite[Proposition~5.27 and Lemma~5.67]{DFL-mixed}, and the weighted
estimate for weights $\delta\in(0,\gamma)$ follows by Fourier transform in
the end coordinate: the stationary operator $\mathcal A$ is self-adjoint
and its spectrum meets $(-\gamma,\gamma)$ only in $0$, so
$\|(\partial_\theta+\mathcal A-\delta)^{-1}\|\le\min(\delta,\gamma-\delta)^{-1}$
(Paper~I, Proposition~10.27). (T) On a flat torus region the operator and its
adjoint are the invertible operators of Paper~I, Proposition~10.3, with
the stated weighted bounds; at a flat face the normal derivatives are
recovered from the equation as in the proof of Paper~I, Theorem~10.5(b).

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(a)}
\begin{lemma}\label{lem:compact-remainder}
Let $\mathfrak F$ be a compact family as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}. There are a compact subset $K$ of the
cores and a constant $C$ such that, for every member and every $x$ in
the domain of its realized operator $D$ and every $\eta$ in the domain
of its weak adjoint,
\[
 \|x\|\le C\bigl(\|Dx\|+\|x\|_{L^2(K)}+|p(x)|\bigr),\qquad
 \|\eta\|\le C\bigl(\|D^*\eta\|+\|\eta\|_{L^2(K)}\bigr),
\]
where $p(x)$ denotes the stationary coordinates.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Choose cutoffs subordinate to a fixed compact core, the ordinary ends,
the mixed ends and, on torus domains, the torus region beyond a fixed
radius. Their gradients can be made small on the noncompact overlaps:
there the cutoffs vary over a length $\ell$, so their gradients are
$O(1/\ell)$, and $\ell$ is chosen after the constants of the model
estimates (Paper~I, Proposition~10.27). On each end
apply the corresponding model of Section~\ref{app:local}; the
coefficients differ from the model by terms which are small beyond a
common truncation, uniformly on $\mathfrak F$. On the core apply the
local estimates. Summing and absorbing the small commutator and
coefficient terms gives the first estimate for the decaying part; the
finite stationary columns and the scalar row on them are bounded, which
accounts for $|p(x)|$. The adjoint estimate is the same argument with
the adjoint models, whose invertibility is recorded in
Section~\ref{app:local}.

Varying geometry is handled as follows. The collar identifications
$\psi_{\mathsf R}$ of Paper~I, Definition~10.1(a), are the identity outside
the region $N_X$, which is compact in the normal direction and extends
along both mixed ends; they change the mixed-end slices from $Y$ to
$Y_{\mathsf R}$. The width identifications in the proof of Paper~I,
Proposition~10.17, are the identity outside the bridge. Pulled back by
them, the metrics form a compact family of uniformly elliptic metrics,
and on the mixed ends model (E) is applied on the slices $Y_{\mathsf R}$
and $D_{i,\lambda}$ for lengths in the given compact range. At positive
collapse scale, the domains $X_\epsilon$, $\epsilon\in[\epsilon_1,\epsilon_2]$, of
Paper~I, Definition~6.2, are built in fixed coordinates, with section
complex structure $j_\epsilon$ and seam embedding $F_\epsilon$ depending smoothly
on $\epsilon$. An isotopy of the collar embeddings $F_\epsilon$, extended to a
diffeomorphism of the section surface, identifies them with $X_{\epsilon_1}$
as decorated manifolds. At each fixed $\epsilon>0$ the collapsing region is
compact with a smooth metric (proof of Paper~I, Proposition~6.9). The zero-scale limit domains are obtained, in
the proofs of Paper~I, Theorems~9.10 and~11.8, by joining DFL-type
domains of compression pairs, with the new mixed ends of Paper~I,
Proposition~6.9, and a polygon domain along ordinary strip ends. The
joined domain has a compact core, and the argument above applies to it
directly, with model (E) at the new ends. On the completed torus domains
a compact family is in torus normal form after the gauge transformations
of Lemma~\ref{lem:split-preglue}(i), and model (T) applies beyond a fixed
radius.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(a)]
Charts are recorded in Section~\ref{app:local}. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:compact-remainder} and Rellich's theorem on $K$, each
realized operator has finite-dimensional kernel and closed range, and
so does its adjoint; hence it is Fredholm with cokernel the kernel of
the weak adjoint. Local regularity for the operator and its weak
adjoint makes these elements smooth. After transport to fixed spaces by
the boundary maps of Section~\ref{app:local} and the domain
identifications above, the coefficients vary continuously in operator
norm on the family, so the operators form continuous Fredholm families
and their determinant lines form continuous line bundles. For the
$W^{1,p}\to L^p$ realization, $p>2$, of the Cauchy--Riemann summand used
in unfolding in Section~\ref{sec:reflection}, the standard
$L^p$ theory of real Cauchy--Riemann operators with totally real
boundary conditions and nondegenerate ends gives the Fredholm property,
and elliptic regularity identifies its kernel and cokernel with those
of the Hilbert realization. The comparison of realizations is
Lemma~\ref{lem:realization}; its paths are compact families of this
kind, and its proof uses only the Fredholm and continuity statements
just proved. The affine-centre comparison is
Proposition~\ref{prop:affine-square}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(b)}
The end critical sets are finite and nondegenerate. The manifolds of
stationary solutions at the mixed ends, the perturbed Lagrangians, are
compact, and the stationary operators there have kernel exactly their
tangent spaces. Hence there is a common gap
for the finitely many end models of a compact family, and a common
small weight below all gaps and scalar thresholds; at mixed ends the
stationary coordinates are split off before the weight is imposed
\cite[Definition~3.9(ii) and Section~4.2.1]{DFL}.

\emph{The scalar operator.} The Neumann operator $d_A^*d_A$ is Fredholm by
the argument of Lemma~\ref{lem:compact-remainder}: its end models have
positive gaps by irreducibility of the end connections, and on the
torus region the fibre operator has mass $\mu_T$ because
$H^0(T;\ad\alpha)=0$. Integration by parts identifies its kernel at
weight zero with parallel sections, which vanish on an irreducible
collar; it is self-adjoint of index zero there, and sufficiently small
weights preserve the index and add no kernel. This is the argument in
the proof of Paper~I, Theorem~5.6(ii). Near the preglued references the
uniform version is the proof of Paper~I, Lemma~10.26, which is stated for
completed index-zero solutions and applies without change to the
constant pairs and to the references along the shifting rectangles.

A regular zero-dimensional solution has an invertible realized operator
by definition, and this does not depend on the realization by
Lemma~\ref{lem:realization}. Uniformity for the preglued families as the
torus lengths tend to infinity is Lemma~\ref{lem:bridge-gluing}. At
positive collapse scale the estimates are those of
Lemma~\ref{lem:compact-remainder} on compact scale intervals; as the
scale tends to zero, the uniform statement used is
Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family}. The constant reference pairs are
treated in the next lemma. For small secondary or inhomogeneous data the
operators remain invertible by openness, with the bounds of
Lemma~\ref{lem:compact-remainder}.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:constant-pairs}
The constant reference pairs, with no secondary or inhomogeneous data,
have invertible linearized operators on each of the following domains.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item Every domain of DFL type in the class of~\ref{I1}, including the
domain defining the mixed map and the truncated output regions of the triangles.
\item The separate domains $W_{a,\mathsf R}$, $\mathsf R\in[0,\infty]$, and
the joined domains $W^J_\lambda$, $\lambda>0$.
\item The closed-cut piece $M_\lambda$ of~\ref{I2}, and every domain of the
deformation from the proof of~\ref{I1} which joins it to the special
quintuple of $(D_0,D_1)$ (Paper~I, Proposition~10.21).
\end{enumerate}
The inverse bounds are uniform on compact
families of lengths, and the positive elements extend continuously
through torus completion.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\emph{The quadratic identity.} Write $d^{\mathfrak h}_A$ for the perturbed exterior
derivative of \cite[(4.42)]{DFL} for the primary perturbation $\mathfrak h$, which adds the primary Hessian term on
the compression cylinders and equals $d_A$ on the flat genus and torus
regions. For every variation $(\zeta,\nu)$ of connection and section satisfying the
linearized matching and arc conditions, with the stationary behaviour of
the realization,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:quadratic-identity}
 \int_X\tr(d^{\mathfrak h}_A\zeta\wedge d^{\mathfrak h}_A\zeta)+\int_U\Omega(d\nu,d\nu)=0 .
\end{equation}
On the original pieces this is \cite[Lemma~4.40]{DFL}: the interior terms
are total derivatives by the symmetry of the primary Hessian and the
primary equation, the matching terms cancel because the surface trace is
closed with class the section value, and the arc terms vanish on the
Lagrangian tangent spaces. A primary tangent vector satisfies the
linearized primary equation, so its pure stationary contribution at a
truncated mixed end vanishes; the terms involving the decaying remainder
tend to zero with the truncation, and the ordinary ends contribute zero.
The flat torus region contributes only Stokes terms. At a finite outer
torus face the conditions are $\zeta(\partial_r)=0$ and
$d_\alpha(\zeta|_T)=0$ (Paper~I, Definition~10.23). Write the trace as
$a+\psi\,d\theta$ with $d_\alpha a=0$; since $H^1(T;\ad\alpha)=0$ we have
$a=d_\alpha f$, and
$\int_T\tr(a\wedge\partial_\theta a)=\int_T\tr(d_\alpha f\wedge
d_\alpha\partial_\theta f)=0$ by Stokes on $T$. The cross term
$\int_T\tr(a\wedge d_T\psi)$ vanishes by Stokes on $T$ because
$d_\alpha a=0$, as in \cite[(4.52)]{DFL}. On a completed domain use spatial
cutoffs equal to one on all primary supports, whose gradients are of
size $C/R$ on the flat tail; the resulting errors tend to zero because
the tail field is in $H^1$. On a joined domain the bridge has no
boundary, and its interfaces cancel those of the two cores. On the
closed-cut piece the ordinary input $Z$ contributes nothing.

\emph{The kernel.} Let $x$ be in the kernel and let $(\zeta,\nu)$ be the
corresponding variation of connection and section. The mixed equations give $(d^{\mathfrak h}_A\zeta)^+=0$ and the
linear Cauchy--Riemann equation for $\nu$, so both integrands in
\eqref{eq:quadratic-identity} are nonnegative with the trace convention
of \cite{DFL}. Hence $d^{\mathfrak h}_A\zeta=0$ and $d\nu=0$. The section variation
decays at its strip output, so $\nu=0$ on each component. The section
limit of the stationary coordinates is their image in the tangent space
of the primary Lagrangian, and the restriction map is an embedding, so
all stationary coordinates vanish. What remains is a decaying $\zeta$
with $d^{\mathfrak h}_A\zeta=0$, $d_A^*\zeta=0$ and the normal and face conditions.

\emph{A primitive.} Construct $\eta$ with $d_A\eta=\zeta$ as in
\cite[Proposition~4.54]{DFL}. On each compression cylinder take the
temporal primitive from the gauge input; it converges because the
kernel field solves the full gauge-fixed equation on the input
cylinder, whose asymptotic operator is nondegenerate, so the field
decays exponentially there. The primary equation and Hessian symmetry
make it a primitive of the spatial part. On a flat genus product the
surface class of $\zeta$ is $\nu=0$, and the surface scalar inverse
gives the primitive; the base components agree because a parallel
adjoint scalar on an odd surface vanishes. On the flat torus region put
$\eta_T=\Delta_\alpha^{-1}d_\alpha^*\zeta_T$ fibrewise. Since
$H^1(T;\ad\alpha)=0$ this is a primitive of the fibre part, and since
$H^0(T;\ad\alpha)=0$ the base components agree. The primitives agree on
overlaps for the same reason, also across a joined bridge. The fibre
estimate $\|\eta_T\|\le\mu_T^{-1}\|\zeta_T\|$, the scalar spatial
estimates on the mixed ends and the irreducible gauge input put $\eta$
in the weighted $H^1$ space. Integration by parts with exhausting cutoffs
gives
$\|\zeta\|^2=\langle d_A\eta,\zeta\rangle=\langle\eta,d_A^*\zeta\rangle=0$;
the boundary term vanishes by the normal and face conditions. Thus the
kernel is zero.

\emph{The index.} On the special quintuple \cite[Proposition~3.19]{DFL}
gives index zero for the realization of \cite{DFL}. That realization and
the one of Definition~\ref{def:realization} use the same decaying space and
the same stationary parameter space; their stationary columns, including
the scalar ones, differ by a bounded finite-rank map, so the indices
agree (as in Paper~I, Remark~10.24); a change of
weight inside the gap does not change the index (Lemma~\ref{lem:realization}).
If the almost-complex structure near the matching line is not the one of
\cite{DFL}, a homotopy of compatible structures localized in the seam
collar keeps the constant pair a solution and the operators Fredholm, by
Lemma~\ref{lem:compact-remainder}. For a DFL-type domain in the class of
Paper~I, Lemma~9.7, and for the closed-cut piece along the family in the
proof of that lemma, the constant pair remains a solution. On each mixed
end the metric is conformal to a product, and the perturbed
anti-self-duality condition on two-forms is conformally invariant. The
flat references do not depend on the end metric, and the set of
stationary solutions at each mixed end, the perturbed-flat connections on
its compression body, does not depend on the metric or the almost-complex structure (proof
of Paper~I, Lemma~9.7). The section is constant. The index is
therefore constant along the family. For $W_{a,\mathsf R}$ with finite $\mathsf R$ the index is
constant along the collar-length family (Paper~I, Theorem~10.13(i)). By
Lemma~\ref{lem:split-preglue}(iii), which does not use the present
lemma, $P_{\mathsf R}(C_{a,x})$ is the constant pair on
$W_{a,\mathsf R}$ and $P_\lambda(C_{-,x_-},C_{+,x_+})=C^{(\lambda)}_{J,x}$.
Hence Lemma~\ref{lem:bridge-gluing}(ii) gives $\ind D_{\mathsf R}=\ind D_\infty=0$
for large $\mathsf R$, and the index at $C^{(\lambda)}_{J,x}$ is the value
$0$ at $\lambda=\infty$ for large $\lambda$; it is constant in
$\lambda\in(0,\infty)$ by Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(a). In all
cases the kernel is zero and the index is zero, so the operator is
invertible.

\emph{Uniformity.} On a compact family of finite lengths the inverses
vary continuously. Through torus completion the positive elements are
continuous by Lemma~\ref{lem:bridge-gluing}(iii), and uniform inverse
bounds for large lengths follow from its part (i), with $E=0$ since the
limiting operators are onto.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(c)}

\emph{Compactness and gluing of pure moduli spaces.} The compression-body
Lagrangians are simply connected with $\pi_2=0$, and $\pi_2(\Msf_h)\cong\ZZ$
is generated by a class of Maslov index four; in the product target the
correspondence $L_C$ is simply connected (Paper~I, Section~11.3) and has
$\pi_2$ generated by spheres of zero area and zero Chern number
(Section~\ref{sec:characters}). The pairs and correspondences are
therefore monotone with minimal Maslov number at least three: for the
compression-body Lagrangians this is \cite[Proposition~2.27]{DFL}, and for
the correspondence $L_C$ it is Paper~I, Section~11.3. Sphere and disc bubbling has
codimension at least two in the dimensions used. The compactification
and boundary structure of the one-dimensional pure continuation, cap and
strip moduli spaces are then as in \cite[Theorem~3.9(c)]{WW-relative}
for the continuation and cap spaces and as in
\cite[Theorem~4.2.3(c)]{WW-quilt} for the Floer strips.
Transversality there is obtained from Hamiltonian perturbations. In the
perturbation class used here, with zero Hamiltonian and
domain-dependent almost-complex structures, it is Paper~I,
Lemma~11.3, for the strips and triangles from which the cup and cap
maps are built, and Proposition~\ref{prop:end-data}(ii) for
continuation strips. The ordinary Heegaard strips are regular by the
definition of the symplectic complex (Paper~I, Section~3.4). Strip gluing
is \cite[Theorems~5.4.1, 5.4.3, 5.4.5 and~5.4.8]{Simcevic},
with the compactness theorem \cite[Theorem~3.3]{MWW} and the
boundary description of \cite[Theorem~3.7]{MWW}, whose gluing step is
\cite{Mau}, for the quilted disc families, and exponential decay on half-strips is
\cite[Corollary~2.3.2]{Simcevic}. For a finite collection of regular
configurations with nondegenerate strip ends, every nearby
zero-dimensional solution at a long neck is the unique correction of a
preglued configuration, the correction tends to zero with the neck
length, and the estimates are uniform over compact families. Along the
path from the preglue to the solution the operators are invertible, by
the uniform right inverse at the preglue and the smallness of the
correction, as in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:gluing-path}; hence
determinant transport preserves the canonical orientation. The unsigned
low-dimensional counts agree with those of Paper~I, Section~11.

\emph{Interior nodes.} Linear gluing of Cauchy--Riemann determinant lines
along strip ends is Lemma~\ref{lem:line-gluing}. For a family of real
Cauchy--Riemann operators on surfaces degenerating at an interior node,
the determinant lines form a topological line bundle
\cite[Proposition~2.4.12]{WW-orientations}, and the line isomorphism is
given by the exact sequence (18) of \cite[Theorem~2.4.5]{WW-orientations}
and \cite[Corollary~2.4.7]{WW-orientations}; it is associative
\cite[Proposition~2.4.8]{WW-orientations}. The sequence is proved there
for a boundary node, and the interior case is stated there to be
similar. In (18) the fibre at the node is a summand of the third term;
at an interior node it is the fibre $E_z$ of the bundle, a complex
vector space (compare \cite[Remark~2.4.6]{WW-orientations}), which is
even and canonically oriented, so the node contributes no additional
sign. Numbering is as in arXiv:1503.07803v6. These statements
localize, by the following stabilization argument. Choose stabilizations of a
mixed or pure operator supported away from a disc containing the node and
separated from the matching and boundary collars. Then the stabilized
kernel near the node is obtained by the local gluing map of the cited
proposition, and the rest of the operator is unchanged. Hence the line
bundle structure and the gluing isomorphism hold for the real
Cauchy--Riemann part of any such operator. Associativity follows from the cited
associativity, with disjoint supports.
Commutation with ordinary strip gluing follows because the two
stabilizations and cutoffs have disjoint supports, as in the proof of
Lemma~\ref{lem:bridge-gluing}(iv). In the collapse application the positive plumbing parameter is
fixed first, and the endpoint insertion lies in an external strip.

\subsection{Proof of the estimates of Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family}}\label{app:collapse-family}
We use the notation of the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family}. Paper~I, Sections~7.1--7.7, proves its linear estimates for the
linearization at a regular index-zero limiting solution, whose operator
is invertible. Here $\{V_b\}$ is an arbitrary compact family of limiting
configurations, and $D_{V_b}$ may have kernel and cokernel. After the
stabilization of that proof we repeat the arguments of Paper~I, not only cite their
conclusions, for the stabilized operators. Four steps change. The
exterior inverse of Paper~I, Proposition~7.13, is proved for the
augmented operator $(D_{V_b},j_b,\Gamma_b)$. The common exponential rate
$\beta$ of the family replaces the decay of solutions. The attachment
inverse of~\ref{I8} (Paper~I, Proposition~7.15) is proved with the
exterior operator
augmented by $F$ and $K_b$. The kernel-patching estimate, proved in
Paper~I for the model strip (Proposition~7.8), is transplanted to the
glued domain. The remaining steps use only bounds on the coefficients of
the linearized operator, not the equation, and apply unchanged.

\emph{Proof of (i) and (ii).} The linear estimates of
Paper~I, Section~7, apply here before assuming that $V_b$ solves
an equation. On the collapsed strip their inputs are a smooth
path of flat compression connections and a smooth section with
the prescribed boundary values. The Hodge, trace and energy
identities use these conditions and bounded derivatives of their
coefficients. The zero-order term of the section linearization
is retained. On the unchanged pieces the operator is the full
$D_{V_b}$. The compactness of $B$ bounds all these coefficients.
The end and transition estimates use only the fixed end operators,
the separated stationary coordinates, and the common gaps. Choose
the auxiliary exterior weight $\delta_*<\min(\beta,\gamma/2)$, where
$\gamma$ is the transition gap of Paper~I, Proposition~7.9, as in
Paper~I, Proposition~7.13. Paper~I's exterior
truncation gives the stationary-column error
\[
 O(\epsilon^{1/4})+
 O(\epsilon^{(\beta-\delta_*)/4}),
\]
uniformly over $B$. Its exponentially convergent coefficient
estimates and their weak-adjoint counterparts therefore apply to
the original configurations. Exact constant tails are not required.
Put $\mathsf A=1+|\log\epsilon|$. First work on the auxiliary
exterior of Paper~I, Section~7.7,
with its free stationary limits and fixed positive end weight.
On this exterior the localized estimate holds with a compact
remainder on the unchanged pieces and the limiting section.
It also holds for the adjoint. If the augmented exterior estimate
failed, normalized fields with image tending to zero would have
a nonzero limiting field in $\ker(D_{V_b},j_b,\Gamma_b)$ after
passing to a subsequence of $b$. The compression Hodge estimate
eliminates the fast complementary component, and harmonic matching
identifies the remaining component with the limiting section.
Compact convergence and the finite stationary and $F$ tests detect
the limiting field. The adjoint argument gives a field in
$\ker(D_{V_b},j_b,\Gamma_b)^*$. Both are impossible. Thus the
augmented exterior inverse is uniformly bounded. Its finite
columns and projections are supported off the new necks. Before
attachment change the exterior weight from the fixed gap weight
to $\delta_L\asymp\mathsf A^{-1}$, using the small-weight exterior
argument of Paper~I. The stationary part is integrated explicitly;
the normal part retains its fixed spectral gap. The finite compact
columns and tests are unaffected. This gives the same polynomial
loss at the small weight and avoids exponential weight costs for
sources whose supports move out along an overlap.

\emph{Proof of (iii).} The preglued domain itself requires an additional attachment argument.
Remove the sources in the middle
of each overlap using the stationary line inverse of Paper~I,
the transition inverse, with its small weight of order $\mathsf A^{-1}$.
This costs $O(\mathsf A)$; cutoff derivatives of order
$\mathsf A^{-1}$ leave bounded sources. Solve those sources on
the augmented exterior and the transitions, setting each
transition's stationary value equal to the corresponding free
exterior value. The two stationary values are independent.
Patch in the source-free bands. The normal components decay
exponentially across a band of length comparable to
$|\log\epsilon|$, so the patching error is bounded by
$C_B\mathsf A^N e^{-c|\log\epsilon|}$ for a fixed finite $N$.
A Neumann series gives a right inverse. Cutting a homogeneous
field in the same bands and applying the component inverses
proves injectivity. The stabilization columns and projections
occur only in the exterior equation and are unchanged by these
operations. Consequently
\[
 \|A_{\epsilon,b}^{-1}\|\le C_B\mathsf A^N.
\]
This is the source-removal proof of the attachment inverse in
Paper~I, with the exterior operator augmented by $F$ and $K_b$.
A bound independent of $\epsilon$ on the whole domain would be
false: stationary fields supported on a long new neck have norm
ratio of order $|\log\epsilon|$. The polynomial loss suffices
below.

\emph{Proof of (iv).} For $k\in K_b$, cutoff differentiation and the horizontal
velocity of the harmonic lift give
\[
 \|A_{\epsilon,b}J_{\epsilon,b}k-(0,k)\|
 \le C_B\epsilon^\sigma(1+|\log\epsilon|)^N\|k\|,
\]
for some $\sigma>0$ and finite $N$. These are the endpoint and
patching estimates of Paper~I, Sections~7.4--7.7, namely the kernel
estimate of Proposition~7.8 and the spatial estimate of
Proposition~7.9. The fast lift has weighted size
$O(\sqrt{\mathfrak s})$, where $\mathfrak s$ is the local compression
scale ($\mathfrak s=\epsilon/r$ on the middle compression in the
conformal representative of Paper~I, Section~7.7, and
$\mathfrak s=\epsilon e^s$ in the endpoint neck coordinate $s$). The
kernel field is stationary on the truncation region, so the lift
contributes only where $\mathfrak s\le\epsilon^{3/4}$. The stationary factors
are unchanged, and the fixed compact family has uniformly bounded
coefficients. Take $\sigma$ no larger than the positive exponents in
these endpoint errors. The truncation estimates are uniform on the
compact exponentially convergent family.

\emph{Choices, lifting actions and gluing.} Two choices of stabilization are compared
by their direct sum. The exact kernel sequence for adding the
extra columns identifies the two cancellation maps. Cutoffs and
compact pairings can be interpolated; the same estimates on the
compact interpolation give a homotopy of the resulting maps.
Lifting actions preserve the operators and the harmonic lift,
so applying a lift to this construction gives the same determinant
map after stabilization independence.

For ordinary end gluing choose the stabilizations off both transition
regions. If $T$ is the ordinary gluing length, the constants of the
cofinal regime in Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family} are chosen so that
$e^{-cT}\mathsf A^{N'}\to0$ for each of the finitely many polynomial
losses occurring in the construction; $T\ge C\log\mathsf A+T_0$ with
$C,T_0$ large suffices. Correcting the common patched kernel at once, or first at
one neck and then at the other, now gives maps uniformly close
to that patched map. Their straight interpolation on the
finite-dimensional kernels is invertible. The threshold for $\epsilon$
may be taken uniform in $T\ge T_0$. Indeed the stabilizations avoid the
neck. In the contradiction argument for the augmented exterior
estimate a normalized sequence with $T_i\to\infty$ retains mass on the
compact core of one of the two pieces, by the compact-remainder estimate
and the gap on the neck; there it converges to a nonzero field annihilated
by the augmented operator of that piece, which is an isomorphism. Thus
the cofinal regime meets the range of the construction. Any two sufficiently
large gluing choices connect through this cofinal regime. The
ordinary gluing determinant maps are unchanged along that
connection by continuity. We obtain the asserted compatibility,
including its graded permutations.

\subsection{Fredholm property of the family of Proposition~\ref{prop:affine-square}}\label{app:affine-fredholm}
We use the notation of the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:affine-square}
and prove the estimates behind its Step~2.

For Fredholmness, first work on the weighted spaces of decaying fields. The
local primal estimates are \cite[Theorem~4.17 and Remark~4.19]{DFL};
the weak-adjoint estimates are \cite[Remark~4.33]{DFL} and
\cite[Theorem~5]{DFL-mixed}. The matching and Lagrangian boundary conditions depend on the
configuration; the maps of \cite[Proposition~5.27 and Lemma~5.67]{DFL-mixed}
transport them to fixed boundary conditions, and their differences from
the identity are bounded by the distance between the configurations.
The changed compact core is smooth at this fixed scale. At a
mixed stationary limit $B$, the translation-invariant end operator with
slice centre $B$ has the form
$\partial_\theta-\mathcal A_B$, with self-adjoint spatial
operator and a positive normal gap $\gamma$. Its weighted
whole-cylinder inverse satisfies
\[
 M_\delta\leq\frac1{\min(\delta,\gamma-\delta)},
 \qquad 0<\delta<\gamma,
\]
as well as the spatial first-order estimate. Stationary
coordinates are adjoined separately. Changing the scalar
centre to $C$ adds a zeroth-order row of norm at most
$c\|C-B\|_{C^0}$. If $cM_\delta\|C-B\|_{C^0}<1/2$, a Neumann
series supplies the affine cylinder inverse and the inverse
of its adjoint, without a self-adjoint spectral decomposition of the
affine operator. The true end coefficients
and matching domains converge to these models, so their errors
are absorbed after a common far cutoff. Ordinary end models
are unchanged. Compact-core centre changes are relatively
compact by Rellich. Hence, after weight conjugation,
\[
 \|v\|_{H^1}\leq C(\|D^{\rm dec}_{s,t}v\|_2+\|v\|_{L^2(K)}),
 \quad
 \|y\|_{H^1}\leq C(\|(D^{\rm dec}_{s,t})^* y\|_2
                                      +\|y\|_{L^2(K)}).
\]
Adding the finite stationary columns preserves Fredholmness.
Higher estimates and smooth finite stabilizations identify the
resulting determinant lines with the strong realizations.

For clarity, the adjoint here is taken after weight conjugation.
A zeroth-order change of slice centre leaves the principal Green
form and its matching annihilator unchanged. Write $K_{s,t}(p)=D_{s,t}(0,p)$ for the full target column
corresponding to the source lift $H_{s,t}$. A cokernel element
satisfies both $(D^{\rm dec}_{s,t})^*y=0$ and
$K_{s,t}^*y=0$. A target map $S$
pulls the weak domain back by $S^*$; it does not leave the old
adjoint boundary label fixed. The source chart, which includes
a boundary trace extension, need not extend to a bounded map
on bulk $L^2$. We use it as a bounded isomorphism of Sobolev
domains, which preserves the cokernel annihilator. Equivalently,
complete its decaying source in the pulled-back norm
$\|J_s^{\rm dec}w\|_{L^2}$ before forming a coordinate adjoint.
Keep the finite stationary space as a separate summand.

\subsection{Boundary Hodge theory and holonomy regularity}
For a flat orthogonal bundle on
a compact compression body,
the input needed in
Proposition~\ref{prop:small-ball}
is the Hodge decomposition on
$W^{1,2}$ with normal boundary
condition. Its harmonic fields
are smooth and finite
dimensional, and its exact
part is the covariant
differential of $W^{2,2}$
sections. Since
$H^0(C;\ad)=0$, the Neumann
Green operator $G_N$ for
$d_B^*d_B$ exists and
preserves smoothness, so, on forms $b$ with
$*b|_{\partial C}=0$, the map
$1-d_BG_Nd_B^*$ is a
smoothness-preserving
projection onto the coclosed
forms with $*b|_{\partial C}=0$;
the homogeneous Neumann
condition leaves the normal
component unchanged.
The decomposition is the normal
form of the flat covariant de Rham
case of
\cite[Section~1.6 and
Theorems~3.10--3.11]{KL}, and the
Neumann Green operator follows
from \cite[Theorem~3.10]{KL} in
degree zero together with
$H^0(C;\ad)=0$.

The boundary elliptic estimate
has the form
\[
 \|b\|_{W^{1,p}}\le C
 (\|d_Bb\|_{L^p}+\|d_B^*b\|_{L^p}
                              +\|b\|_{L^p})
\]
for $*b|_{\partial C}=0$.
For smooth fixed $B$, adjoint
bundle coefficients add only
bounded zeroth-order terms in
local trivializations. This
estimate is Theorem~D(a) of the
introductory statements in
\cite{Wehrheim-book}, and the
compactness and Coulomb-slice
inputs are Theorems~A and~F
there.

Finite holonomy functions have
bounded gradients and
Lipschitz gradient differences
as used in
\eqref{eq:primary-norm}
\cite[Proposition~6.1]{DFL}.
Their higher derivatives obey
the local tame estimates
obtained by differentiating
parallel transport. Combined
with the boundary elliptic
estimate, these give the
bootstrap from the slice
fixed point to the smooth
perturbed-flat solution.
For holonomy families with
moving basepoints, restriction
and composition are performed
in the smooth topology.

\subsection{Shifting and self-gluing}
Relative preparation and shifting are proved in
Appendix~\ref{app:shifting}. Naturality under isomorphisms of the
decorated data is automatic: the pullback of a shift is a shift, and the
orientation maps are independent of the shifting path
(Proposition~\ref{prop:transport}).

For ordinary gauge self-gluing,
Proposition~\ref{prop:selfgluing} applies the low-dimensional
instanton compactification, verifies matching on the prescribed
bundle and identifies the lifting fibre. Its orientation comparison
first traces over the full determinant-one torus complex and then
takes the larger quotient. Together with the finite group calculation
in Section~\ref{subsec:lifting-mult}, this gives integral unit multiplicity.

\subsection{Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:bridge-gluing}}\label{app:bridge-gluing}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:bridge-gluing}]
Once a uniformly bounded right inverse is available, the construction is
the standard gluing of determinant lines along a long neck, as in the proof
of Lemma~\ref{lem:line-gluing} and in \cite[proof of Proposition~3.9 and
Proposition~5.11]{Donaldson-book}. Two points are specific to the torus
bridge. The uniform estimates must be proved directly, because the
cross-section of the bridge runs into the mixed ends. The stationary
columns at finite and at infinite width differ on the whole mixed ends,
not only on the tails.

\emph{Uniform estimates.} The uniform inverse of Paper~I,
Proposition~10.27, is obtained by patching model
estimates. On the torus region beyond a fixed $S$ the operator is the flat
odd-torus operator $D_\alpha$ of Paper~I, Proposition~10.3, plus a small
zeroth-order term, and the normal derivatives at a flat face are recovered
as in the proof of Paper~I, Theorem~10.5(b). On the mixed ends the operator
is transported to the stationary models by the maps of
\cite[Proposition~5.27]{DFL-mixed}, with errors tending to zero, and the
slice estimates of Paper~I, Proposition~10.7 and Corollary~10.4, hold. The
ordinary asymptotic operators are invertible, and local elliptic estimates
hold on a fixed compact region. The patching also uses that the
coefficients are bounded in $C^m$ on unit patches of finitely many isometry
types, and that the weight satisfies $\delta<\gamma/2$ and $\delta<\mu_T$,
with the weight function equal to $\tau_0(\theta)$ on the torus region (Paper~I, Definition~10.1). For the operators
$D_\lambda(c)$ these inputs hold uniformly on
$\mathfrak F\times[2r_0+2,\infty]$. The smallness beyond $S$ follows from
the uniform tails in the definition of torus normal form and Sobolev
embedding, and the other inputs follow from the local estimates and end
models of Section~\ref{app:local} and the compactness of $\mathfrak F$. The
finite stationary columns and the scalar row of the realization are
bounded, and they act through the fixed cores and the mixed ends. The same
patching therefore gives
\[
 \|x\|\le C\bigl(\|D_\lambda(c)x\|+\|x\|_{L^2(K_1)}+|p(x)|\bigr),
\]
where $K_1$ is a fixed compact subset of the two cores and $p(x)$ the
stationary coordinates. The adjoint of $D_\alpha$ is invertible with the
same bound, the stationary models are self-adjoint, and the local
estimates for the weak adjoint are recorded in
Section~\ref{app:local}. Hence the same patching gives
\[
 \|\eta\|\le C\bigl(\|D_\lambda(c)^*\eta\|+\|\eta\|_{L^2(K_1)}\bigr)
\]
on the dual weighted spaces.

(i) At $\lambda=\infty$ each $D_\infty(c)$ is Fredholm by
Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(a). Smooth compactly
supported target sections are dense, so finitely many of them span a
complement of its range, and by continuity also near $c$. A finite
cover of $\mathfrak F$ gives $\Lambda$, with sections supported in a
compact set $K_\Lambda$; for $\lambda\ge\lambda_1$ this set lies in the part
of $W^J_\lambda$ identified with the completed pair, so $\Lambda$ is defined
there. If every $D_\infty(c)$ is onto,
no sections are needed and $E=0$. Suppose there were $\lambda_j\to\infty$,
$c_j\in\mathfrak F$ and $\eta_j$ with $\|\eta_j\|=1$ and
$\|(D_{\lambda_j}(c_j)\oplus\Lambda)^*\eta_j\|\to0$. By the adjoint estimate,
$\|\eta_j\|_{L^2(K_1)}\ge1/C$. Pass to $c_j\to c$. The parts of $W^J_{\lambda_j}$ within
distance $\lambda_j/2$ of each core are isometric to the corresponding
parts of $W_{\pm,\infty}$. Local elliptic bounds give a subsequence
converging weakly on compact sets and strongly in $L^2(K_1)$ to some
$\eta_\infty$ on the completed pair. The stationary columns converge by the
estimate for $(C_\lambda-C_\infty)p$ in (ii) below, so
$C_{\lambda_j}^*\eta_j\to C_\infty^*\eta_\infty$. Then
$(D_\infty(c)\oplus\Lambda)^*\eta_\infty=0$ and
$\eta_\infty\neq0$, which contradicts the choice of $\Lambda$. Hence
$\|\eta\|\le C\|(D_\lambda(c)\oplus\Lambda)^*\eta\|$ for
$\lambda\ge\lambda_1$. Then $Q_\lambda=T^*(TT^*)^{-1}$, with
$T=D_\lambda(c)\oplus\Lambda$ and $T^*$ its Hilbert adjoint for the weighted
inner products, is a uniformly bounded right inverse. The estimate for the
formal adjoint on the dual weighted space is equivalent to one for $T^*$
through the Riesz isometry; it is proved first at the $H^1$ level and then
upgraded by the local elliptic estimates on unit patches. The map $Q_\lambda$ is
continuous in $(c,\lambda)$ for finite $\lambda$.

(ii) At $\lambda=\infty$ the kernels form a vector bundle over
$\mathfrak F$, since the operators are onto. The frames $\iota_\lambda$ are
the transplanted kernels of \cite[Step~3 of the proof of
Proposition~3.9]{Donaldson-book}, with $Q_\lambda$ in place of the patched
inverse there. They are injective for large $\lambda$ and converge to the
identity once $\|(D_\lambda\oplus\Lambda)\beta_\lambda v\|\to0$ uniformly for
unit $v$, where $\beta_\lambda$ acts on the decaying part and leaves the
stationary and $E$-components unchanged. The unit sphere bundle of the
limiting kernels is compact, so the tails of its elements on $r\ge r_1$
are uniformly small as $r_1\to\infty$. The error consists of
$\sigma(d\chi_\lambda)v$ and of the coefficient difference on the tail,
both supported where $r\ge\lambda/2-1$, and of the difference
$(C_\lambda-C_\infty)p$ of the stationary columns, read through the
identification of (i). The last term is not localized on the tail, but it
tends to zero uniformly for $|p|\le1$. The spatially Coulomb stationary
representatives on the joined slices $D_{i,\lambda}$ and on the separate
slices $Y_{\pm,\infty}$ differ by the gradients of their scalar Neumann
corrections, whose sources coincide and are supported away from the torus
neck. On the neck the scalar Laplacian is bounded below by $\mu_T^2$, the
square of the fibre gap, so the two corrections differ by
$O(e^{-\mu\lambda})$ in every norm, for some $\mu>0$, uniformly in the
stationary limits, as in the proof of Paper~I, Proposition~10.7. The frames
are also onto: otherwise unit kernel elements orthogonal to their image
would, by the estimate for $D_\lambda$ and the argument in (i), converge to
a nonzero kernel element at $\lambda=\infty$ orthogonal to the whole
limiting kernel. For finite $\lambda$ the kernels of the surjective family
form a bundle as usual.

(iii) This is the stabilization construction of \cite[Step~4 of the proof
of Proposition~3.9, and Proposition~5.11]{Donaldson-book}, as in the proof
of Lemma~\ref{lem:line-gluing}. The exact sequence
$0\to\ker D\to\ker(D\oplus\Lambda)\to E\to\operatorname{coker}D\to0$
identifies $\det D$ with $\det\ker(D\oplus\Lambda)\otimes(\det E)^*$,
and (ii) makes these lines continuous. At $\lambda=\infty$ the ordered
direct-sum isomorphism gives $\det D_{c_-}\otimes\det D_{c_+}$. Two choices
$\Lambda,\Lambda'$ are compared through $\Lambda\oplus\Lambda'$ by the
standard stabilization maps, which are continuous in $(c,\lambda)$ because
they are built from the same kernel bundles. On an invertible operator the
stabilization identification carries the canonical element to the
canonical element, so the positive elements are continuous where the
operators are invertible.

(iv) The sections of $\Lambda$ are supported in $K_\Lambda$, which lies at
a fixed distance from the ordinary gluing neck and from the torus bridge
once both are long. The glued operators then form a family over the
product of the two closed neck-length intervals, including their limits.
The kernel transplants of (ii) and of Lemma~\ref{lem:line-gluing} act by
cutoffs and projections in disjoint regions, and the estimates of (i) hold
uniformly on this product. The product of the two intervals is
contractible, so the two orders of gluing give the same identification.

For one side use the half-plane and strip models of Paper~I,
Proposition~10.3, with the flat-fibre face, in place of the bridge.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:weights}}\label{app:weights}
We use the notation of Section~\ref{subsec:weights}: the autonomous end
operators $\partial_s-\mathcal A_e$ in outward coordinates, the gaps
$\gamma_e,\gamma_e^{\rm opp}$ and the weights $r_e<\gamma_e$, and $P$ for the finite summand of mixed-end stationary coordinates.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:weights}]
All comparisons use closed intervals strictly inside the gaps.
At spatially completed torus ends no extra spatial weight is
introduced. The longitudinal weights are constant on surface
fibres, have zero normal derivative at the boundary faces of the
domain and agree on matching traces. Write each longitudinal exponent
as $r_eW_e$. Choose $|dW_e|\leq1$, with cutoff derivatives supported
in disjoint longitudinal transitions. The complete stationary
mixed operator is self-adjoint on the domain given by its boundary
conditions, by Green's formula. On a completed compression, its
compact-remainder estimate and the acyclic torus model give a normal
gap, without compactness of the resolvent. The following argument
uses its spectral projections.

Solutions decay exponentially at some positive rate along every end,
by the mixed-end estimates of Paper~I and \cite[Section~5.3]{DFL}.
To raise the rate, choose a far end and put the solution in the small
Coulomb slice of the stationary connection, with a Dirichlet condition
on an artificial initial slice and the Neumann condition of the
problem at the boundary faces. The small-weight scalar inverse
provides this gauge and preserves the chosen stationary limit of the
gauge transformation. Subtract the exact stationary primary limit and
use its translation-invariant matching chart. The equation becomes
\[
 (\partial_s-\mathcal A_e)v=f(v),\qquad f(0)=df(0)=0,
 \qquad v(s)\longrightarrow0,
\]
and the chart and holonomy estimates bound $f(v)$ quadratically in
$v$ on nested unit windows, with a fixed finite loss of derivatives.
For such an equation the standard bootstrap, by variation of constants
in the spectral decomposition of $\mathcal A_e$ and local elliptic
estimates on nested windows, raises the decay rate to every rate below
the spectral gap; compare \cite[Section~4.1]{Donaldson-book} for
nondegenerate gauge ends. It gives every rate $b<\gamma_e$ with all
derivatives, and uniform bounds on compact families with common
tail-entry windows and spectral margins. Two points are specific to
mixed ends. The component in the stationary kernel is integrated from
infinity and uses the prescribed limiting primary coordinate. The
component in the positive spectral subspace is also integrated from
infinity, so the rate is not restricted by the opposite gap
$\gamma_e^{\rm opp}$. A rate $b>r_e$ gives membership in the desired
weighted space.

Gauge transformations between these representatives have their
prescribed stationary limit $g_\infty$. The temporal comparison
equation $\partial_s g=g a_s-a'_s g$, integrated from infinity, gives
the same rates for $g-g_\infty$. The spatial comparison equation
and differentiation give all higher derivatives, after subtracting
the stationary intertwining relation. The lifting classes and
central transition data are unchanged. This concerns gauge
transformations between the representatives controlled above, not
arbitrary gauge transformations applied to a stationary solution.

Parameter derivatives require differentiating the stationary
subtraction. In a family the identity $\mathcal F_b(0,q)=0$
cancels the nondecaying parameter column. Its remainder and every
finite stationary column then have all the above decay rates.
The linearized equation improves the first parameter derivative;
induction improves higher ones. Polynomial factors caused by
parameter differentiation are absorbed in a strict rate margin.
A common data parameter contributes only its one original column.

Write the full realized operator as
\[
 D_r:X_r^{\rm dec}\oplus P\longrightarrow Y_r,
 \qquad D_r(w,p)=D^{\rm dec}w+C p.
\]
On an end, weight conjugation gives
$\partial_s-\mathcal A_e-r_e$. Its inverse and adjoint inverse
are uniformly bounded on a closed weight interval by the reciprocal
spectral distance. Small coefficient and domain-transport errors
are absorbed beyond a common cutoff. On an infinite spatial torus
region the unweighted flat operator has mass $\mu_T>0$. The
conjugation term has norm at most the largest relevant mixed-end
exponent $r_T$; its inverse bound is at most
$(\mu_T-r_T)^{-1}$. The permitted completed spatial gap is bounded
by this essential-spectrum threshold. The original closed incoming
weights do not vary on that added torus region. Compact-core
estimates for the operator and for its weak adjoint give
Fredholmness over the entire weight rectangle. Smooth stationary and
parameter columns remain in every target. If a scalar inverse at a
larger weight is needed, scalar Fredholmness throughout its indicial
gap, index zero from the small weight and the positive kernel energy
identity give it. Scalar gauge modes occur in the full end operator,
so the full permissible gap is no larger than that scalar threshold.
This avoids extending the sufficient small-weight coercivity bound
beyond its range.

For $r_-\leq r_+$ the inclusions of the weighted spaces give a
commuting square of source and target inclusions, with identity
on $P$. A kernel element is a stationary column plus a remainder.
The coefficient errors and the images of stationary columns have
every permitted rate. The same spectral formulas improve the
remainder to every weight. This applies also to kernels with
compactly supported stabilization columns and to kernels of
parametrized operators.

For the cokernel use distributions paired in the unweighted
volume form of the domain. A weak cokernel element at $r_+$ lies at
dual weight $-r_+$. Window estimates initially bound its growth by
$e^{r_+s}$. Choose $\beta$ with $r_+<\beta<\gamma_e$; multiplying
by the coefficient error gives a decaying forcing of rate
$\beta-r_+$. The adjoint spectral formula excludes growing
normal modes, whose rates are at least $\gamma_e$. Its zero
mode has a finite limit. The resulting bounded field improves
its forcing again, and its remainder has every rate below
$\min(\gamma_e,\gamma_e^{\rm opp})$. It therefore belongs to
the dual space at $-r_-$ as well. At an ordinary end there is
no zero mode, so $r_-=0$ is included. Retain the conditions
$C^*y=0$ and, in a parametrized problem, the parameter-column
annihilator. These are the same convergent pairings
at every weight. Hence the kernels and cokernel duals, and
therefore the finite-dimensional cokernel quotients, are canonically
identified. For unordered weight vectors insert their componentwise
minimum and maximum.

Choose finitely many smooth compactly supported target columns
$\rho:V\to Y$ spanning the cokernel on a compact parameter patch.
They work at every weight. With $E_r=\ker(D_r+\rho)$, the identity
map of fields identifies
\[
 \det D_r=\det E_r\otimes(\det V)^*.
\]
Enlarging the stabilization gives the same map by its exact
sequence. These identifications are continuous, transitive and
preserve canonical orientations at invertible operators. They
commute with the gauge transformations above and the shared parameter
factor. The weight rectangle is contractible, so its transport
has no additional choice of path or component sign.

The reference-orientation paths can initially have slower tails.
Prepare them by cutting off their decaying coordinates on a common
far end, retaining the exact stationary coordinates and carrying the
transition data of the bundle through the preparation transport. The
preparation homotopy is made at the initial small weight. A
stationary endpoint is fixed; at a solution the endpoint segment has
all permitted decay rates by the preceding argument. Thus the prepared
path and its endpoint segments admit the full weight rectangle, even
when the original path does not. Determinant transport on this
rectangle and on the preparation homotopy proves compatibility with
the original reference normalization. Affine-centre comparisons are
made first at their common small weight, as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:affine-square}. The comparison of solutions then
uses the full gap of this proposition; the spectrum of the original
end operator is not assigned to an affine operator.

Finally consider a sequence in an already established small-weight
compactification. Fix translations on its retained components.
Energy exhaustion gives common tail-entry windows. The estimates
above, with $r<b<\gamma_e$, bound a tail beyond $R$ by a constant
times $e^{-(b-r)R}$ in the desired weighted norm. Compact convergence
therefore improves to convergence in that weight. At ordinary
breaks this is a componentwise statement in the gluing charts;
at spatial completion it retains the existing strong spatial
tails. The small-weight forward gluing produces the same
solutions, and the converse and uniqueness give the same local
branches in larger weights. Common compactly supported stabilizations
make the gluing exact sequences commute with the inclusions of the
weighted spaces. Cutoffs, evaluation maps, stationary factors, and
conversion lines are the same ordered geometric maps. Thus all
boundary signs, counts and homotopies are unchanged.
\end{proof}

\section{Relative preparation and shifting}\label{app:shifting}

This appendix proves Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting}. The one-input
statement is a version of \cite[Lemma~4.62]{DFL} and of its family
form \cite[Lemma~4.64]{DFL}, whose proof is sketched there; we give it in the topology of
Section~\ref{sec:geometry}, jointly continuous in configurations and
data, relative to the terminal subspace defined below, and on the domains of this
paper. Throughout, a family is a continuous map from a compact metrizable
space $K$ to configurations on a fixed bundle, that is, to connections
and sections rather than gauge equivalence classes, together with
continuously varying data. No quotient by gauge transformations is
taken; the lifting groups enter only through
Proposition~\ref{prop:transport}.

\emph{The terminal subspace.} For an output label $x$, a
\emph{representative} of the critical point over $x$ is a connection
$c=B\cup\mathrm{pr}^*b\cup B'$ on $Y_\#$, where $b$ is a flat connection
on $\Sigma$ over the point of $\mathcal M(\Sigma)$ determined by $x$ and
$B,B'$ are primary solutions on $Y_0,Y_0'$ which are products with value
$b$ on the collars $[-5/2,-2]\times\Sigma$ of their boundaries fixed in
Section~\ref{app:shift-prep}. Every such $c$ is a critical
point of the perturbed Chern--Simons functional in the gauge orbit of
$a_x$ or of its other lift, and the constant pair $C^c$ built from it is
equivalent to $C_x$ under the lifting group, by a gauge transformation
when $c$ lies in the orbit of $a_x$. Its operator is therefore invertible, its
positive element corresponds to that of $C_x$ under conjugation, and the
almost-complex gauge orientations are invariant under the bundle
automorphism. The lines at $c$ are identified with those at $a_x$ by this
automorphism. The identification agrees with transport along any path of
connections from $a_x$ to $c$: the almost-complex orientation of the
gauge line is invariant under the full group of bundle automorphisms,
of every degree and lifting class (Section~\ref{sec:gauge},
\cite[Section~5.1]{KM-unknot}), and the sign of the conversion line depends only on the lifting class
(Proposition~\ref{prop:character-gauge}), so it is trivial for
automorphisms of trivial lifting class, of any degree. For a
representative in the orbit of the other lift, the relating automorphism
has lifting class in the framing group, on which this sign is trivial by the last clause of Proposition~\ref{prop:character-gauge},
since all structures agree with the fixed germ near the odd torus; at
the cut $Z$ this is~\eqref{eq:cut-Chern}. The terminal subspace $\mathcal T_x$ consists of the
configurations which, outside the gauge input end, agree with $C^c$ for
some representative $c$; they are gauge paths ending at $c$ followed by
$C^c$. Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting} is to be read with this terminal
subspace. It cannot be replaced by the configurations ending at one fixed
$C_x$: those have a single topological energy, whereas attaching a sphere
of nonzero area changes the topological energy of a configuration without
changing its labels $(a,x)$.

\subsection{Reduction to a special quintuple}\label{app:shift-reduction}
Let $(Y,Y')$ be a pair of odd compression bodies with common boundary
$\Sigma$, a surface with two components on each of which the bundle is
odd (Paper~I, Definition~3.2), with primary data whose Lagrangians
$L(Y),L(Y')\subset\mathcal M(\Sigma)$ are embedded (Paper~I, Section~3.2).
For the torus domains $\Sigma=\Sigma_h\sqcup T$; for the closed-cut piece
and the pairs with product target $\Sigma=\Sigma_h\sqcup\Sigma_g$. Let
$X_D$, $U_+$ be the gauge region and section domain of the special
quintuple \cite[Section~3.1]{DFL}: the gauge region is
$\RR\times Y_0\cup U_-\times\Sigma\cup\RR\times(-Y_0')$, the matching seam is
$U_\partial\times\Sigma$, the Lagrangian arcs $\eta_+,\eta'_+$ carry $L(Y)$
and $L(Y')$, and the gauge input end is $(-\infty,-3]\times Y_\#$. Every
flat connection on $\Sigma$ is irreducible with central stabilizer.

The configuration space of Section~\ref{sec:geometry} depends only on the
decorated smooth manifold (bundle, matching seam, arcs with their
Lagrangians, ends with their limits) and on the primary data. It does
not depend on the metric, the almost-complex structure or the secondary
data: perturbed flatness involves no metric, and the weights are
functions of the end coordinates. Each one-input domain of
Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting}(a) with finite torus length is identified
with a special quintuple by a diffeomorphism of decorated manifolds,
depending continuously on the lengths and widths: for $W_{a,\mathsf R}$
the map $\psi_{\mathsf R}$ of Paper~I, Definition~10.1(a), which is
independent of $\theta$ and is a translation near the flat face; for the
closed-cut piece with input $Z$ the identification of Paper~I,
Lemma~10.18(ii); for the domains defining the mixed maps of Paper~I, Lemma~9.7, a
decorated diffeomorphism which carries end coordinates to end
coordinates. It is the composite of a diffeomorphism supported in a
compact set, which realizes by the isotopy extension theorem the
compactly supported changes of the seam placement and of the neck
lengths by which a domain of~\ref{I1} differs from the special
quintuple, and of a diffeomorphism which on
each mixed end is the identity in the end coordinate times a
diffeomorphism of the cross-section. The second factor accounts for the
translation-invariant data on the mixed ends, which that lemma allows to
differ from those of the quintuple; an example is the width of the
section interval, which is $\pi$ for the polar ends of Paper~I,
Lemma~6.3(ii). Both factors depend continuously on the parameters of
these changes, by isotopy extension with parameters. Since the weights are functions of
the end coordinates,
the weighted smooth topology is preserved, and the metric does not enter
the configuration space. The output regions used in part~(b) are identified in
Section~\ref{app:shift-local}. It therefore suffices to construct the
deformations on a special quintuple, for families in which the primary
data vary in a compact set.

\subsection{Preparation of the ends and collars}\label{app:shift-prep}
Write $C_Y=[-2,0]_s\times\Sigma$ for the collar of $\Sigma=\partial Y$ in
$Y$ which forms part of $U_-\times\Sigma$ on the mixed ends
\cite[Section~3.1]{DFL}, and $C_{Y'}$ likewise. It extends to a flat
collar $[-3,0]\times\Sigma$ whose part $[-3,-2]\times\Sigma$ in $Y_0$ is free of
the primary perturbation, so that primary solutions are flat on it. This
is arranged as follows. The primary perturbations of a compact family
are supported in finitely many fixed solid tori in the interior of
$Y_0$, so some collar of $\partial Y_0$ in $Y_0$ avoids them, and a
decorated diffeomorphism supported in $Y_0$, as in
Section~\ref{app:shift-reduction}, makes this collar
$[-3,-2]\times\Sigma$. The
collars of $\partial Y_0$ used below are $[-9/4,-2]\times\Sigma$, in the
normal form, and $[-5/2,-2]\times\Sigma$, in the definition of
$\mathcal T_x$. We use the following normal form, with constants
$\Theta\ge2$ and $S\ge3$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(N\arabic*)]
\item $u$ is the constant map to $x$ on $[S,\infty)\times[-1,1]$;
\item near $\eta_+$ and $\eta'_+$, $u$ is independent of the normal
coordinate;
\item on the mixed-end regions $|\theta|\ge\Theta$ the configuration is a
stationary pair $(\pi^*B,q)$, $(\pi'^*B',q')$, where $B$ equals
$\mathrm{pr}^*b$ on $[-9/4,0]\times\Sigma$, that is, on $C_Y$ and on the collar of
width $1/4$ of $\partial Y_0$ in $Y_0$, for a flat connection $b$ on
$\Sigma$, and similarly for $B'$;
\item $u(s,\theta)$ is independent of $s$ for $s\in[0,1]$;
\item on $[-1,0]\times\RR_\theta\times\Sigma$ the connection is the pullback
of a connection $b(\theta)+\psi(\theta)\,d\theta$ on $\RR_\theta\times\Sigma$
whose slices $b(\theta)$ are flat, with $\psi=0$ for $|\theta|\ge\Theta$.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:preparation}
Let $\mathcal A(a,x)$ be the space of smooth configurations on the
special quintuple with gauge input $a$ and output $x$, in the topology
of Section~\ref{sec:geometry}. Let $K$ be a compact metrizable space and
$f:K\to\mathcal A(a,x)$ a continuous family, with primary and secondary
data varying continuously in a compact set. Then there are constants
$\Theta\ge2$ and $S\ge3$ and a deformation $f_t$, $t\in[0,1]$, jointly continuous in
$(k,t)$ and in the data, with $f_0=f$ and $f_1$ in normal form. It is
the identity on the gauge input end $(-\infty,-3]\times Y_\#$. Members
that are stationary on the original ends beyond $\Theta$ and $S$ remain
so, and members of $\mathcal T_x$ are fixed.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\emph{Collar gauge at the mixed ends.} Let $B_q$ be the limiting
connection of the configuration at the $+$ mixed end, a representative
of $q$; it is continuous in the family and
flat on the flat collar. Let $K$ be its temporal gauge along the collar,
the solution of $\partial_sK=-(B_q)_sK$ on $[-3,0]$ with $K(0)=1$. Then
$K^*B_q$ has no $ds$ component on $[-3,0]\times\Sigma$ and, being flat, is a
product there. Choose a smooth $\sigma_N:[-3,0]\to[-5/2,0]$ with
$\sigma_N(s)=s$ on $[-9/4,0]$ and $\sigma_N=0$ near $-3$, and put
$k_\lambda=K(\lambda\sigma_N(s))$, extended by $1$. This is a path of gauge
transformations from $1$ to $k=k_1$, with $k_\lambda=1$ at the seam for every
$\lambda$, and $k^*B_q=\mathrm{pr}^*b_q$ on $[-9/4,0]\times\Sigma$, because $k=K$
there. Apply the gauge $k_{\lambda(\theta)}$ on the $+$ mixed end, with
$\lambda(\theta)=0$ for $\theta\le\Theta_0$ and $1$ for $\theta\ge\Theta_0+1$,
and do the same at the $-$ end. Gauge transformations preserve the
matching condition, and this one is the identity at the seam. If the limit
representative is already a product on $[-5/2,0]\times\Sigma$, then $K=1$ on
$[-5/2,0]$, which contains the range of $\sigma_N$, so $k_\lambda=1$; in
particular members of $\mathcal T_x$ are fixed.

\emph{Ends.} On the strip output end write $u=\exp_x\xi$ in an exponential
chart at $x$ in which both Lagrangians are linear, and put
$u_t=\exp_x((1-t\beta)\xi)$ with a cutoff $\beta(s)$ equal to one for
$s\ge S$. On a mixed end, beyond some $\Theta_1\ge\Theta_0+1$ the
configuration lies in the matching chart $\Psi_{R_q}$ (Section~\ref{app:local})
centred at its stationary limit $R_q=(\pi^*k^*B_q,q)$:
$c=\Psi_{R_q}(\zeta)$ with $\zeta$ in the linearized boundary conditions.
Put $c_t=\Psi_{R_q}((1-t\beta(\theta))\zeta)$ with $\beta=1$ for
$\theta\ge\Theta$. The chart is fibrewise over the base, and the linear
conditions are pointwise in $\theta$, so they are preserved; the chart
gives the nonlinear matching and Lagrangian conditions. A compact family
has uniformly small tails in the topology of Section~\ref{sec:geometry},
so common $\Theta_1,\Theta,S$ exist, and the charts depend continuously on
the limits and the primary data. Members already stationary beyond $\Theta$
remain so.

\emph{Collars.} Let $\phi_0:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)$ be smooth and
nondecreasing, with $\phi_0=0$ on $[0,1]$ and $\phi_0(s)=s$ near and beyond
$2$, and put $\phi_t=(1-t)\mathrm{id}+t\phi_0$. Pull back $u$ by $\phi_t$ in the
normal coordinate of the seam in $U_+$, and $A$ by
$(s,\theta,y)\mapsto(-\phi_t(-s),\theta,y)$ on $[-2,0]\times\RR\times\Sigma$;
since $\phi_t$ is the identity near $2$, the result is smooth where
$[-2,0]\times\Sigma$ meets $Y_0$. Since $\phi_t(0)=0$, the seam values, hence
the matching condition, are unchanged. Do the same in the normal
coordinates of $\eta_+,\eta'_+$, which preserves the arc values. On the
strips $[0,2]\times[2,\infty)$ both maps act on the same coordinate and do
not commute. In the coordinate $s\in[0,2]$ across such a strip, with the
seam at $s=0$ and the arc at $s=2$, write
$\Phi_{\rm seam}(s)=\phi_t(s)$ and $\Phi_{\rm arc}(s)=2-\phi_t(2-s)$. Take
the arc pullback first and the seam pullback second, so that $u$ is
replaced by $u\circ\Phi_{\rm arc}\circ\Phi_{\rm seam}$. At $t=1$ this
composite maps $[0,1]$ to the seam, because $\phi_0=0$ there and
$\Phi_{\rm arc}(0)=0$, and a neighbourhood of $2$ to the arc, because
$\phi_0$ is the identity near $2$; for every $t$ it fixes the seam and
the arc pointwise. At $t=1$, (N2), (N4) and (N5) hold; stationarity
beyond $\Theta$ is preserved, and so is (N3), because $\mathrm{pr}^*b$ is
independent of $s$. Members of $\mathcal T_x$ are independent of $s$ on
these collars and are fixed.

All three steps are explicit, continuous in the data, and supported away
from the gauge input end.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Positive extension, shift and straightening}\label{app:shift-shift}
Use a variant of the reparametrizations $\Phi_\pm$ of
\cite[Section~4.4]{DFL}: polar about the corners for $|\theta|\ge\Theta+1$,
with distance $e^{-\theta}$ to the corner as in their formula, preserving
the lines of constant $\theta$ near the seam for $|\theta|\le\Theta$,
smooth in between, where the prepared configuration is stationary, and
carrying the normal collars of $\eta_+,\eta'_+$ onto collars of
$t=\pm1$. DFL take $\Phi_+$ polar on the whole arms
$[0,2]\times[2,\infty)$; the present variant depends on $\Theta$, hence on
the family. In these coordinates the gauge region is
$(-\infty,0]\times Y_\#$ with $\{0\}\times Y_0$ and $\{0\}\times Y_0'$
removed, the section domain is $[0,\infty)\times[-1,1]$ with the corners
$\pm i$ removed, the matching seam is $\{0\}\times[-1,1]\times\Sigma$ against
$\{0\}\times[-1,1]$, and the collars $C_Y,C_{Y'}$ of the mixed ends collapse
to $\{0\}\times\partial Y_0$ and $\{0\}\times\partial Y_0'$, the collar
coordinate becoming the angle about these corners. By (N3)--(N5) a prepared
configuration is independent of the normal coordinate near $r=0$, it is
$\mathrm{pr}^*b$ with no angular component near the corners, and its seam
restriction is $b(\theta(t))+\widetilde\psi(t)\,dt$ with
$\widetilde\psi(t)=\psi(\theta(t))\theta'(t)$, which vanishes near $t=\pm1$.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:representatives}
Let $\mathcal P$ be the space of smooth primary solutions on $Y_0$ which
are products on the collar $[-9/4,-2]\times\Sigma$ of $\partial Y_0$, and let $\mathcal S$ be the
space of smooth flat connections on $\Sigma$ whose class lies in $L(Y)$; a
torus component of $\Sigma$ contributes only the point of $\mathcal M(T)$.
Restriction $\rho:\mathcal P\to\mathcal S$ is a locally trivial fibre bundle,
also over a compact family of primary data. A continuous family
$a:K\times[0,\infty)\to\mathcal S$, smooth in $r$ with all $r$-derivatives
continuous, with a lift $B(\cdot,0)$ of $a(\cdot,0)$, has a lift $B$ with
the same regularity, constant in $r$ where $a$ is.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Near $a_0=\rho(B_0)$ choose a smooth local section $[a]\mapsto\widehat B$ of
$\mathcal P\to L(Y)$ by a primary slice; the primary moduli space is regular
and its restriction map is an embedding (Paper~I, Section~3.2). Then
$\rho(\widehat B)$ and $a$ represent the same class, so $\rho(\widehat B)=g^*a$
for a gauge $g$ near $1$ on $\Sigma$, unique up to the centre, by the
surface Coulomb slice and irreducibility on both components. Extend $g$
over the collar by $\exp(\beta\log g)$, with $\beta=1$ on
$[-9/4,-2]\times\Sigma$ and $0$ at depth; this gives a local section with $\rho=a$. Thus $\rho$ is
locally trivial; its fibre is the space of gauge transformations of $Y_0$
which are central constants on the collar, modulo the centre. On
$K\times[0,\infty)$ patch the local sections with a partition of unity that
is smooth in $r$ to a connection on the pulled-back bundle; the horizontal
lift solves an ordinary differential equation $g'g^{-1}=\xi$ pointwise on
$Y_0$, so it has the stated regularity and is constant where $a$ is.
\end{proof}

\emph{Positive extension.} For a prepared family, let $\alpha(r,t)$ be the
horizontal lift of $r\mapsto u(r,t)$ in flat connections on $\Sigma$, with
$d^*_{\alpha}\partial_r\alpha=0$ and initial value $b(\theta(t))$; on a torus
component it is constant in $r$, since $H^1(T;\ad)=0$. It exists, is unique
and depends smoothly on $u$, since the flat connections are irreducible;
near $t=\pm1$ it is independent of $t$ by (N2). By
Lemma~\ref{lem:representatives} choose $B(r)$ on $Y_0$ and $B'(r)$ on $-Y_0'$,
products with values $\alpha(r,\pm1)$ on the collars of (N3), with $B(0),B'(0)$
the limit representatives of (N3). Let $A_u$ be the connection on
$[0,\infty)\times Y_\#$ equal to $B(r)$ on $Y_0$, $B'(r)$ on $-Y_0'$, and
$\alpha(r,t)+\widetilde\psi(t)\,dt$ on $[-1,1]\times\Sigma$, with no $dr$
component. By (N3)--(N5) the configuration and $A_u$ are independent of $r$
near $r=0$ and agree there, so the connection $\widetilde A$ on $\RR\times Y_\#$
equal to $A$ for $r\le0$ and to $A_u$ for $r\ge0$ is smooth. For $r\ge S$ it
is independent of $r$; write $c_S$ for its restriction there, whose
$\Sigma$-slices $\alpha(S,t)$ all represent the point determined by $x$.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:shift}
For $\tau\in[0,S+4]$ put
\[
 A_\tau(r,y)=\widetilde A(r+\tau,y)\ (r\le0),\qquad
 u_\tau(r,t)=u(r+\tau,t).
\]
This is a family of configurations with labels $(a,x)$, jointly continuous
in $(k,\tau)$ and the data, beginning at the prepared family, and it
translates the gauge input end by $\tau$. At $\tau=S+4$ the region
$r\ge-4$ carries $\mathrm{pr}^*c_S$ and $u_\tau\equiv x$. Members of $\mathcal T_x$
remain in $\mathcal T_x$ for all $\tau$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
At the seam the slice of $A_\tau$ over $t$ is $\alpha(\tau,t)$, which represents
$u(\tau,t)=u_\tau(0,t)$; the arcs carry $u(r+\tau,\pm1)\in L$. Near a
removed corner the pair is smooth in the reparametrized coordinates, with
limit the stationary pair of $B(\tau)$, whose collar value is $\alpha(\tau,1)$,
a product. In the original end coordinate it converges to this stationary
pair at the exponential rate one. This exceeds the mixed-end weight,
which we choose below one. Every condition imposed on that weight here
and in Paper~I is an upper bound, and by Proposition~\ref{prop:weights}
the counts do not depend on the choice. The
configuration therefore lies in the space of Section~\ref{sec:geometry}, with
the mixed limit moving along the arc. Translation over a bounded range is
continuous in that topology. For $\tau=S+4$ and $r\ge-4$ we have $r+\tau\ge S$.
If a member lies in $\mathcal T_x$, then $u\equiv x$ and its seam restriction
is $\mathrm{pr}^*b$, so $\alpha\equiv b$, $\widetilde\psi=0$, $B,B'$ are constant,
and $\widetilde A$ is $\mathrm{pr}^*c$ on $[-3,\infty)\times Y_\#$; the translates
keep this form.
\end{proof}

\emph{Straightening.} At $\tau=S+4$ contract the $t$-dependence of $c_S$. The
slices $\alpha(S,t)$ are independent of $t$ for $|t|\ge t_0$, for some $t_0<1$, by (N2).
Choose a smooth nondecreasing $\rho:[-1,1]\to[-1,1]$ with $\rho(t)=t$ for
$|t|\le t_0$ and $\rho=\pm1$ near $\pm1$. For $\lambda\in[0,1]$ let
$c_\lambda$ be the connection on $Y_\#$ equal to
$\alpha(S,(1-\lambda)\rho(t))+(1-\lambda)\widetilde\psi(t)\,dt$ on
$[-1,1]\times\Sigma$ and to the lifts $B_\lambda,B'_\lambda$ of
Lemma~\ref{lem:representatives} of the paths
$\lambda\mapsto\alpha(S,\pm(1-\lambda))$, starting at $B(S),B'(S)$. Near
$t=\pm1$ the slices are the constant values of these paths, so the pieces
join smoothly, and at $\lambda=0$ the connection is $c_S$. With a smooth
$\chi$ equal to $0$ for $r\le-5$ and to $1$ for $r\ge-4$, replace the
connection $A_{S+4}$ by
$A_{S+4}+\chi(r)(\mathrm{pr}^*c_\lambda-\mathrm{pr}^*c_S)$ and keep $u\equiv x$. This is
$\mathrm{pr}^*c_\lambda$ on $[-4,0]\times Y_\#$ and the unchanged connection for
$r\le-5$; the interpolation lies in the gauge input end, where no boundary
condition is imposed, and at $\lambda=0$ nothing changes. The slices
represent the point of $x$, so matching holds, and the mixed limits are
stationary pairs of $B_\lambda,B'_\lambda$. At $\lambda=1$ the connection
$c_1=B_1\cup\mathrm{pr}^*b\cup B'_1$ is independent of $t$ on the neck, with
$B_1,B'_1$ products on the collars of (N3). On members of $\mathcal T_x$ the
slices $\alpha(S,\cdot)$ are constant and $\widetilde\psi=0$, so this step is
the identity.

\emph{Collar widening.} Let $K_1$ be the temporal gauge of $B_1\cup\mathrm{pr}^*b$
along the flat collar, as in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:preparation}; it
equals $1$ on $[-9/4,0]$. Choose a smooth $\sigma_W:[-3,0]\to[-3,0]$ with
$\sigma_W(s)=s$ on $[-5/2,0]$ and $\sigma_W=0$ near $-3$, put
$g_\mu=K_1(\mu\sigma_W(s))$ on $Y_0$, extended by $1$, and define $g'_\mu$ on
$Y_0'$ likewise. For $\mu\in[0,1]$ these gauge transformations equal $1$ on
$[-9/4,0]\times\Sigma$, hence near the neck and the seam, and
$g_1^*B_1$ is a product on $[-5/2,-2]\times\Sigma$. Apply the gauge
transformation $g_{\mu\chi(r)}\cup1\cup g'_{\mu\chi(r)}$ on the gauge region,
with $\chi$ as above. It changes the mixed limits to the stationary pairs
of $g_\mu^*B_1$ and $g'^*_\mu B'_1$, and at $\mu=1$ the region $r\ge-4$
carries $\mathrm{pr}^*c$ with $c=g_1^*B_1\cup\mathrm{pr}^*b\cup g'^*_1B'_1$, a
representative of the critical point over $x$ in the sense above. The
configuration therefore lies in $\mathcal T_x$. A member of $\mathcal T_x$
is carried to the constant pair of $g_\mu^*B\cup\mathrm{pr}^*b\cup g'^*_\mu B'$,
which is again a representative since $g_\mu=1$ on $[-5/2,0]$ in that case;
so members of $\mathcal T_x$ remain in $\mathcal T_x$.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting}(a) for finite torus lengths]
Concatenate Lemma~\ref{lem:preparation}, Lemma~\ref{lem:shift}, the
straightening and the collar widening, and transport by the identifications of
Section~\ref{app:shift-reduction}. Relative preparation of the original
tails is the second step of Lemma~\ref{lem:preparation}. Continuity in the
data holds because every choice above (the collar gauges, the charts at
stationary pairs, the lifts of Lemma~\ref{lem:representatives}, the horizontal
lifts, the widening gauges) depends continuously on the primary data, and
the identifications depend continuously on the lengths. The deformation is
the identity on, or a translation of, the gauge input end away from
$[-5,-3]\times Y_\#$. Hence if
the family has the form $A_0\#_\ell c$ with an ordinary configuration $A_0$
glued at the input with a long neck $\ell$, the deformation is
$A_0\#_{\ell+\tau}c_\tau$, and the determinant gluing maps of
Lemma~\ref{lem:line-gluing} are continuous along it. This is the coherence with
ordinary gluing at the gauge input, which is the coherence asserted in
Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting}. A strip glued at the output is not
carried along by the deformation, since preparation truncates the output
tail. A configuration with a strip glued at its output is treated as a
configuration in its own right, with the new output label, and the
output coherence of the orientations is proved by the two-stage shift in
the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:transport}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Completed torus domains}\label{app:shift-completed}
Let a compact family on $W_{a,\infty}$ be given. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:split-preglue}(i) it is in torus normal form beyond some
$r_0$ after a continuous family of small gauge transformations. The
truncation $\alpha+(1-t+t\chi_{R})b$ on the tail, with $\chi_R=1$ for
$r\le R$ and $0$ for $r\ge R+1$, is continuous in the topology of
Section~\ref{sec:geometry}, whose weight is independent of $r$, and fixes
members that are $\alpha$ beyond $R$; at $t=1$ every member is $\alpha$ beyond
$R+1$. The mixed-end limits are not changed: in torus normal form they are
already $\alpha$ beyond $r_0$, because a flat connection in the
torus-fibre slice of Paper~I, Lemma~10.9, which is $C^1$-close to $\alpha$
equals $\alpha$. Indeed its fibre part is flat, coclosed and small, hence
zero since $H^1(T;\ad\alpha)=0$, and then $d_\alpha\phi_j=0$ forces
$\phi_j=0$ since $H^0(T;\ad\alpha)=0$. Such a configuration is the extension by $\alpha$ of a configuration on
$W_{a,R+2}$ which equals $\alpha$ near the flat face, and the face is part of
the matching seam. The deformations of
Sections~\ref{app:shift-prep}--\ref{app:shift-shift} preserve this condition:
the collar gauges and the chart cutoffs are the identity where the
configuration and its limit are $\alpha$, the widening gauges are supported
away from the neck and the seam, the collar pullbacks fix
configurations equal to $\alpha$ near the seam, the seam term $\widetilde\psi$
vanishes on the torus component, the horizontal lifts and the representatives
are $\alpha$ on $T$, and the translation and the straightening bring the
positive extension, which is $\alpha$ on $T$, to the neighbourhood of the face.
Extending by $\alpha$ along the whole deformation gives
Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting}(a) on $W_{a,\infty}$, ending in $\mathcal T_x$
with a representative which is $\alpha$ on $T$; the constant pair is then $\alpha$
on the tail.

\subsection{Shifting on domains with two gauge inputs}\label{app:shift-local}
On a triangle or folded triangle at positive collapse scale, and on a
finite-width joined torus domain, the outgoing closed cut writes the gauge
region as an ordinary cobordism followed by an output region $M$. For the
joined torus domain this is~\ref{I2}. For the triangles and folded
triangles the cut is the closed three-manifold that separates the two
gauge inputs from the output and crosses the collapsing region
(Paper~I, Lemma~9.11); a change of metric at fixed configuration gives
it a product collar (Paper~I, Lemma~9.12, and the corresponding path
for the folded triangle in Paper~I, Theorem~11.8).
In each case $M$, with a smooth product collar at the cut, is identified
with the complement of $(-\infty,-c_1]\times Y_\#$ in a special quintuple by a
diffeomorphism of decorated manifolds, continuous in the width and in the
scale on compact positive ranges, which carries the product collar of $M$
onto $[-c_1,-3]\times Y_\#$ after a reparametrization of its normal
coordinate. Such a reparametrization does not change product
configurations $\mathrm{pr}^*c$, and the metric on this collar may be
replaced by one with a longer product collar along a path of data, since
the configuration spaces do not depend on the metric
(Section~\ref{app:shift-reduction}). Its compression bodies are those of the
pair $(D_0,D_1)$, or of the marked pair of the triangle. The cut is
$\{-c_1\}\times Y_\#$, with $c_1>5$. The steps of
Sections~\ref{app:shift-prep}--\ref{app:shift-shift} are supported in
$r\ge-5$, away from a collar of the cut, except for the translation. Replace
the translation by the localized map: with $\chi$ nondecreasing, zero near
$-c_1$ and one on $[-5,\infty)$, put $F_\tau(r)=r+\tau\chi(r)$ and
\[
 A_\tau=F_\tau^*\widetilde A,\qquad u_\tau(r,t)=u(r+\tau,t).
\]
Since $F_\tau'>0$ this is a smooth configuration; it is a translation on
$[-5,\infty)$, so the seam and the corners are treated as in
Lemma~\ref{lem:shift}, and near the cut it is the identity, so it glues with
the ordinary cobordism. For $\tau=S+4$ followed by the straightening and
the collar widening, the region $r\ge-4$ carries a constant pair $C^c$, and the configuration is
$A_E\#C^c$, with $A_E$ an ordinary cobordism configuration ending at $c$ on
the parallel copy $\{-7/2\}\times Y_\#$ of the cut, whose collar
$[-4,-3]\times Y_\#$ carries $\mathrm{pr}^*c$, and with the original inputs
unchanged. The configuration $A_E\#C^c$ is not a product of two gauge
paths. The identification above carries the product collar of the cut
onto $[-c_1,-3]\times Y_\#$, so the copy $\{-7/2\}\times Y_\#$ of the cut
and the collar $[-4,-3]\times Y_\#$, on which the configuration is
$\mathrm{pr}^*c$, lie in the product collar, as ordinary gluing at the
copy requires.

Pull back the transition functions of the bundle as well. If
$C_{i,\tau}$ is the parameter component of the pulled-back connection,
solving $\partial_\tau v_i=-C_{i,\tau}v_i$ identifies its transitions
with the initial ones. This retains the lifting classes and central
cocycles. The stationary representatives are the paths of
perturbed-flat mixed-end limits of the configuration, including the
corner pullback, and both operator rows are transported. A common
sufficiently small weight gives the family in
Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(a); the intermediate operators need
not be invertible.

This is the localized shift used in
Definition~\ref{def:mixed-orientation}, and it
proves Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting}(b). Relative and continuity statements
follow as in Lemma~\ref{lem:shift}.

\section{A sign calculation for projective composition}\label{app:signs}

For strictly commuting maps $F$ and $G$ of
degrees $f$ and $g$, the closed maps
$F^{\mathrm r}=F\eps^f$ and $G^{\mathrm r}=G\eps^g$
of Section~\ref{sec:algebra} compose
